\Image[midpoint-trapezoidal-quadratic-areas.png]
{title=Midpoint rectangles and trapezoids approximate the area under a quadratic}

For the <polynomial> $f(x)=x^2$ on $[0,1]$, four panels give <composite midpoint quadrature rule> value $21/64$ and <composite trapezoidal rule> value $11/32$, bracketing the exact <integral> $1/3$. Individual midpoint rectangles can cross the curve; it is their total signed area that underestimates the <integral>.

For $f\in C^2[a,b]$, meaning that its first two <derivatives> exist and are <continuous functions> on the closed interval, the signed <approximation error> has the form
$$
M(f)-I(f)=-\frac{(b-a)^3}{24}f''(\xi_M),\qquad a<\xi_M<b.
$$
Hence a <convex function> with $f''\geq0$ is underestimated, and a <concave function> with $f''\leq0$ is overestimated. A short justification uses the <Taylor formula with integral remainder>: writing $m=(a+b)/2$, it gives $I(f)-M(f)=\int_a^b k(t)f''(t)\,dt$, where $k(t)=(t-a)^2/2$ for $a\leq t\leq m$ and $k(t)=(b-t)^2/2$ for $m\leq t\leq b$. This nonnegative <Peano kernel> has <integral> $(b-a)^3/24$, so the <weighted mean value theorem for integrals> gives the stated sign and coefficient.

= Composite midpoint quadrature rule
{parent=Midpoint quadrature rule}
{title2=$M_n(f)=h\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}f(a+(j+1/2)h)$}

= Composite midpoint rule
{synonym}

Apply the <midpoint quadrature rule> on each of $n$ equal panels of a <partition of an interval>, with $h=(b-a)/n$:
$$
M_n(f)=h\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}f\left(a+(j+\tfrac12)h\right).
$$
Each <quadrature node> is a panel midpoint and each <quadrature weight> is $h$. For $f\in C^2[a,b]$, put $K=\max_{x\in[a,b]}|f''(x)|$. Summing the panel <quadrature errors> gives
$$
M_n(f)-I(f)=-\frac{b-a}{24}h^2f''(\xi),\qquad |M_n(f)-I(f)|\leq\frac{b-a}{24}h^2K
$$
for some $\xi\in(a,b)$. Each panel contributes at most $Kh^3/24$; there are $n=(b-a)/h$ panels, explaining the <composite midpoint rule error> of order $O(h^2)$ in <big O notation>. On a nonuniform <partition of an interval> with panel widths $h_j$ and largest width $H$, the corresponding bound is $K\sum_jh_j^3/24\leq K(b-a)H^2/24$.

For $f(x)=x^2$ on $[0,1]$, summing the sampled <polynomial> exactly yields
$$
M_n(f)=\frac13-\frac{1}{12n^2}.
$$
Doubling $n$ divides the <absolute error> by exactly four. For a general $C^2$ <function>, halving $h$ divides the displayed error bound by four, but the actual <absolute error> need not follow that ratio: cancellation can make it smaller or zero. <Continuous functions> still give convergence as the largest panel width tends to zero, by <uniform continuity>; without the bounded second <derivative>, the second-order rate is not guaranteed. <Noise sensitivity of a quadrature rule> explains a separate limitation when samples are inexact.

= Approximation error
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{title2=$x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x$}

For a numerical reference value $x$ and a computed value $x_{\mathrm{approx}}$, the signed <approximation error> is $x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x$. Stating the reference matters: comparison with a measured value assesses agreement with that value, rather than with an unknown exact value.

= Absolute error
{parent=Approximation error}
{title2=$|x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x|$}

The <absolute error> is the <absolute value> of the <approximation error>, $|x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x|$, measured in the same units as the numerical reference $x$. For example, approximating $1/3$ by $0.33$ gives <absolute error> $|0.33-1/3|=1/300$.

= Relative error
{parent=Approximation error}
{title2=$|x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x|/|x|$}

For a nonzero reference value $x$, the relative error is the <absolute error> divided by the reference's <absolute value>:
$$
\frac{|x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x|}{|x|}.
$$
It is <dimensionless> and expresses the size of the error compared with the quantity being approximated. Approximating $1/3$ by $0.33$ has <absolute error> $1/300$ and <relative error> $1/100=1\%$. The definition is unavailable when the reference is zero; use an <absolute error> or a separately stated scale in that case.

= Signed relative error
{parent=Relative error}
{title2=$\varepsilon=(x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x)/x$}

The signed relative error is $\varepsilon=(x_{\mathrm{approx}}-x)/x$ for a nonzero reference $x$. Its <absolute value> is the <relative error>. For a positive reference, a negative <signed relative error> means underestimation, and a positive one means overestimation. For a negative reference that interpretation reverses, so retain the displayed definition when interpreting its sign.

= Truncation error
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

<Truncation error> is introduced by replacing an exact operation with a finite approximation. It remains even when all arithmetic is exact. For $f(x)=x^2$, the forward <finite difference> for its <derivative> at $x=1$ gives
$$
\frac{f(1+h)-f(1)}h=2+h,\qquad f'(1)=2.
$$
Thus the <truncation error> is exactly $h$: $h=0.1$ gives $2.1$ and $h=0.01$ gives $2.01$. In finite precision, however, the numerator subtracts nearly equal values, and their <roundoff error> can be amplified by division by $h$. For smooth data with bounded evaluation errors of scale $u$, a typical bound has competing terms $C_1h+C_2u/h$. Smaller $h$ reduces the first term and increases the second; with fixed positive constants the balancing scale is $h\sim\sqrt u$. This is why blindly refining a <finite difference> eventually stops improving it.

= Spectral method
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

A spectral method approximates a differential equation in a global basis and enforces the equation after projection or collocation. Smooth and analytic solutions often give rapidly convergent coefficients.

= Spectral accuracy
{parent=Spectral method}

A <spectral method> has <spectral accuracy> for a class of smooth data when its error decreases faster than any fixed inverse power of the truncation size. For periodic <Fourier series>, this follows from arbitrarily many integrations by parts when every derivative matches across the periodic boundary. An analytic periodic extension to a complex strip gives exponential coefficient decay. Analyticity inside one period and matching only endpoint values do not suffice: a jump in a derivative of the periodic extension yields algebraic decay instead.

= Fourier spectral method
{c}
{parent=Spectral method}

A Fourier spectral method represents a periodic unknown by finitely many <Fourier modes> and converts differentiation into multiplication by wavenumber.

= Spectral truncation
{parent=Fourier spectral method}

A spectral truncation retains a finite set of basis modes and sets all omitted coefficients to zero.

= Fourier spectral system for a cosine-modulated second derivative
{c}
{parent=Fourier spectral method}

For the two-periodic problem
$$
-\frac{1+2\cos\pi x}{\pi^2}u''+u=f,
$$
the Fourier coefficients satisfy the tridiagonal system
$$
(m-1)^2\widehat u_{m-1}
+(m^2+1)\widehat u_m
+(m+1)^2\widehat u_{m+1}
=\widehat f_m,
\qquad m\in\mathbb Z.
$$

= Fourier–Galerkin method
{c}
{parent=Fourier spectral method}

The Fourier–Galerkin method seeks a finite <Fourier series> whose residual is orthogonal to every retained <Fourier mode>.

= Stability of a numerical method
{parent=Numerical analysis}

= Stability
{synonym}

= Numerical stability
{synonym}

For a numerical evolution, <numerical stability> means that perturbations remain controlled over a fixed finite interval by a bound independent of sufficiently small discretization parameters. With perturbations $\delta_n$ to starting data and added step defects $d_n$, a typical estimate is
$$
\max_{nh\leq T}\|\delta_n\|\leq C_T\left(\|\delta_0\|+\sum_{nh<T}\|d_n\|\right),
$$
where $C_T$ is independent of $h$. For a linear homogeneous recurrence, a uniform bound on powers of its update <matrix> supplies this control. <Von Neumann stability analysis> and the <energy method> provide complementary tools.

Finite-interval <numerical stability> differs from <absolute stability> at a fixed step size. Applying the <Euler method> to $y'=-2y$ multiplies a perturbation by $1-2h$ each step. At $h=0.2$ this is $0.6$, so perturbations shrink; at $h=1.2$ it is $-1.4$, so they alternate and grow. Nevertheless the <Euler method> converges as $h\to0$ for this problem: failure at a coarse step does not contradict the convergence theorem. <Absolute stability> asks whether a selected step preserves boundedness of the <Dahlquist test equation> over arbitrarily many steps. MIT's analysis uses strict decay, $|1+h\lambda|<1$, rather than the boundary-inclusive boundedness convention.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-330-introduction-to-numerical-analysis-spring-2012/a9d2bd9be098f0ada172af40379a17cc_MIT18_330S12_Chapter5.pdf[Laurent Demanet, MIT 18.330, Chapter 5, Example 14, 2012]{ref}

= Consistency of a numerical method
{parent=Numerical analysis}

= Numerical consistency
{synonym}

For a one-step approximation $y_{n+1}=y_n+h\phi(t_n,y_n,h)$ to an <ordinary differential equation>, insert the exact solution and define the unscaled <local truncation error>
$$
\tau_{n+1}=y(t_n+h)-y(t_n)-h\phi(t_n,y(t_n),h).
$$
<Consistency of a numerical method> requires $\tau_{n+1}/h\to0$ uniformly on the finite interval being approximated, under the stated smoothness assumptions. Merely asking $\tau_{n+1}\to0$ is too weak: the useless update $y_{n+1}=y_n$ has defect $h y'(t_n)+O(h^2)$, which tends to zero but generally fails the scaled test. Here $O$ denotes <Big O notation>. MIT uses this division-by-step convention.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-330-introduction-to-numerical-analysis-spring-2012/a9d2bd9be098f0ada172af40379a17cc_MIT18_330S12_Chapter5.pdf[Laurent Demanet, MIT 18.330, Chapter 5, Definition 6, 2012]{ref}

For the <Euler method>, <Taylor's theorem> gives $\tau_{n+1}=h^2y''(\xi_n)/2$. A bounded second <derivative> therefore gives <local truncation error> $O(h^2)$ and a scaled defect $O(h)$. Under a uniform <Lipschitz continuity> assumption on $f$ in $y$, within a region containing the exact and numerical solutions, <numerical stability> controls accumulation over about $T/h$ steps. This yields <global error> $O(h)$ with exact starting data. <Consistency of a numerical method> checks each step; <numerical stability> controls their combination.

= Order of a numerical method
{parent=Consistency of a numerical method}
{title2=$R_h=O(h^{p+1})$}

For a time-stepping formula written with an unscaled step residual $R_h$, order at least $p$ means that substituting a smooth exact solution gives $R_h=O(h^{p+1})$. Under the relevant <stability of a numerical method> assumptions and starting errors $O(h^p)$, this produces <global error> $O(h^p)$. Residuals divided by the step size instead use the equivalent convention $O(h^p)$.

= Local truncation error
{parent=Order of a numerical method}

The local truncation error is the defect left when exact solution values are inserted into a numerical update. For a one-step method started from the exact solution,
$$
\tau_{n+1}
=y(t_{n+1})-y(t_n)-h\phi(t_n,y(t_n),h).
$$
A local error $O(h^{p+1})$ generally leads to global error $O(h^p)$ under a uniform stability bound.

= Normalized local truncation error
{parent=Local truncation error}
{title2=$\mathcal T_{h,k}=\delta_{h,k}/k$}

For a time update with one-step defect $\delta_{h,k}$ after exact solution values are inserted, the normalized defect divides by the time step $k$. If $\delta_{h,k}=O(k^{p+1})$, the normalized <local truncation error> is $O(k^p)$. A spatial mesh contributes its own powers: for a diffusion update, $\mathcal T_{h,k}=O(k+h^2)$ means $\delta_{h,k}=O(k^2+kh^2)$. Under $k=O(h^2)$ these are respectively second and fourth powers of $h$. Neither normalization changes the actual <order of a numerical method>; it changes the power used to express its residual.

= Energy method
{parent=Numerical analysis}

The energy method differentiates a norm or quadratic energy along a differential equation or numerical evolution and estimates the resulting terms. Dissipative, skew-adjoint, and boundary contributions can yield stability or conservation without diagonalizing the operator.

= Dirichlet convection-diffusion contraction
{c}
{parent=Energy method}
{title2=$u_t=u_{xx}+2\kappa u_x$}

For zero endpoint values and real constant drift, integration by parts gives $\tfrac12 d\|u\|_{L^2}^2/dt=-\|u_x\|_{L^2}^2$. The drift contributes only a zero boundary term in the real part. Thus the solution is contractive, uniformly in the drift. Multiplication by $e^{\kappa x}$ converts the equation to a <heat equation> with constant killing rate $\kappa^2$, providing existence and uniqueness as well as the direct <energy estimate>.

= Periodic centered advection-diffusion contractivity
{parent=Energy method}
{title2=$\frac d{dt}\|U\|_h^2=-2\|D_+U\|_h^2$}

The semidiscrete system $U'=D_{xx}U+\alpha D_0U$, for real fixed $\alpha$, is contractive in the periodic discrete <L2 norm>. <Discrete summation by parts> makes the drift term's real energy contribution zero and the diffusion term nonpositive. Equivalently its <Fourier amplification symbol> generator has real part $-4h^{-2}\sin^2(\theta/2)$. This continuous-time estimate does not prove stability of an arbitrarily chosen time integrator.

= Centered Dirichlet drift-diffusion energy identity
{parent=Energy method}
{title2=$\operatorname{Re}\langle U,(D_2+\kappa D_1)U\rangle_d=-d^{-1}\sum_{m=0}^M|u_{m+1}-u_m|^2$}

For the centered second difference and centered first difference on a finite interval with zero <Dirichlet boundary conditions>, the diffusion <matrix> is symmetric negative definite and the drift <matrix> is <skew-symmetric>. Discrete <summation by parts> gives the displayed mesh-weighted identity. It proves contraction in the discrete <L2 norm> without needing periodic modes, positive stencil coefficients or simultaneous diagonalization.

= Energy stability for variable-coefficient reaction diffusion
{parent=Energy method}
{title2=$\|U(t)\|_h\leq e^{a_+t}\|U(0)\|_h$}

For a zero-boundary centered semidiscretization $U'=D_hU+V_hU$, where $D_h$ is the scaled <Dirichlet discrete Laplacian> and $V_h=\operatorname{diag}(a_j)$ with $a_j\leq a_+$, <summation by parts> gives $(U,D_hU)_h\leq0$. Hence $\tfrac12(d/dt)\|U\|_h^2\leq a_+\|U\|_h^2$. The <Gronwall inequality> yields $\|U(t)\|_h\leq e^{a_+t}\|U(0)\|_h$, a mesh-independent <stability> bound on every fixed finite interval. Positive reaction coefficients can permit physical growth without violating this form of <stability>.

= Uniqueness of Sobolev weak solutions of a wave equation
{parent=Energy method}

For the <weak energy solution of a variable-coefficient wave equation>, uniqueness can be proved without using $u_t$ as a spatial $H_0^1$ test function. For a difference with zero data, fix $s$ and test by $v(t)=\int_t^su(r)dr$ for $t<s$, extended by zero afterwards. Put $z(t)=\int_0^tu(r)dr$, so $v(t)=z(s)-z(t)$. <Integration by parts> in time for the symmetric principal form, and in space for the first-order terms, give
$$
\|u(s)\|_2^2+\theta\|Dz(s)\|_2^2
\leq C\int_0^s\bigl(\|u(t)\|_2^2+\|Dz(t)\|_2^2\bigr)dt+Cs\|Dz(s)\|_2^2.
$$
For short enough intervals the last term is absorbed. The <Gronwall inequality> forces $u=0$, and repetition proves uniqueness on the full interval. This antiderivative test is valid at the stated space-time $H^1$ regularity, unlike an unqualified direct energy test by $u_t$.

= Finite element method
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

= FEM
{c}
{synonym}

The <finite element method> replaces an infinite-dimensional <weak formulation> by finitely many unknowns attached to a <finite element mesh>. Simple local functions, often <piecewise polynomial functions>, join to form a global approximation. The attraction is that each <finite element> can be integrated separately, while the assembled equations enforce a global balance. A <piecewise-linear hat function> only meets its immediate neighbors, so a large problem can have a <sparse matrix> rather than a dense one. Gilbert Strang's MIT lecture develops this connection from the one-dimensional <weak formulation> through local hats to the <stiffness matrix> and load integrals.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-085-computational-science-and-engineering-i-fall-2008/676ba0a00eef45a6cb3823c4da61ee11_18-085F08-L18.pdf[Gilbert Strang, MIT 18.085, Lecture 18: Finite Elements in 1D, transcript pp. 2–8, 2008; accessed 2026-10-11]{ref}

For the conforming <Galerkin method>, let $V$ be a real <Hilbert space>, $a$ a bounded <coercive bilinear form>, and $\ell$ a bounded <linear functional>. Given a <conforming finite element space> $V_h\subset V$, the continuous and discrete problems are
$$
\begin{aligned}
u\in V:&\quad a(u,v)=\ell(v)&&\text{for every }v\in V,\\
u_h\in V_h:&\quad a(u_h,v_h)=\ell(v_h)&&\text{for every }v_h\in V_h.
\end{aligned}
$$
Writing $u_h=\sum_j c_j\phi_j$ in a <basis> of $V_h$ gives the <linear system>
$$
Kc=F,\qquad K_{ij}=a(\phi_j,\phi_i),\qquad F_i=\ell(\phi_i).
$$
Here $K$ is the <stiffness matrix> and $F$ is the vector of loads tested against the <basis> functions. The <Galerkin method> uses the same trial and test space in this construction. Finite element methods also use the <Petrov-Galerkin method>, with different trial and test spaces; testing against the trial space is therefore a property of this construction, rather than a definition of every <finite element method>.

The <finite element solution of the one-dimensional Poisson equation> below gives every entry explicitly. Refining the <finite element mesh> then improves the approximation by adding local degrees of freedom. For a symmetric <coercive bilinear form>, <Galerkin orthogonality> makes the discrete solution the best approximation in the <energy norm>; the geometry of <orthogonal projection> explains why this local construction yields a globally meaningful answer.

= Finite element solution of the one-dimensional Poisson equation
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$-u''=1$, $u(0)=u(1)=0$}

Consider the <Poisson equation> on an <interval> with homogeneous <Dirichlet boundary conditions>:
$$
-u''(x)=1\quad(0<x<1),\qquad u(0)=u(1)=0.
$$
Two <integrations> and the endpoint conditions give the exact solution $u(x)=x(1-x)/2$. To approximate it, multiply by a <test function> $v$ that vanishes at both endpoints. <Integration by parts> gives
$$
\int_0^1(-u'')v\,dx
=\bigl[-u'v\bigr]_0^1+\int_0^1u'v'\,dx
=\int_0^1v\,dx.
$$
The boundary term is zero. Thus the <weak formulation> is to find $u\in V=H_0^1(0,1)$ such that
$$
a(u,v):=\int_0^1u'v'\,dx=\ell(v):=\int_0^1v\,dx\qquad(v\in V).
$$
The <zero-trace Sobolev space> $H_0^1(0,1)$ consists of square-integrable functions with square-integrable <weak derivatives> and zero endpoint values in their continuous representatives; it does not require a classical second derivative. This is why continuous <piecewise linear functions> are admissible despite corners. The <Poincaré inequality> makes $a$ a <coercive bilinear form> on $V$. Strang derives this one-dimensional <weak formulation>, the <piecewise-linear hat functions>, and their load and derivative-product integrals in the cited lecture; its endpoint example uses a free left endpoint, while both endpoints here are fixed.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-085-computational-science-and-engineering-i-fall-2008/676ba0a00eef45a6cb3823c4da61ee11_18-085F08-L18.pdf[Strang, MIT 18.085, Lecture 18, transcript pp. 2–8: weak form, hat basis, and assembly]{ref}

Take the uniform <finite element mesh> $x_j=jh$, $j=0,\ldots,n$, with $h=1/n$. The continuous $P_1$ trial space is
$$
V_h=\{v_h\in C([0,1]):v_h(0)=v_h(1)=0,\quad v_h|_{[x_j,x_{j+1}]}\text{ is affine for every }j\}.
$$
Here $P_1$ means <polynomials> of degree at most one on each <interval>. This is a <conforming finite element space> in $V$, with $n-1$ interior <piecewise-linear hat functions> $\phi_i$ as a <basis>. Their endpoint values enforce the <Dirichlet boundary conditions> automatically. Set $u_h=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}c_i\phi_i$ and test the <weak formulation> against each $\phi_i$:
$$
K_{ij}=\int_0^1\phi_j'\phi_i'\,dx,\qquad
F_i=\int_0^1\phi_i\,dx=h,\qquad Kc=F.
$$
Each interior hat has two slopes $1/h$ and $-1/h$, each over a length $h$. Its squared derivative therefore integrates to $2/h$. Adjacent hats have opposite slopes on their one shared <interval>, giving $-1/h$. All other derivative products vanish. The <stiffness matrix> is the <tridiagonal matrix>
$$
K_{ii}=\frac2h,\qquad K_{i,i+1}=K_{i+1,i}=-\frac1h,\qquad K_{ij}=0\quad(|i-j|>1).
$$
The load $F_i=h$ is the area of a triangle of base $2h$ and height one, including hats next to the boundary. Boundary hats are omitted because the boundary values are prescribed. The <piecewise-linear hat function> article explains how the same entries arise by adding local two-node contributions.

With four <mesh> intervals, $h=1/4$, there are three unknowns:
$$
K=\begin{pmatrix}8&-4&0\\-4&8&-4\\0&-4&8\end{pmatrix},\qquad
F=\begin{pmatrix}1/4\\1/4\\1/4\end{pmatrix},\qquad
c=\begin{pmatrix}3/32\\1/8\\3/32\end{pmatrix}.
$$
For example, the first row gives $8(3/32)-4(1/8)=1/4$ and the middle gives $-4(3/32)+8(1/8)-4(3/32)=1/4$. The <coefficients> are the nodal values $u_h(x_i)=c_i$. At $x=3/8$, <linear interpolation> gives $u_h(3/8)=7/64=0.109375$, whereas $u(3/8)=15/128=0.1171875$: exactness at the nodes still leaves a visible error between them.

In this model the <Galerkin method> solution equals the nodal approximation $I_hu$ obtained by <linear interpolation> on any <finite element mesh>. Indeed, on each <interval> $[a,b]$, $(I_hu)'$ is the average of $u'$ and $v_h'$ is constant. Therefore $\int_a^b(u'-(I_hu)')v_h'\,dx=0$. Summing proves <Galerkin orthogonality> for $I_hu$, and uniqueness gives $u_h=I_hu$. This argument relies on the constant diffusion coefficient, conformity and exact integration; nodal exactness is not a general promise for <finite element methods> with other operators or integration rules.

On a uniform <mesh>, direct subtraction of the chord from the <parabola> gives, on every element $[a,b]$ of length $h$,
$$
e(x):=u(x)-u_h(x)=\frac{(x-a)(b-x)}2,\qquad
e'(x)=\frac{a+b}2-x.
$$
The largest value error is at an element midpoint. Its size and the <energy norm> error are exactly
$$
\|e\|_{\infty}=\frac{h^2}{8},\qquad
\|e\|_a^2=\int_0^1|e'|^2\,dx
=n\int_{-h/2}^{h/2}t^2\,dt=\frac{h^2}{12},\qquad
\|e\|_a=\frac{h}{\sqrt{12}}.
$$
Thus $h=1/4$ gives $\|e\|_\infty=1/128=0.0078125$ and $\|e\|_a=1/(4\sqrt{12})\approx0.0721688$. With eight elements, $h=1/8$, these become $1/512=0.001953125$ and $1/(8\sqrt{12})\approx0.0360844$. Halving the <mesh> spacing quarters the maximum value error but only halves the <energy norm> error: values and slopes converge at different rates. The figure shows the small value error at its own scale, where the four-to-one reduction becomes clear.

\Image[finite-element-poisson-refinement.png]
{title=Exact Poisson parabola and continuous piecewise-linear finite element solutions on four and eight intervals, with midpoint errors shown separately}

= Semidiscrete finite element heat equation
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$M\dot c+Sc=F$}

The spatial <Galerkin method> for the <heat equation> gives a <mass matrix> $M$ and a diffusion <stiffness matrix> $S$. With zero forcing, $\frac{d}{dt}(c^TMc)=-2c^TSc\leq0$. The <quadratic form> $c^TMc$ is the squared <L2 norm> of the represented finite-element function, proving an energy contraction. For nonzero forcing, the corresponding identity has the additional term $2c^TF$, which can be estimated using the <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality>.

= Forward Euler limit for a consistent hat mass matrix
{parent=Semidiscrete finite element heat equation}
{title2=$0\leq\mu\leq\frac16\quad\text{uniformly over refinements}$}

For a uniform piecewise-linear Galerkin heat discretization, the consistent <mass matrix> is $M=(h/6)\operatorname{tridiag}(1,4,1)$ and the <stiffness matrix> is $K=h^{-1}\operatorname{tridiag}(-1,2,-1)$. The generalized <eigenvalues> are $6(1-\cos\theta)/[h^2(2+\cos\theta)]$. Applying <forward Euler method> requires $k\lambda\leq2$, giving the displayed uniform bound for $\mu=k/h^2$. For $J$ intervals with Dirichlet endpoints the exact finite-grid upper bound is $(2-\cos(\pi/J))/[3(1+\cos(\pi/J))]$, which approaches $1/6$. Replacing the consistent <mass matrix> by a lumped one changes the <stability> restriction.

= Finite element
{parent=Finite element method}

A finite element specifies a reference region, a finite-dimensional space of local trial functions, and degrees of freedom that uniquely determine each such function. Assembling compatible copies over a <finite element mesh> gives a global trial space. For a one-dimensional linear element, the region is an interval, the functions are <affine functions>, and the degrees of freedom are the endpoint values.

= Conforming finite element space
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$V_h\subseteq V$}

A conforming finite element space is a finite-dimensional <vector subspace> of the solution's variational space $V$. For a second-order problem with homogeneous <Dirichlet boundary conditions>, continuous piecewise-polynomial functions vanishing on the boundary form such a subspace of the <zero-boundary Sobolev space> $H_0^1(\Omega)$. Conformity allows exact <Galerkin orthogonality> and the <Céa lemma>.

= Finite element mesh
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$\mathcal T_h$, $h=\max_j(x_{j+1}-x_j)$}

= Mesh
{synonym}

A <finite element mesh> partitions a domain into simple regions called elements, such as <intervals>, <triangles> or <tetrahedra>. In a conforming partition, distinct element interiors are disjoint, their closures cover the domain closure, and any nonempty intersection is a common face, edge or vertex of both elements. Chen's author notes give this compatibility condition for simplicial <meshes>.https://webapps.math.uci.edu/~chenlong/226/Ch2FEM.pdf[Long Chen, Introduction to Finite Element Methods, §2.1, pp. 3–4; updated January 17, 2024]{ref} Local <piecewise polynomial functions> and local <integrals> are defined on these regions; compatible degrees of freedom join them into a global <conforming finite element space>. The assembled <stiffness matrix> and <mass matrix> then inherit the local overlap pattern.

For the <finite element solution of the one-dimensional Poisson equation>, a <mesh> is simply an ordered list
$$
0=x_0<x_1<\cdots<x_n=1,\qquad
\mathcal T_h=\{[x_j,x_{j+1}]:0\leq j<n\},\qquad
h=\max_{0\leq j<n}(x_{j+1}-x_j).
$$
Nodes are the endpoints; elements are the intervening <intervals>. Four equal elements have five nodes but only three unknown nodal values after imposing the zero <Dirichlet boundary conditions>. A nonuniform partition such as $0,1/8,1/4,1/2,1$ has element lengths $1/8,1/8,1/4,1/2$ and $h=1/2$, so counting elements alone does not measure the largest unresolved scale.

Refining a <finite element mesh> replaces some or all elements by smaller ones. For continuous <piecewise linear functions> in one dimension, retaining all old nodes gives nested <conforming finite element spaces> $V_h\subset V_{h/2}$ when every <interval> is bisected. The old approximation remains available, and the new interior nodes allow additional changes of slope. With a symmetric <coercive bilinear form> and exact integration, best approximation from <Galerkin orthogonality> means that the <energy norm> error cannot increase under such nested refinement.

For the <finite element solution of the one-dimensional Poisson equation>, uniform refinement from four to eight elements increases the unknown count from three to seven. The exact formulas give maximum errors $0.0078125$ and $0.001953125$, and <energy norm> errors approximately $0.0721688$ and $0.0360844$. Refinement near a region of strong curvature can spend unknowns where the <linear interpolation> error matters most; convergence in a general problem still needs approximation and regularity assumptions such as those in the <finite element interpolation estimate>.

= Shape-regular mesh
{parent=Finite element mesh}

= Shape regularity
{synonym}

A family of simplicial meshes is shape-regular when the ratio of each element's diameter to its inscribed-ball radius stays uniformly bounded. This excludes degenerating shapes and makes <finite element interpolation estimates> uniform in the mesh size. The element sizes may still vary substantially across the domain.

= Finite element interpolation estimate
{parent=Finite element method}

On a <shape-regular mesh> in dimensions at most three, nodal piecewise-linear interpolation of $u\in H^2(\Omega)$ satisfies
$$
\|u-I_hu\|_{L^2(\Omega)}+h\|u-I_hu\|_{H^1(\Omega)}
\leq Ch^2\|u\|_{H^2(\Omega)},
$$
where $h$ is the largest element diameter and $C$ is independent of $h$. For homogeneous <Dirichlet boundary conditions>, $I_hu$ belongs to the <conforming finite element space>. Scaling each element to a reference element gives the estimate; <shape regularity> bounds the scaling constants, while subtraction of an <affine function> leaves an error controlled by the second derivatives.

= Mass matrix
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$M_{ij}=\int_\Omega\phi_i\phi_j$}

The mass matrix is the <Gram matrix> of a <finite element> basis in the <L2 inner product>. Its coefficient <quadratic form> is the squared <L2 norm> of the represented trial function, so it is a <positive-definite matrix> for a linearly independent basis.

= Mass eigenstate
{parent=Mass matrix}
{title2=$M|n\rangle=m_n|n\rangle$}

A <mass eigenstate> diagonalizes the Hermitian <mass> operator or <mass matrix> and has a definite <mass>. In a flavor basis with off-diagonal <mass> terms, it is a linear combination of flavor states. For unstable particles, decay propagation instead involves an effective complex matrix; its <eigenvectors> need not inherit the orthogonality of a Hermitian <mass> problem.

= Affine-weighted hat mass matrix
{parent=Mass matrix}
{title2=$W_{ij}=\int w(x)\phi_i(x)\phi_j(x)\,dx$}

On a one-dimensional uniform <finite element mesh> of spacing $h$, let $w$ be an <affine function> and $\phi_i$ the interior <piecewise-linear hat functions>. The weighted <mass matrix> has entries
$$
W_{ii}=\frac{2h}{3}w(x_i),\qquad
W_{i,i+1}=W_{i+1,i}=\frac h{12}\bigl(w(x_i)+w(x_{i+1})\bigr),
$$
and zero entries for nonadjacent indices. The diagonal product $\phi_i^2$ is symmetric about $x_i$ and integrates to $2h/3$; the neighbouring product is symmetric about $(x_i+x_{i+1})/2$ and integrates to $h/6$. The odd part of an <affine function> integrates to zero around each centre, giving the formulas. This evaluates a variable reaction term exactly without substituting a constant coefficient or a quadrature approximation.

= Stiffness matrix
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$K_{ij}=a(\phi_j,\phi_i)$}

The stiffness matrix represents a variational <bilinear form> $a$ in a <finite element> basis. A symmetric <coercive bilinear form> gives a <positive-definite matrix>. Local support of the <piecewise-linear hat functions> produces a sparse <matrix>, allowing efficient numerical solution.

= Reaction-diffusion finite element matrix
{parent=Stiffness matrix}
{title2=$K=S+M$}

For $-u''+u=f$ with homogeneous <Dirichlet boundary conditions>, the conforming <piecewise-linear hat functions> on a uniform grid give a diffusion <stiffness matrix> $S$ and a <mass matrix> $M$. Their nonzero entries are $S_{ii}=2/h$, $S_{i,i+1}=-1/h$, $M_{ii}=2h/3$, and $M_{i,i+1}=h/6$, with symmetric counterparts. The sum is a <positive-definite matrix> because its coefficient <quadratic form> is $\int((u_h')^2+u_h^2)$. For forcing $f(x)=x$, the load at $x_i$ is $hx_i$.

= Energy conservation for semidiscrete Galerkin advection
{parent=Finite element method}

For periodic $u_t=u_x$, the conforming <Galerkin method> has $M\dot U=CU$, with <mass matrix> $M_{ij}=\int\phi_i\phi_j$ and $C_{ij}=\int\phi_i\phi_j'$. <Integration by parts> and periodicity give $C^T=-C$. Therefore
$$
\frac{d}{dt}(U^TMU)=2U^TCU=0.
$$
The conserved quantity is the squared <L2 norm> of the <finite element> function. On a uniform one-dimensional mesh of spacing $h$, the <piecewise-linear hat functions> give $M_{ii}=2h/3$, $M_{i,i\pm1}=h/6$, $C_{i,i+1}=1/2$ and $C_{i,i-1}=-1/2$.

= Cubic Hermite finite element
{c}
{parent=Finite element method}

A cubic Hermite finite element is piecewise cubic and joins with continuous first derivative. It is therefore conforming for fourth-order variational problems whose energy space lies in $H^2$.

= Rayleigh-Ritz method
{c}
{parent=Finite element method}
{wiki=Rayleigh–Ritz_method}

= Ritz method
{c}
{synonym}

The Ritz method minimizes a quadratic energy over a finite-dimensional trial space. For basis functions $\phi_j$ and bilinear form $a$, its linear system is $Ka=F$ with $K_{jk}=a(\phi_k,\phi_j)$ and $F_j=\langle f,\phi_j\rangle$.

= Ritz-Galerkin equivalence for a symmetric coercive form
{c}
{parent=Rayleigh-Ritz method}
{title2=$J(v)-J(u)=\tfrac12a(v-u,v-u)$}

For a bounded symmetric <coercive bilinear form> on a Hilbert space, a solution of $a(u,w)=\ell(w)$ minimizes $J(v)=a(v,v)/2-\ell(v)$. The displayed identity proves strict minimality and uniqueness. Restricting to a <conforming finite element space> makes the discrete <Ritz method> equivalent to the <Galerkin method>. <Galerkin orthogonality> gives exact best approximation in the <energy norm>; the <Céa lemma> translates it into a reference-norm estimate.

= Symmetry and coercivity in quadratic energy minimization
{parent=Ritz-Galerkin equivalence for a symmetric coercive form}
{title2=$I(v)=a(v,v)-2\ell(v)$}

A continuous symmetric <coercive bilinear form> $a$ on a real <Hilbert space> and a continuous <linear functional> $\ell$ give a unique minimum of $I$. The parallelogram identity makes any minimizing sequence Cauchy in the energy norm; coercivity and completeness give its limit. Differentiation gives $a(u,w)=\ell(w)$, and $I(v)-I(u)=a(v-u,v-u)$ proves uniqueness. A nonsymmetric operator instead differentiates to its symmetric part. Mere pointwise positivity without a lower bound in the chosen norm need not yield a minimizing vector.

= Energy norm
{parent=Rayleigh-Ritz method}

A symmetric coercive bilinear form $a$ defines the energy norm $\|v\|_a=\sqrt{a(v,v)}$. In this norm the <Ritz method> is an orthogonal projection onto its finite-dimensional trial space.

= Galerkin orthogonality
{c}
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$a(u-u_h,v_h)=0$}

Let $V_h\subset V$ be a <conforming finite element space>. Suppose the exact solution satisfies $a(u,v)=\ell(v)$ for every $v\in V$ and its <Galerkin method> approximation satisfies $a(u_h,v_h)=\ell(v_h)$ for every $v_h\in V_h$, using the same <bilinear form> and <linear functional>. Subtracting the two identities gives <Galerkin orthogonality>:
$$
a(u-u_h,v_h)=0\qquad(v_h\in V_h).
$$
This identity itself does not require symmetry. Its interpretation as an <orthogonal projection> in an <energy norm> does require a bounded symmetric <coercive bilinear form>: then $a$ is an <inner product>, $e=u-u_h$ is perpendicular to every available discrete correction, and
$$
\|u-v_h\|_a^2=\|u-u_h\|_a^2+\|u_h-v_h\|_a^2\qquad(v_h\in V_h).
$$
Consequently $u_h$ is the unique closest point in $V_h$ in the <energy norm>. This <Pythagorean theorem> in a function space explains the beauty of the <Galerkin method>: integrated local equations compute a global best approximation. The <Céa lemma> supplies the corresponding estimate in a chosen reference norm.

For the <finite element solution of the one-dimensional Poisson equation>, $a(w,v)=\int_0^1w'v'\,dx$ and $\|w\|_a=\|w'\|_{L^2(0,1)}$. Testing the error against the $i$th <piecewise-linear hat function> on a uniform <mesh> gives
$$
\frac1h\int_{x_{i-1}}^{x_i}e'\,dx
-\frac1h\int_{x_i}^{x_{i+1}}e'\,dx=0.
$$
In this model $e$ vanishes at every node, so each of these <integrals> is individually zero, although $e$ is positive inside every element. <Galerkin orthogonality> controls the derivative-weighted balance; it does not say that the pointwise error vanishes.

The exact identity assumes conformity and exact evaluation of the continuous forms, including any <numerical integration> that is used. If assembly instead uses $a_h$ and $\ell_h$, subtraction gives the consistency defect
$$
a(u-u_h,v_h)=(\ell-\ell_h)(v_h)+(a_h-a)(u_h,v_h).
$$
Thus inexact <numerical integration> need not preserve the projection. In the <Petrov-Galerkin method> the error is tested against a different test space, and for a nonsymmetric <bilinear form> the displayed identity need not describe perpendicularity in any <energy norm>.

= Céa's lemma
{c}
{parent=Galerkin orthogonality}
{wiki}

= Céa lemma
{c}
{synonym}

For a continuous coercive bilinear form with continuity constant $M$ and coercivity constant $\alpha$, a conforming finite-element solution satisfies
$$
\|u-u_h\|_V\leq\frac M\alpha
\inf_{v_h\in V_h}\|u-v_h\|_V.
$$
For a symmetric problem measured in its <energy norm>, the constant is one.

For any $v_h\in V_h$, <Galerkin orthogonality> gives $a(e,e)=a(e,u-v_h)$, where $e=u-u_h$. Coercivity and continuity yield $\alpha\|e\|_V^2\leq M\|e\|_V\|u-v_h\|_V$. Divide by $\|e\|_V$ when it is nonzero and take the infimum. In the symmetric case, the <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> in the <energy norm> gives the constant one.

= Aubin–Nitsche duality argument
{parent=Céa's lemma}
{c}

For a symmetric elliptic variational problem with <Galerkin orthogonality>, let $e=u-u_h$ and solve the dual problem $a(v,z)=(e,v)_{L^2}$ for every $v\in H_0^1(\Omega)$. If <elliptic regularity> gives $\|z\|_{H^2}\leq C\|e\|_{L^2}$, then a <finite element interpolation estimate> gives
$$
\|e\|_{L^2}^2=a(e,z-I_hz)
\leq Ch\|e\|_{H^1}\|z\|_{H^2}
\leq Ch\|e\|_{H^1}\|e\|_{L^2}.
$$
Thus $\|e\|_{L^2}\leq Ch\|e\|_{H^1}$. Combining this with the <Céa lemma> and an $O(h)$ <energy norm> error gives an $O(h^2)$ <L2 norm> error. The additional order depends on the dual regularity assumption, which can fail on unsuitable domains.

= Piecewise-linear hat function
{parent=Finite element method}
{title2=$\phi_i(x_j)=\delta_{ij}$}

= Hat function
{synonym}

= Chapeau function
{synonym}

A <piecewise-linear hat function> is a continuous <piecewise linear function> that selects one node of a <finite element mesh>: it is one there and zero at every other node. For an interior node $x_i$, its exact formula on a possibly nonuniform <mesh> is
$$
\phi_i(x)=\begin{cases}
(x-x_{i-1})/(x_i-x_{i-1}),&x_{i-1}\leq x\leq x_i,\\
(x_{i+1}-x)/(x_{i+1}-x_i),&x_i\leq x\leq x_{i+1},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
$$
Its <support of a function> is $[x_{i-1},x_{i+1}]$. It rises on the left <interval> and falls on the right, producing its hat-shaped graph. The interior <hat functions> form a <basis> of the continuous $P_1$ <conforming finite element space> with homogeneous <Dirichlet boundary conditions>. Chen's author notes define this nodal <basis> and illustrate it in one and two dimensions.https://webapps.math.uci.edu/~chenlong/226/Ch2FEM.pdf[Chen, Introduction to Finite Element Methods, §3.1, pp. 5–6 and Figure 5: nodal hat basis]{ref} The nodal property $\phi_i(x_j)=\delta_{ij}$ makes the <coefficients> directly readable: if $v_h=\sum_i c_i\phi_i$, then $v_h(x_i)=c_i$. Unlike the global <Lagrange cardinal polynomials>, each of these functions is confined to two neighboring elements.

For example, with $x_j=j/4$, the middle <hat function> is $\phi_2(x)=4x-1$ on $[1/4,1/2]$, $3-4x$ on $[1/2,3/4]$, and zero elsewhere. Hence $\phi_2(3/8)=1/2$, while $\phi_1(3/8)=1/2$ and $\phi_3(3/8)=0$. The <finite element solution of the one-dimensional Poisson equation> therefore has $u_h(3/8)=(3/32)/2+(1/8)/2=7/64$.

Local assembly makes this locality computational. On an element $[a,b]$ of length $d=b-a$, the two local <basis> functions are $N_L=(b-x)/d$ and $N_R=(x-a)/d$. For the <Poisson equation> with unit forcing, their local <stiffness matrix> and load vector are
$$
K^{(e)}=\frac1d\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
F^{(e)}=\frac d2\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Add these four matrix entries and two loads to the rows and columns belonging to the element's endpoints, then impose the prescribed boundary values. An interior node receives contributions from its two neighboring elements. Writing $d_L=x_i-x_{i-1}$ and $d_R=x_{i+1}-x_i$ gives
$$
K_{ii}=\frac1{d_L}+\frac1{d_R},\qquad
K_{i,i-1}=-\frac1{d_L},\qquad K_{i,i+1}=-\frac1{d_R},\qquad
F_i=\frac{d_L+d_R}{2},
$$
with off-diagonal entries included only for interior neighbors. On a uniform <mesh> these reduce to $2/h$, $-1/h$ and $h$. Nonadjacent <hat functions> have no derivative overlap of positive length, so all distant entries vanish. This produces a <sparse matrix> with only three diagonals: the global coupling is built entirely from local two-node interactions.

\Image[finite-element-hat-assembly.png]
{title=Three interior hat functions on a four-interval mesh, with local stiffness and load contributions assembled into the three-unknown Poisson system}

= Arithmetic operation
{parent=Numerical analysis}

An arithmetic operation is one scalar addition, subtraction, multiplication, or division. Counting such operations gives a basic model of an algorithm's computational cost.

= Schur stability criterion
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{c}

For a real monic quadratic $z^2+a_1z+a_2$, both roots lie in the closed unit disk when
$$
|a_2|\leq1,
\qquad
1+a_1+a_2\geq0,
\qquad
1-a_1+a_2\geq0,
$$
with the usual simplicity requirement for roots on the unit circle.

= Complex quadratic Schur criterion
{parent=Schur stability criterion}
{title2=$|\overline a b-c\overline b|\leq |a|^2-|c|^2$}

For a complex <polynomial> $p(z)=az^2+bz+c$, assume $|a|>|c|$ and put $D=|a|^2-|c|^2$ and $E=\overline a b-c\overline b$. Both <roots of a polynomial> lie in the closed unit disk if and only if $|E|\leq D$. Strict inequality puts both roots in the open disk. Under the strict product assumption $|c/a|<1$, any unit-modulus root is automatically simple.

To prove the strict version, define $p^\#(z)=z^2\overline{p(1/\overline z)}$ and $q=\overline a p-cp^\#=z(Dz+E)$. On the unit circle $|p^\#|=|p|$, so the <Rouche theorem> shows that a stable $p$ and $q$ have the same two interior zeros. Conversely $Dp=aq+cq^\#$, and the same <Rouche theorem> implies that two interior zeros of $q$ give two interior zeros of $p$. The nonzero root of $q$ is $-E/D$, proving the criterion. The closed version follows by continuity, replacing $b$ by $(1-\varepsilon)b$ in the sufficiency direction. In the necessity direction with a boundary root, replace $p(z)$ by $p(z/r)$ for $r<1$ close to one and let $r\to1$. The product of the two <roots of a polynomial> excludes two unit-modulus roots and excludes a repeated unit-modulus root.

= Lie product formula
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{c}
{wiki}

= Lie-Trotter product formula
{c}
{synonym}

= Lie-Trotter splitting
{c}
{synonym}

= Trotter product formula
{c}
{synonym}

For bounded <matrices> $A_j$, the Lie product formula gives <operator norm> convergence
$$
e^{t\sum_jA_j}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(\prod_je^{tA_j/k}\right)^k.
$$
For two summands, expanding their <matrix exponentials> gives the one-step difference below. For <Hermitian matrices> multiplied by $-i$, the factors are <unitary operators> and the <first-order unitary product-formula error bound> gives an explicit error estimate.

= Exponential product defect identity
{parent=Lie product formula}
{title2=$e^{tA}e^{tB}-e^{t(A+B)}$}

For finite <matrices>, differentiate the ordered product to obtain $\Phi'-(A+B)\Phi=[e^{tA},B]e^{tB}$. Multiplying the error by $e^{-t(A+B)}$ and integrating gives $\Phi(t)-e^{t(A+B)}=\int_0^t e^{(t-s)(A+B)}[e^{sA},B]e^{sB}\,ds$. This exact <variation-of-constants formula> identifies noncommutativity as the source of <Lie-Trotter splitting> error.

= Decaying bound for symmetric negative matrix splitting
{parent=Exponential product defect identity}
{title2=$\|E(t)\|_2\leq2\|B\|_2(e^{t(a+b)}-e^{tc})/(a+b-c)$}

For symmetric negative-definite <matrices>, put $a=\lambda_{\max}(A)$, $b=\lambda_{\max}(B)$ and $c=\lambda_{\max}(A+B)\leq a+b$. The <spectral theorem> gives exact exponential norm bounds, and $\|[e^{sA},B]\|_2\leq2\|B\|_2e^{sa}$. Integration in the <exponential product defect identity> proves the displayed estimate if $c<a+b$. At equality its continuous limit is $2t\|B\|_2e^{tc}$; the quotient must not be interpreted as division by zero.

= Symmetrized exponential-splitting defect identity
{parent=Lie product formula}
{title2=$F(t)=\tfrac12(e^{tA}e^{tB}+e^{tB}e^{tA})$}

For bounded <matrices>, differentiating the symmetrized product gives $F'-(A+B)F=\tfrac12([e^{tB},A]e^{tA}+[e^{tA},B]e^{tB})$. The <variation-of-constants formula> turns this into an integral expression for $F-e^{t(A+B)}$. This retains the noncommutativity of the factors explicitly; cancellation of their leading opposite <commutators> explains why the averaged product is more accurate than either ordering alone.

= Lie-Trotter splitting commutator error
{c}
{parent=Lie product formula}

For bounded <matrices>,
$$
e^{hB}e^{hC}=e^{h(B+C)}+\frac{h^2}{2}(BC-CB)+O(h^3).
$$
Commutativity removes this splitting error, though first-order substeps such as <Backward Euler method> retain their own quadratic local errors.

= First-order unitary product-formula error bound
{parent=Lie product formula}

For <Hermitian matrices> $H_1,\ldots,H_m$ and $t\geq0$, let $S(t)=\prod_{j=1}^m e^{-itH_j}$. Then
$$
\boxed{\left\|S(t)-e^{-it\sum_jH_j}\right\|
\leq\frac{t^2}{2}\sum_{j<l}\|[H_j,H_l]\|.}
$$
Here every norm is the <spectral norm>. A short proof starts with two summands. Differentiate $F(s)=e^{-i(t-s)(A+B)}e^{-isA}e^{-isB}$; its derivative has norm at most $\|[e^{-isA},B]\|$. Differentiating $e^{-iuA}Be^{iuA}$ and integrating yields $\|[e^{-isA},B]\|\leq s\|[A,B]\|$, because <unitary operators> preserve the norm. Integrating $s$ from zero to $t$ gives $t^2\|[A,B]\|/2$. Inductively separate $H_1$ from the remaining sum, use the <triangle inequality> on their <commutator>, and apply the <telescoping bound for products of operators> to obtain the displayed many-term bound.

Repeating steps of size $t/k$ and telescoping across $k$ steps gives
$$
\left\|S(t/k)^k-e^{-it\sum_jH_j}\right\|
\leq\frac{t^2}{2k}\sum_{j<l}\|[H_j,H_l]\|.
$$
For a chain of $O(n)$ bounded nearest-neighbor terms, only $O(n)$ pairs fail to commute. Consequently first-order <product-formula Hamiltonian simulation> has error $O(nt^2/k)$ and uses $O(nk)$ constant-size gates. The general bound is also given in Proposition 9 of https://journals.aps.org/prx/pdf/10.1103/PhysRevX.11.011020[Childs and collaborators' analysis of Trotter error].

= Strang splitting
{c}
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

Strang splitting approximates $e^{h(A+B)}$ by $e^{hA/2}e^{hB}e^{hA/2}$. Its time symmetry cancels the quadratic local commutator defect, giving local error $O(h^3)$ and global order two.

= Strang splitting contraction for symmetric diffusion and skew advection
{parent=Strang splitting}
{title2=$\|e^{kA/2}e^{kB}e^{kA/2}\|_2\leq1$}

If real matrices satisfy $A^T=A\leq0$ and $B^T=-B$, then $e^{tA}$ is a contraction and $e^{tB}$ is an <orthogonal matrix> for $t\geq0$. Hence $\|e^{kA/2}e^{kB}e^{kA/2}\|_2\leq1$ for every $k\geq0$. The bound is independent of commutativity and gives unconditional stability for a <centered convection-diffusion semidiscretization> with homogeneous <Dirichlet boundary conditions>. It does not itself bound the mesh-dependent commutators that enter a time-error estimate.

= Higher-order composition of a symmetric splitting
{parent=Strang splitting}

If $S_h$ is a symmetric second-order splitting, then $S_{\gamma h}S_{\delta h}S_{\gamma h}$ is fourth order for $\gamma=(2-2^{1/3})^{-1}$ and $\delta=-2^{1/3}(2-2^{1/3})^{-1}$. The identities $2\gamma+\delta=1$ and $2\gamma^3+\delta^3=0$ preserve the total step and cancel the leading odd defect.

= Composite midpoint rule error
{parent=Numerical analysis}

For $f\in C^2[a,b]$, the composite midpoint rule with mesh width $h$ has local error $O(h^3)$ and global error $O(h^2)$.

= Quadratic substitution for a square-root endpoint singularity
{parent=Composite midpoint rule error}

If $f(x)=x^{-1/2}g(x)$ with $g$ smooth near zero, the substitution $x=s^2$ gives
$$
\int_0^1f(x)\,dx=\int_0^12g(s^2)\,ds.
$$
The transformed integrand is smooth, so an ordinary second-order midpoint rule recovers its usual convergence rate.

= Interpolation
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}
{title2=$g(x_i)=y_i$}

Interpolation constructs a <function> agreeing exactly with prescribed values at sample points. The resulting function is an <interpolant>. Choosing a family determines how missing values are filled: <polynomial interpolation> selects a <polynomial>, while <linear interpolation> joins adjacent samples by straight segments. The <McShane extension theorem> instead permits any <Lipschitz function> with a specified bound and provides envelopes controlling all allowed choices.

= Interpolant
{parent=Interpolation}
{title2=$g(x_i)=y_i$}

An interpolant is a <function> satisfying the prescribed sample conditions $g(x_i)=y_i$. It agrees with the data exactly, but need not agree with an unknown underlying function between samples. The <polynomial interpolation> example shows that a family or a <polynomial degree> restriction can supply uniqueness; the <McShane extension theorem> shows a useful nonunique alternative.

= Polynomial interpolation
{parent=Interpolation}
{wiki}
{title2=$p_n(x_i)=y_i,\quad\deg p_n\leq n$}

<Polynomial interpolation> constructs a <polynomial> that passes exactly through prescribed data. Given $n+1$ distinct <interpolation nodes> $x_0,\ldots,x_n$ and values $y_0,\ldots,y_n$, there is exactly one <interpolation polynomial> $p_n$ of <polynomial degree> at most $n$ with $p_n(x_i)=y_i$ for every $i$. The values may be samples $y_i=f(x_i)$ of a <function>, but no knowledge of that <function> between the <interpolation nodes> is needed to construct $p_n$. The <Lagrange polynomial> and <Newton polynomial> are two representations of this same answer.https://fncbook.com/polynomial/[Toby A. Driscoll and Richard J. Braun, Fundamentals of Numerical Computation, §9.1, Theorems 9.1.1–9.1.2]{ref}

For example, the three data points $(0,1),(1,2),(2,5)$ give the <quadratic polynomial>
$$
p_2(x)=x^2+1,\qquad p_2(0)=1,\quad p_2(1)=2,\quad p_2(2)=5.
$$
Thus <polynomial interpolation> supplies $p_2(1/2)=5/4$ as an intermediate value. Whether this approximates an unknown underlying <function> well is a separate question about <polynomial interpolation error>.

Both hypotheses in the uniqueness statement matter. The <Lagrange cardinal polynomials> prove existence constructively. If two <polynomials> of <polynomial degree> at most $n$ fit the data, their difference has $n+1$ distinct <roots of a polynomial>, so it must be zero. Repeated <interpolation nodes> with conflicting values have no solution, and repeated equal values provide no additional independent condition; prescribed <derivatives> instead lead to <Hermite interpolation>. Without the <polynomial degree> restriction there are infinitely many solutions:
$$
p(x)=p_n(x)+q(x)\prod_{i=0}^n(x-x_i),
$$
where $q$ is any <polynomial>. By the <factor theorem>, this describes every <polynomial> fitting the same data. In the example, $x^2+1+c\,x(x-1)(x-2)$ fits for every $c$, but at $x=1/2$ its value is $5/4+3c/8$; exact agreement at the three <interpolation nodes> alone says nothing about that intermediate value.

The usefulness of <polynomial interpolation> is that data become an explicit <function> whose values, <derivatives>, and <integrals> can be calculated. Differentiating or integrating the <Lagrange cardinal polynomials> produces <differentiation weights from nodal evaluations> or a <quadrature rule>. Exact passage through observations is the defining constraint. In <polynomial regression>, <linear least squares> instead chooses a <polynomial degree> and minimizes a sum of squared residuals, generally without passing through every observation. Interpolating noisy values reproduces their errors at the <interpolation nodes> and can amplify them between <interpolation nodes>; <polynomial interpolation> does not itself smooth measurement noise. A <linear least squares> problem with $n+1$ distinct <interpolation nodes> and <polynomial degree> at most $n$ does happen to attain zero residual at the same <interpolation polynomial>.

= Interpolation polynomial
{parent=Polynomial interpolation}
{title2=$p(x_i)=y_i$}

An interpolation polynomial for distinct nodes $x_0,\ldots,x_n$ and prescribed values $y_0,\ldots,y_n$ is a <polynomial> $p$ satisfying $p(x_i)=y_i$. There is a unique such <polynomial> of degree at most $n$: the <Lagrange interpolation polynomial> constructs it explicitly, and uniqueness follows because a nonzero degree-at-most-$n$ difference cannot have $n+1$ distinct roots. <Newton interpolation polynomials> express the same object using <divided differences>.

= Interpolation nodes from best uniform approximation
{parent=Polynomial interpolation}
{title2=$\ell_n(f)=p_n^*$}

For a real <continuous function> on a compact interval, choose its <best uniform approximation> $p_n^*$ of <polynomial degree> at most $n$. If its error is nonzero, the <Chebyshev alternation theorem> supplies $n+2$ ordered alternating extrema. The <intermediate value theorem> gives a zero of $f-p_n^*$ in each intervening interval. At these $n+1$ distinct points, the <polynomial interpolant> is exactly $p_n^*$. If the best error is zero, arbitrary distinct nodes suffice. The <Weierstrass approximation theorem> shows that these function-dependent interpolants converge uniformly. The nodes depend on the target function; the conclusion does not provide one universal node sequence for every continuous target.

= Interpolation node
{parent=Polynomial interpolation}
{title2=$x_i$}

An <interpolation node> is an argument value $x_i$ at which an <interpolation polynomial> must take a prescribed value $y_i$. The <interpolation nodes> specify where the constraints apply; the values specify how high the graph must pass there. In the three-point example, the <interpolation nodes> are $0,1,2$, while the prescribed values are $1,2,5$. Changing the <interpolation nodes> changes the <Lagrange cardinal polynomials> and the <polynomial interpolation error>, even if their number stays fixed.

= Chebyshev-Lobatto node
{c}
{parent=Interpolation node}
{title2=$x_k=\cos(k\pi/n),\quad k=0,\ldots,n$}

= Chebyshev extreme point
{c}
{synonym}

For $n\geq1$, the $n+1$ <Chebyshev-Lobatto nodes> are the <extrema> of the <Chebyshev polynomial> $T_n$ on $[-1,1]$, including both endpoints: $T_n(x_k)=(-1)^k$. Imagine $n+1$ equally spaced angles on a <semicircle> and project their points onto the horizontal axis. The <interpolation nodes> crowd near $-1$ and $1$ because equal angular steps have smaller horizontal projections there. Reversing the order gives the same set. These are distinct from the <roots of a polynomial> $\cos((2k+1)\pi/(2n+2))$ of $T_{n+1}$, which omit the endpoints. Driscoll and Braun call this endpoint-including family Chebyshev points of the second kind or Chebyshev extreme points.https://fncbook.com/python/stability-1/[Driscoll and Braun, Fundamentals of Numerical Computation, §9.3.2, equation 9.3.2]{ref}

On an interval $[a,b]$, use $x_k=(a+b)/2+(b-a)\cos(k\pi/n)/2$. The <Runge phenomenon> example compares <polynomial interpolation> at eleven <Chebyshev-Lobatto nodes> with eleven equally spaced <interpolation nodes>. The distinction matters whenever sampling locations are a choice rather than fixed observations.

= Runge's phenomenon
{c}
{parent=Polynomial interpolation}
{wiki}

= Runge phenomenon
{c}
{synonym}

The <Runge phenomenon> is the failure of increasing-degree <polynomial interpolation> at equally spaced <interpolation nodes> to converge to some smooth target <functions>, with large oscillations near the ends of the interval. It is a property of the approximation problem, and can occur even with exact arithmetic. A numerically stable evaluation method cannot remove it. Endpoint clustering, as in <Chebyshev-Lobatto nodes>, changes the approximation problem and can greatly improve it.https://fncbook.com/python/stability-1/[Driscoll and Braun, Fundamentals of Numerical Computation, §9.3.1–9.3.2]{ref}

Take $f(x)=1/(1+25x^2)$ on $[-1,1]$ and <polynomial degree> at most $10$. Use either $x_k=-1+k/5$ or $x_k=\cos(k\pi/10)$ for $k=0,\ldots,10$. Each <interpolation polynomial> passes through all eleven of its own samples, yet the first has severe endpoint oscillations and the second stays much closer to the target <function>. Evaluating both by the <barycentric interpolation formula> on $20{,}001$ equally spaced points, including the endpoints, gives sampled maximum absolute errors of approximately $1.915659$ and $0.132197$, respectively: about a $14.49$-fold reduction. These are maxima over the stated grid, not certified maxima over the continuous interval. The generator separately checks exact reproduction of all stored nodal values.

\Image[interpolation-node-placement.png]
{title=Degree-10 interpolation of the Runge function at equally spaced and Chebyshev-Lobatto nodes, with absolute errors}

The <polynomial interpolation error> formula explains why <interpolation nodes> enter the analysis. If $f$ has $n+1$ continuous <derivatives> on an interval containing $x$ and all the <interpolation nodes>, then for a suitable $\xi_x$ in that interval,
$$
f(x)-p_n(x)=\frac{f^{(n+1)}(\xi_x)}{(n+1)!}\prod_{i=0}^n(x-x_i).
$$
At an <interpolation node> the error is zero directly. Away from the <interpolation nodes>, both the product and the high-order <derivative> matter. A short proof applies <Rolle's theorem> repeatedly to $f(t)-p_n(t)-[f(x)-p_n(x)]\prod_i(t-x_i)/\prod_i(x-x_i)$, which vanishes at $n+2$ distinct points.https://fncbook.com/polynomial/[Driscoll and Braun, Fundamentals of Numerical Computation, §9.1.2, Theorem 9.1.3]{ref}

The successful comparison here does not assert that <Chebyshev-Lobatto nodes> solve every approximation problem for every <function>. The <derivatives>, the <polynomial degree>, and measurement errors still matter. The <Lebesgue constant of interpolation> measures worst-case amplification of nodal errors, while the <polynomial reproduction error bound> relates interpolation to the <best uniform approximation> available at that <polynomial degree>.

= Lagrange polynomial
{c}
{parent=Polynomial interpolation}
{wiki}
{title2=$p_n(x)=\sum_{i=0}^n y_i\ell_i(x)$}

= Lagrange interpolation polynomial
{c}
{synonym}

= Lagrange interpolation
{c}
{synonym}

= Polynomial interpolant
{synonym}

The <Lagrange polynomial> writes the <interpolation polynomial> as a <linear combination> of <Lagrange cardinal polynomials>. Each one selects a single <interpolation node>, so the data values themselves become the coefficients:
$$
p_n(x)=\sum_{i=0}^n y_i\ell_i(x),\qquad
\ell_i(x)=\prod_{\substack{0\leq j\leq n\\j\ne i}}\frac{x-x_j}{x_i-x_j}.
$$
Distinct <interpolation nodes> make every denominator nonzero. At $x_j$, all terms vanish except $y_j\ell_j(x_j)=y_j$. This gives an explicit construction without solving for <monomial> coefficients. The elegance of the <Lagrange polynomial> is that it builds all constraints into the <basis>, leaving no equations to solve for the data values.

For $(x_0,x_1,x_2)=(0,1,2)$ and $(y_0,y_1,y_2)=(1,2,5)$, substitution gives
$$
p_2(x)=\frac{(x-1)(x-2)}2-2x(x-2)+\frac{5x(x-1)}2=x^2+1.
$$
At $x=1/2$, the <Lagrange cardinal polynomial> values are $3/8,3/4,-1/8$, so $p_2(1/2)=3/8+2(3/4)+5(-1/8)=5/4$. A negative <Lagrange cardinal polynomial> value is allowed: these weights sum to one but need not be <probabilities>.

The product form is especially useful for deriving <differentiation weights from nodal evaluations> and <quadrature rules>. For repeated numerical evaluation, the <barycentric interpolation formula> reorganizes the same <Lagrange polynomial> into precomputed weights and sums; it avoids evaluating every full product at every argument.

= Differentiation weights from nodal evaluations
{parent=Lagrange polynomial}
{title2=$p'(x_0)=\sum_j\ell_j'(x_0)p(a_j)$}

At distinct nodes $a_0,\ldots,a_n$, the <Lagrange interpolation polynomial> has basis $\ell_j(x)=\prod_{i\ne j}(x-a_i)/(a_j-a_i)$. Differentiating its exact expression for a polynomial of degree at most $n$ gives the displayed weights. Their uniqueness follows because the nodal evaluations form a basis of the <dual space> of that polynomial space.

= Discrete minimax interpolation on ordered nodes
{parent=Lagrange polynomial}
{title2=$p=t-\lambda r,\quad\lambda=[x^{n+1}]t/[x^{n+1}]r$}

For $n+2$ increasingly ordered nodes $y_j$, let $r_j$ be their <Lagrange cardinal polynomials>, $t=\sum f(y_j)r_j$, and $r=\sum(-1)^jr_j$. The leading coefficient of $r_j$ has sign $(-1)^{n+2-j}$, so all summands in the leading coefficient of $r$ have the same nonzero sign. There is a unique $\lambda$ canceling degree $n+1$ in $t-\lambda r$. Its errors at the nodes are $\lambda(-1)^j$, and a better degree-at-most-$n$ approximation would have a difference polynomial with at least $n+1$ roots. Thus it minimizes the maximum node error. Increasing order matters: for the permutation $(0,1/3,1,2/3)$ the alternating interpolant is already the quadratic $-1+9x(1-x)$, so the leading-coefficient assertion for $n=2$ fails.

= Lagrange cardinal polynomial
{parent=Lagrange polynomial}
{c}
{title2=$\ell_i$}

= Lagrange basis polynomial
{c}
{synonym}

For distinct <interpolation nodes> $x_0,\ldots,x_n$, the <Lagrange cardinal polynomial> $\ell_i$ has value one at its own <interpolation node> and zero at every other <interpolation node>:
$$
\ell_i(x)=\prod_{j\ne i}\frac{x-x_j}{x_i-x_j},\qquad
\ell_i(x_j)=\delta_{ij}=
\begin{cases}1,&i=j,\\0,&i\ne j.\end{cases}
$$
Here $\delta_{ij}$ is the <Kronecker delta>. These <Lagrange cardinal polynomials> form a <basis> of the <vector space> of <polynomials> of <polynomial degree> at most $n$: every such <polynomial> has the unique expansion $p(x)=\sum_i p(x_i)\ell_i(x)$. The nodal evaluation <linear functionals> $E_j(p)=p(x_j)$ form the <dual basis>, since $E_j(\ell_i)=\delta_{ij}$.

For the <interpolation nodes> $0,1,2$, the three <Lagrange cardinal polynomials> are
$$
\ell_0(x)=\frac{(x-1)(x-2)}2,\qquad
\ell_1(x)=-x(x-2),\qquad
\ell_2(x)=\frac{x(x-1)}2.
$$
Their values on the <interpolation nodes> are the rows of the <identity matrix>. In the figure, each colored curve passes through its own unit-height marker and through zero at the other two <interpolation nodes>. Multiplying the curves by the data $1,2,5$ and adding gives the <Lagrange polynomial> $x^2+1$.

\Image[lagrange-interpolation-cardinals.png]
{title=Lagrange cardinal polynomials at nodes 0, 1, 2 and their weighted sum interpolating values 1, 2, 5}

Since the constant <polynomial> one interpolates data values all equal to one, uniqueness gives $\sum_i\ell_i(x)=1$. Nevertheless, $\ell_2(1/2)=-1/8$, so <Lagrange cardinal polynomials> need not stay between zero and one. If a single data value $y_i$ changes by $\varepsilon$, the <interpolation polynomial> changes by exactly $\varepsilon\ell_i(x)$. Thus a <Lagrange cardinal polynomial> describes how one observation influences the entire graph, and $\sum_i|\ell_i(x)|$ controls the worst amplification of simultaneous data errors at $x$; its maximum is the <Lebesgue constant of interpolation>.

= Endpoint derivative signs of Lagrange cardinal polynomials
{parent=Lagrange cardinal polynomial}
{title2=$(-1)^iL_i^{(k)}(1)>0$}

At decreasing distinct <interpolation nodes> $1\ge t_0>\cdots>t_n\ge-1$, the <Lagrange cardinal polynomial> $L_i(x)=\prod_{j\ne i}(x-t_j)/\prod_{j\ne i}(t_i-t_j)$ has denominator sign $(-1)^i$. For $1\le k\le n$, its numerator's $k$th <derivative> at one is $k!$ times a sum of products of $n-k$ nonnegative factors $1-t_j$. At most one factor vanishes, and at least one product avoids it, so that sum is strictly positive. The same alternating nodal signs therefore maximize every endpoint derivative simultaneously.

= Nodal derivative norm from Lagrange cardinal polynomials
{parent=Lagrange cardinal polynomial}
{title2=$\sup_{|p(t_i)|\le1}|p^{(k)}(x)|=\sum_i|\ell_i^{(k)}(x)|$}

At distinct <interpolation nodes>, differentiating <Lagrange interpolation> gives $p^{(k)}(x)=\sum_i p(t_i)\ell_i^{(k)}(x)$. The <triangle inequality> gives the displayed upper bound, attained by prescribing each nodal value to be the sign of the corresponding <derivative> weight. For increasingly ordered real nodes, the <Markov interlacing lemma> and <monotonicity of polynomial critical points in their roots> imply that $(-1)^i\ell_i^{(k)}(x)$ has at most one sign change, ignoring zero weights. Thus an extremizer has alternating nodal signs with at most one reversal of that alternating pattern. This reduces a choice among $2^{n+1}$ sign patterns to $n+1$ candidate <polynomials>.

= Barycentric interpolation formula
{parent=Lagrange polynomial}
{title2=$p_n(x)=\dfrac{\sum_i w_i y_i/(x-x_i)}{\sum_i w_i/(x-x_i)}$}

The <barycentric interpolation formula> evaluates the same <Lagrange polynomial> using weights that depend only on the distinct <interpolation nodes>:
$$
w_i=\frac1{\prod_{j\ne i}(x_i-x_j)},\qquad
p_n(x)=\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^n\frac{w_i y_i}{x-x_i}}
{\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^n\frac{w_i}{x-x_i}},\qquad x\ne x_i.
$$
At $x=x_i$, return $y_i$ directly, before dividing; the formula has <removable singularities> at the <interpolation nodes>. Multiplying every $w_i$ by the same nonzero constant leaves the answer unchanged. Driscoll and Braun derive this formula as a more efficient, numerically stable way to evaluate <polynomial interpolation>.https://fncbook.com/barycentric/[Driscoll and Braun, Fundamentals of Numerical Computation, §9.2, Theorem 9.2.1 and Algorithm 9.2.1]{ref}

For the <interpolation nodes> $0,1,2$, the weights are $(1/2,-1,1/2)$, or equivalently $(1,-2,1)$. With data $(1,2,5)$, at $x=1/2$ the denominator using the latter weights is $2+4-2/3=16/3$ and the numerator is $2+8-10/3=20/3$, giving $p_2(1/2)=5/4$ again. For eleven <interpolation nodes> in the <Runge phenomenon> figure, the generator computes the weights by the direct products above and returns stored values exactly at every <interpolation node>.

The direct products require $O(n^2)$ <arithmetic operations> once; each subsequent value uses $O(n)$ <arithmetic operations>. Here $O$ denotes <Big O notation>. At large <polynomial degree> or on poorly scaled intervals, forming these products can exceed the range of <floating-point arithmetic>; normalization or specialized weight formulas may be needed. Stable evaluation also cannot cure sensitivity intrinsic to the chosen <interpolation nodes>, measured by the <Lebesgue constant of interpolation>, or the approximation failure in the <Runge phenomenon>.

= Divided difference
{parent=Polynomial interpolation}
{wiki}
{title2=$f[x_0,\ldots,x_k]$}

For $k+1$ distinct <interpolation nodes>, the <divided difference> $f[x_0,\ldots,x_k]$ is the coefficient of $x^k$ in the <interpolation polynomial> of <polynomial degree> at most $k$ through those <interpolation nodes>. It is the <leading coefficient> when that <polynomial degree> is exactly $k$, and zero when it is smaller. Explicitly,
$$
f[x_0,\ldots,x_k]
=\sum_{j=0}^k\frac{f(x_j)}
{\prod_{i\ne j}(x_j-x_i)}.
$$
starting with $f[x_i]=f(x_i)$. The <divided differences> obey the recurrence
$$
f[x_0,\ldots,x_k]
=\frac{f[x_1,\ldots,x_k]-f[x_0,\ldots,x_{k-1}]}
{x_k-x_0}.
$$
For data $f(0)=1$, $f(1)=2$, $f(2)=5$, the first <divided differences> are $f[0,1]=(2-1)/(1-0)=1$ and $f[1,2]=(5-2)/(2-1)=3$. The second <divided difference> is $f[0,1,2]=(3-1)/(2-0)=1$, the <leading coefficient> of $x^2+1$. The first <divided difference> is the slope of a <secant line>; higher <divided differences> supply successive coefficients for the <Newton polynomial>.

= Omitted-node identities for divided differences
{parent=Divided difference}
{title2=$D_i-D_j=(x_j-x_i)f[x_0,\ldots,x_n]$}

For distinct <interpolation nodes>, write $D_i$ for the <divided difference> with $x_i$ omitted. Symmetry and the divided-difference recurrence give the displayed identity. Consequently, for $0<i<n$,
$$
D_i=\frac{x_i-x_0}{x_n-x_0}D_n+\frac{x_n-x_i}{x_n-x_0}D_0.
$$
The identity is algebraic and does not require the nodes to be ordered; the coefficients need not be nonnegative.

= Exponential divided difference
{parent=Divided difference}
{title2=$D_t(\beta,\gamma)=\int_0^te^{(t-x)\gamma+x\beta}\,dx$}

The displayed integral is $(e^{t\beta}-e^{t\gamma})/(\beta-\gamma)$ for distinct exponents and $te^{t\gamma}$ when they coincide. The latter is the continuous limit, so a zero denominator is a removable singularity. This quantity appears in <variation-of-constants formula> estimates of <matrix> evolution and avoids omitting an equal-exponent case.

= Leibniz rule for divided differences
{c}
{parent=Divided difference}

For $h=fg$,
$$
h[t_0,\ldots,t_k]=\sum_{m=0}^kf[t_0,\ldots,t_m]g[t_m,\ldots,t_k].
$$

= Newton polynomial
{c}
{parent=Divided difference}
{wiki}
{title2=$p_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n f[x_0,\ldots,x_k]\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(x-x_i)$}

= Newton interpolation polynomial
{c}
{synonym}

The <Newton polynomial> expresses the <interpolation polynomial> in products that successively vanish at all previously used <interpolation nodes>. With $y_i=f(x_i)$, the coefficients are <divided differences>:
$$
p_n(x)=f(x_0)+\sum_{k=1}^nf[x_0,\ldots,x_k]
\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(x-x_i).
$$
A triangular <divided difference> table computes all its coefficients using exactly $n(n+1)/2$ divisions. The <Newton polynomial> and the <Lagrange polynomial> have the same <polynomial degree> bound and the same nodal values, hence are equal by uniqueness of <polynomial interpolation>.

For $f(0)=1$, $f(1)=2$, $f(2)=5$, the complete <divided difference> table is
$$
\begin{array}{c|r|r|r}
x_i&f[x_i]&f[x_i,x_{i+1}]&f[x_i,x_{i+1},x_{i+2}]\\\hline
0&1&1&1\\
1&2&3&\\
2&5&&
\end{array}
$$
The first <divided differences> are $1$ and $3$, and their <divided difference> is $(3-1)/(2-0)=1$. Reading the top entries gives
$$
p_2(x)=1+1(x-0)+1(x-0)(x-1)=1+x+x(x-1)=x^2+1.
$$
The initial term fits $(0,1)$; adding $x$ also fits $(1,2)$; adding $x(x-1)$ fits $(2,5)$ while leaving the first two data values unchanged. This is the practical beauty of the <Newton polynomial>: each new correction respects all previous interpolation constraints.

If a new distinct <interpolation node> $x_{n+1}$ with value $y_{n+1}$ arrives, append one term:
$$
p_{n+1}(x)=p_n(x)+c\prod_{i=0}^n(x-x_i),\qquad
c=\frac{y_{n+1}-p_n(x_{n+1})}{\prod_{i=0}^n(x_{n+1}-x_i)}.
$$
For example, appending $(3,11)$ to the three-point data gives $c=(11-10)/(3\cdot2\cdot1)=1/6$, hence $p_3(x)=x^2+1+x(x-1)(x-2)/6$. Changing the order of the <interpolation nodes> changes the <Newton polynomial> coefficients and products, but not the final <interpolation polynomial> in exact arithmetic.

The products also permit nested evaluation analogous to <Horner's method>. If $a_k=f[x_0,\ldots,x_k]$, start with $b_n=a_n$ and work backwards by $b_k=a_k+(x-x_k)b_{k+1}$; the answer is $b_0$. The three-point example becomes $1+x[1+(x-1)]$, needing two multiplications for each evaluation. This organization is useful when adding observations incrementally or evaluating a stored <Newton polynomial> many times; the <Runge phenomenon> still depends on the <interpolation nodes> rather than on which representation is used.

= Discrete Fourier transform
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}
{title2=$X_k=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}x_ne^{-2\pi ink/N}$}

= DFT
{c}
{synonym}

The <discrete Fourier transform> turns a finite list of samples into the weights of its <discrete Fourier modes>. The goal is to reveal a simple oscillatory recipe hidden in complicated-looking data, while retaining enough information to reconstruct every sample exactly. In <signal processing>, those weights identify sampled <frequencies> and <wave phases>; in <numerical analysis>, they simplify <circular convolution> and the <discrete sine transform Poisson solver>.

Start with eight real samples, indexed by $n=0,\ldots,7$:
$$
x_n=1+\cos\left(\frac{2\pi n}{8}\right)+\frac12\sin\left(\frac{4\pi n}{8}\right).
$$
Writing $a=\sqrt2/2$, the actual list is
$$
x=\left(2,\frac32+a,1,\frac12-a,0,\frac32-a,1,\frac12+a\right).
$$
The <discrete Fourier transform> recovers the recipe as
$$
X=(8,4,-2i,0,0,0,2i,4).
$$
The constant contributes $X_0=8$; the <cosine> contributes $X_1=X_7=4$; the half-sized <sine> contributes $X_2=-2i$ and $X_6=2i$. All other <coefficients> vanish exactly. The imaginary <coefficients> carry the <sine>'s <wave phase>, which a plot of <absolute values> alone would hide.

\Image[dft-frequency-decomposition.png]
{title=Eight samples become a constant, a cosine pair, and a sine pair in the discrete Fourier transform}

Precisely, for a positive <integer> $N$ and a <vector> $x\in\mathbb C^N$, the forward <discrete Fourier transform> and <inverse discrete Fourier transform> used here are
$$
X_k=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}x_n\exp\left(-\frac{2\pi ink}{N}\right),\qquad k=0,\ldots,N-1,
$$
$$
x_n=\frac1N\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}X_k\exp\left(\frac{2\pi ink}{N}\right),\qquad n=0,\ldots,N-1.
$$
Thus the forward exponent has a minus sign and no scale factor; the inverse exponent has a plus sign and the factor $1/N$. The <coefficients> in the reconstruction are $X_k/N$, rather than $X_k$. In the example, $X_0/8=1$, the pair $4/8$ reconstructs the <cosine>, and the pair $-2i/8,2i/8$ reconstructs the half-sized <sine>. These conventions agree with the scaled <Fourier coefficients> in MIT's authored notes.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-02-introduction-to-eecs-ii-digital-communication-systems-fall-2012/06d7972059805d20e6a7db3a52ec23f2_MIT6_02F12_chap13.pdf[Hari Balakrishnan, Christopher Terman, and George Verghese, MIT 6.02 notes, Chapter 13, equations (13.15)–(13.16), 2012]{ref}

The proof idea is <orthogonality of roots of unity>: for <integers> $k,\ell\in\{0,\ldots,N-1\}$,
$$
\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}e^{2\pi in(k-\ell)/N}=\begin{cases}N,&k=\ell,\\0,&k\ne\ell.\end{cases}
$$
Distinct <discrete Fourier modes> cancel in this finite <geometric series>. Substituting the forward formula into the <inverse discrete Fourier transform> leaves only the desired sample. The same <orthogonality> makes the normalized <discrete Fourier modes> an <orthonormal basis> and gives the <discrete Parseval identity>, in the present convention,
$$
\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}|x_n|^2=\frac1N\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}|X_k|^2.
$$
For the eight-sample example, direct squaring gives $\sum_nx_n^2=13$, while $\sum_k|X_k|^2=64+16+4+4+16=104$, so $104/8=13$. This identity preserves the squared <L2 norm> after accounting for normalization. The existing <discrete Parseval identity> instead scales the forward <discrete Fourier transform> by $N^{-1/2}$, producing a <unitary matrix> and equal squared <L2 norms> without the factor $1/N$.

For real input, <discrete Fourier conjugate symmetry> pairs positive and negative <frequencies>. The label $k$ is a residue modulo $N$: $k=7$ when $N=8$ represents the same <discrete Fourier mode> as $k=-1$, since $e^{2\pi i7n/8}=e^{-2\pi in/8}$. It therefore represents negative <frequency> $-1/8$ cycles per sample. If samples are $\Delta t$ seconds apart, the corresponding signed <frequency> is $-1/(8\Delta t)$ cycles per second. Similarly, index $6$ represents $-2$, while index $4$ is its own negative modulo $8$.

The <discrete Fourier transform> is an invertible map between two finite <vectors>. The continuous <Fourier transform> instead describes a <function> on the real line by an <integral> over a continuous <frequency> variable. A finite list of samples does not determine the full continuous <frequency spectrum>: <aliasing of discrete Fourier modes> already supplies distinct waveforms with identical observations. Applying the inverse formula at arbitrary <integer> indices gives the <periodic extension of a finite sequence>, repeating the recorded block every $N$ samples. This is a property of that representation, rather than evidence that the physical <signal> repeats beyond the recording. Laurent Demanet's MIT notes place the <discrete Fourier transform> alongside the continuous <Fourier transform> and <Fourier series> to explain the role of sampling and repetition.https://math.mit.edu/icg/resources/teaching/18.330/18.330-Mar21.pdf[Laurent Demanet, Introduction to Numerical Analysis, MIT 18.330, §§6.2–6.3, pp. 57–60, March 22, 2012]{ref}

The beauty is that a change of <basis> exposes cancellation and structure without losing data. Direct evaluation needs $N$ sums of $N$ terms; a <fast Fourier transform> computes the same <discrete Fourier transform> with far less work. Computational speed and the mathematical meaning of the <coefficients> are separate questions.

= Discrete Fourier conjugate symmetry
{parent=Discrete Fourier transform}
{title2=$X_{(-k)\bmod N}=\overline{X_k}$}

For real samples, taking the <complex conjugate> of the forward <discrete Fourier transform> changes the exponent's sign and proves the displayed symmetry. Hence $X_0$ is real; for even $N$, $X_{N/2}$ is also real. In the eight-sample example, $X_7=\overline{X_1}=4$ and $X_6=\overline{X_2}=2i$. This is the finite counterpart of <conjugate symmetry of trigonometric polynomial coefficients>. It reduces storage for real <signals> and provides a quick consistency check on a computed <discrete Fourier transform>.

= Periodic extension of a finite sequence
{parent=Discrete Fourier transform}
{title2=$x_{n+N}=x_n$}

A block $x_0,\ldots,x_{N-1}$ determines a repeating <sequence> by setting $x_n=x_{n\bmod N}$ for every <integer> $n$. The <inverse discrete Fourier transform> gives this extension because each <discrete Fourier mode> satisfies $e^{2\pi ik(n+N)/N}=e^{2\pi ikn/N}$. The extension explains why <circular convolution> wraps indices around the block. It specifies values at discrete indices; a continuous curve between samples requires an additional choice of <interpolation>.

= Aliasing of discrete Fourier modes
{parent=Discrete Fourier transform}
{title2=$e^{2\pi i(m+qN)n/N}=e^{2\pi imn/N}$}

For <integers> $m,q,n$, adding $qN$ to a <frequency> index changes the <complex exponential> by $e^{2\pi iqn}=1$. Thus different continuous <Fourier modes> become identical <discrete Fourier modes> on the sampling grid. This is <aliasing>, caused by sampling before any <discrete Fourier transform> is computed.

For $N=8$, compare the continuous <functions> $\cos(2\pi mt)$ and $\sin(2\pi mt)$ with $m=1$ and $m=9$, sampled at $t=n/8$, $n=0,\ldots,7$. With $a=\sqrt2/2$, both <cosines> give exactly the first row and both <sines> give exactly the second:
$$
\begin{array}{c|rrrrrrrr}
n&0&1&2&3&4&5&6&7\\\hline
\cos(2\pi n/8)=\cos(18\pi n/8)&1&a&0&-a&-1&-a&0&a\\
\sin(2\pi n/8)=\sin(18\pi n/8)&0&a&1&a&0&-a&-1&-a
\end{array}
$$
The nine-cycle <sine> agrees in sign as well as magnitude with the one-cycle <sine>: the <frequency> indices differ by $8$, rather than by a sign reversal. Consequently, either <cosine> produces the same <discrete Fourier transform>, $X_1=X_7=4$ with all other <coefficients> zero; either <sine> produces $X_1=-4i$, $X_7=4i$, with all other <coefficients> zero.

\Image[discrete-sampling-frequency-aliasing.png]
{title=One-cycle and nine-cycle cosine and sine waves have exactly the same eight samples}

An observed low <frequency> therefore does not establish that physical high <frequencies> are absent. To exclude such alternatives, the <Nyquist–Shannon sampling theorem> requires a specified class of <bandlimited functions>.
