The Crank--Nicolson method applies the <trapezoidal rule> to a semidiscrete evolution equation. For a linear system $u'=Lu$ its update is
$$
\left(I-\frac h2L\right)u^{n+1}=\left(I+\frac h2L\right)u^n.
$$

= Contractivity of split Crank-Nicolson diffusion
{parent=Crank-Nicolson method}
{title2=$\|C_A(h)C_B(h)\|_2\le1$}

For symmetric negative semidefinite matrices $A,B$, each factor $C_A(h)=(I-hA/2)^{-1}(I+hA/2)$ has norm at most one, since its eigenvalues are $(1+h\lambda/2)/(1-h\lambda/2)$ with $\lambda\le0$. The same holds for $B$, so their ordered product is a contraction for every $h\ge0$, whether or not $A,B$ commute. This proves unconditional discrete <L2 norm> stability of the split <Crank--Nicolson method> for directional diffusion. Its splitting error remains first order in general, because the product's quadratic defect is $h^2[A,B]/2$.

= Collocation Runge-Kutta method
{parent=Implicit Runge-Kutta method}

Given distinct nodes $c_i$, a collocation Runge-Kutta method uses the Lagrange basis $\ell_j$ with $a_{ij}=\int_0^{c_i}\ell_j(\tau)d\tau$ and $b_j=\int_0^1\ell_j(\tau)d\tau$. Its stages enforce the differential equation at the collocation nodes.

This applies the <collocation method> to the time-step polynomial of an <ordinary differential equation>.

= Two-node collocation A-stability criterion
{parent=Collocation Runge-Kutta method}
{title2=$c_1+c_2\geq1$}

For distinct nodes $c_1,c_2\in[0,1]$, the two-stage <collocation Runge-Kutta method> has <stability function>
$$
R(z)=\frac{1+(1-a)z+rz^2}{1-az+dz^2},\qquad a=(c_1+c_2)/2,\quad d=c_1c_2/2,\quad r=(1-c_1)(1-c_2)/2.
$$
The denominator has no zeros in the closed left half-plane. On the imaginary axis the denominator's squared modulus minus the numerator's is $(d^2-r^2)y^4$. Thus $d\geq r$, equivalently $c_1+c_2\geq1$, is necessary and sufficient for <A-stability>, using the <maximum modulus principle> and the bound at infinity.

= Collocation order theorem
{parent=Collocation Runge-Kutta method}
{title2=$p=s+k$}

For $s$ distinct collocation nodes, let $\pi_s(t)=\prod_{i=1}^s(t-c_i)$. If $\int_0^1t^j\pi_s(t)dt=0$ for $0\le j<k$, and the next moment is nonzero, the associated interpolatory quadrature has order $s+k$, and the <collocation Runge-Kutta method> has the same classical order for sufficiently smooth differential equations. Here $0\le k\le s$. Equivalently, if the quadrature is exact through degree $p-1$ but not degree $p$, the method has order $p$. The generic order is at least $s$, <Gauss collocation methods> reach $2s$, and <Radau IIA methods> reach $2s-1$.

= Collocation endpoint superconvergence by quadrature orthogonality
{parent=Collocation order theorem}
{title2=$p=q$}

For $s$ collocation nodes, the residual of the collocation polynomial vanishes at each node and contains the factor $\omega(\tau)=\prod_i(\tau-c_i)$. Quadrature exactness through degree $q-1$ implies $\int_0^1\omega(\tau)p(\tau)d\tau=0$ for every polynomial of degree at most $q-s-1$. Expand the smooth vector-field interpolation error and the derivative of the exact flow in powers of the step. Every endpoint-error term of degree below $q$ has this vanishing weighted moment, so the endpoint local error is $O(h^{q+1})$, although the interior stage accuracy is generally only $s$. A step Lipschitz estimate and <discrete Gronwall inequality> then give global order $q$. Gaussian nodes attain $q=2s$, while the first nonexact quadrature moment bounds the order from above.

= Collocation tableau row-sum identity
{parent=Collocation Runge-Kutta method}
{title2=$A\mathbf1=c$}

For a <collocation Runge-Kutta method>, $a_{ij}=\int_0^{c_i}\ell_j(t)dt$, where the <Lagrange interpolation> basis obeys $\sum_j\ell_j=1$. Hence each row sum is its collocation node. Failure of this identity disproves a claimed collocation tableau with those nodes. The identity alone is necessary rather than sufficient: the individual integrated basis coefficients must also agree.

= Lobatto IIIA method
{parent=Collocation Runge-Kutta method}
{c}

A Lobatto IIIA method collocates at the <Lobatto quadrature> nodes, including both endpoints of the time interval. Its three-stage version has
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\5/24&1/3&-1/24\\1/6&2/3&1/6\end{pmatrix},
\qquad b=(1/6,2/3,1/6)^T,
\qquad c=(0,1/2,1)^T.
$$
The <Butcher order conditions> give order four. Its <stability function> is
$$
R(z)=\frac{1+z/2+z^2/12}{1-z/2+z^2/12},
$$
which is <A-stable> but not <L-stable>. It is not <algebraically stable>: the first diagonal entry of the defining <matrix> is $2b_1a_{11}-b_1^2=-1/36$. This distinguishes linear <A-stability> from nonlinear <algebraic stability>.

= Gauss-Legendre method
{parent=Collocation Runge-Kutta method}
{c}
{wiki=Gauss–Legendre_method}

= Gauss--Legendre Runge-Kutta method
{c}
{synonym}

= Gauss collocation method
{c}
{synonym}

The $s$-stage Gauss--Legendre Runge--Kutta method collocates at the Gauss--Legendre quadrature nodes on $[0,1]$. It is symmetric, A-stable, and has order $2s$.

= Gauss collocation coefficient construction
{parent=Gauss-Legendre method}
{c}
{title2=$a_{ij}=\int_0^{c_i}\ell_j(t)dt$}

Choose $c_1,\ldots,c_s$ as the <polynomial roots> of $P_s(2c-1)$ in $(0,1)$, and form the <Lagrange interpolation polynomials> $\ell_j(t)=\prod_{m\ne j}(t-c_m)/(c_j-c_m)$. The <Gauss collocation method> has $a_{ij}=\int_0^{c_i}\ell_j(t)dt$ and $b_j=\int_0^1\ell_j(t)dt$. This gives explicit coefficient formulas after finding <polynomial roots>: if $\ell_j(t)=\sum_rd_{jr}t^r$, these <integrals> are $\sum_rd_{jr}c_i^{r+1}/(r+1)$ and $\sum_rd_{jr}/(r+1)$. <Gaussian quadrature> gives order $2s$.

= Fourth-order two-stage Gauss collocation method
{parent=Gauss-Legendre method}
{title2=$R(z)=(1+z/2+z^2/12)/(1-z/2+z^2/12)$}

The two-stage <Gauss--Legendre Runge-Kutta method> uses nodes $c_{1,2}=1/2\mp\sqrt3/6$, weights $b_1=b_2=1/2$ and coefficients $a_{11}=a_{22}=1/4$, $a_{12}=1/4-\sqrt3/6$, $a_{21}=1/4+\sqrt3/6$. The <Butcher order conditions> give order four. Its <stability function> is the displayed rational function, which is <A-stable>. The <algebraic stability> matrix is zero, so positive weights also give nonlinear contractivity for dissipative vector fields.

= Radau IIA method
{parent=Collocation Runge-Kutta method}
{c}

An $s$-stage Radau IIA method collocates at the right-endpoint Radau nodes. It has order $2s-1$ and is both <algebraically stable> and <L-stable>.

= Padé identification of a Radau stability function
{c}
{parent=Radau IIA method}
{title2=$R(z)=[s-1/s]_{e^z}$}

Suppose an $s$-stage <Runge-Kutta method> has order $2s-1$, an <invertible matrix> $A$, and $b^TA^{-1}\mathbf1=1$. Its <stability function> is $R(z)=1+zb^T(I-zA)^{-1}\mathbf1$. The <adjugate matrix> formula gives a <rational function> with denominator degree $s$ and numerator degree at most $s$; its zero <limit> at infinity lowers the latter degree to at most $s-1$. The <order of a numerical method> gives $R(z)-e^z=O(z^{2s})$, identifying the $[s-1/s]$ <Padé approximant>. Uniqueness follows because the cross-multiplied difference of two candidates has degree at most $2s-1$ but a zero of order at least $2s$, so is identically zero. The <A-stability of near-diagonal exponential Padé approximants> then proves <A-stability>, and the zero limit proves <L-stability>. These conditions identify the stability function; they do not alone claim uniqueness of all entries of the <Runge-Kutta method> tableau.

= Algebraic stability of a Runge-Kutta method
{parent=Collocation Runge-Kutta method}

= Algebraic stability
{synonym}

= Algebraically stable
{synonym}

A Runge-Kutta method is algebraically stable when $b_i\geq0$ and the matrix $M$ with $M_{ij}=b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}-b_i b_j$ is positive semidefinite. Algebraic stability implies contractivity for dissipative differential equations.

= Runge-Kutta conservation of quadratic invariants
{parent=Algebraic stability of a Runge-Kutta method}
{c}
{title2=$b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}=b_i b_j$}

For stage states $Y_i=y_n+h\sum_ja_{ij}k_j$ and a vector field with $Y_i^Tk_i=0$, expansion of the new squared norm gives $\|y_{n+1}\|^2-\|y_n\|^2=-h^2\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}k_i^Tk_j$, where $M_{ij}=b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}-b_i b_j$. Hence $M=0$ preserves the <quadratic form> exactly for every well-defined step. The same argument with a fixed symmetric matrix in the inner product preserves any quadratic invariant of the vector field. In particular it preserves the Euclidean norm for a <skew-symmetric matrix> differential equation.

= Butcher contractivity theorem
{c}
{parent=Algebraic stability of a Runge-Kutta method}

An <algebraically stable> <Runge-Kutta method> is <B-stable>: for a <dissipative vector field>, a well-defined step cannot increase the distance between two starting values. This assumes that the compared stage equations have solutions, as required by <stage solvability of an implicit Runge-Kutta method>. The <Runge-Kutta contractivity identity> expresses the distance change as a nonpositive weighted term coming from the <dissipative vector field> minus a nonnegative <quadratic form> involving the matrix defining <algebraic stability>. Positive weights also rule out stage poles in the open left half-plane on the scalar test equation, giving <A-stability> there; zero-weight redundant stages need separate solvability attention.

= Runge-Kutta contractivity identity
{parent=Algebraic stability of a Runge-Kutta method}
{c}

For differences $D_i$ between corresponding stages and differences $F_i$ between their vector fields, a Runge--Kutta step satisfies
$$
\|d_{n+1}\|^2=\|d_n\|^2+2h\sum_i b_i\operatorname{Re}\langle D_i,F_i\rangle-h^2\sum_{i,j}m_{ij}\operatorname{Re}\langle F_i,F_j\rangle.
$$
To derive the identity, expand the squared <norm> of $d_{n+1}=d_n+h\sum_i b_iF_i$ and substitute $d_n=D_i-h\sum_j a_{ij}F_j$ in the linear term. Symmetrizing the double sum gives the displayed coefficients $m_{ij}$. For a <dissipative vector field>, the first sum is nonpositive when $b_i\geq0$, and the second sum is nonnegative when the <matrix> $(m_{ij})$ is a <positive semidefinite matrix>. Thus <algebraic stability> implies <B-stability>.

= Trapezoidal rule
{parent=Implicit Runge-Kutta method}
{wiki}
{title2=$T(f)=(b-a)(f(a)+f(b))/2$}

As a <quadrature rule>, the <trapezoidal rule> replaces an <integrand> by the <linear interpolation> joining its two endpoint values:
$$
I(f)=\int_a^bf(x)\,dx\approx T(f)=\frac{b-a}{2}\bigl(f(a)+f(b)\bigr),\qquad a<b.
$$
For nonnegative endpoint values, this is the area of a <trapezoid>; in general it is a signed area. The <quadrature nodes> are $a,b$, each with <quadrature weight> $(b-a)/2$. Unlike the <midpoint quadrature rule>, it uses endpoint samples, which can be reused between adjacent panels.

Every <affine function> $f(x)=\alpha x+\beta$ agrees with its <linear interpolation>, so the <trapezoidal rule> integrates it exactly. On $[0,2]$, $f(x)=2x+1$ gives $T(f)=(2/2)(1+5)=6=I(f)$. On $[0,1]$, the <polynomial> $f(x)=x^2$ instead gives $T(f)=1/2$, exceeding $I(f)=1/3$ by $1/6$. Its <degree of precision of a quadrature rule> is exactly one.

If $f\in C^2[a,b]$, its signed <approximation error> satisfies
$$
T(f)-I(f)=\frac{(b-a)^3}{12}f''(\xi_T),\qquad a<\xi_T<b.
$$
A <convex function> lies below its endpoint chord, so the <trapezoidal rule> overestimates its <integral>; a <concave function> gives the reverse inequality. The error formula assumes that the second <derivative> exists and is a <continuous function> throughout the closed interval.<NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions/3.5 Quadrature, subsection (i) Trapezoidal Rules>[§3.5(i), equation 3.5.1 and its C² hypothesis; the source writes integral minus rule]{ref} A short derivation is the identity
$$
T(f)-I(f)=\frac12\int_a^b(t-a)(b-t)f''(t)\,dt,
$$
obtained by two <integrations by parts>. The nonnegative <Peano kernel> has <integral> $(b-a)^3/12$, and the <weighted mean value theorem for integrals> gives the formula.

The <trapezoidal rule> for an <ordinary differential equation> averages <vector fields> at the old and new states and is <A-stable>.

This time-stepping use applies the same <trapezoidal rule> to the <integral> of the <derivative> in an <ordinary differential equation>. That connection explains its place under <implicit Runge-Kutta methods>; its quadrature behavior and the nonlinear issue in <trapezoidal rule fails B-stability> concern different uses of the same endpoint averaging.

= Composite trapezoidal rule
{parent=Trapezoidal rule}
{title2=$T_n(f)=h(f(a)/2+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}f(a+jh)+f(b)/2)$}

On an equal-panel <partition of an interval>, with $h=(b-a)/n$, sum the <trapezoidal rule> over adjacent panels:
$$
T_n(f)=h\left[\frac{f(a)}2+\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}f(a+jh)+\frac{f(b)}2\right].
$$
There are $n+1$ distinct <quadrature nodes>. Each interior sample belongs to two panels and therefore has <quadrature weight> $h$, while each endpoint has <quadrature weight> $h/2$. Equivalently, the <composite trapezoidal rule> integrates the <piecewise linear function> obtained by <linear interpolation> exactly.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others/5.6 Numerical integration>[§5.6.1, equations 5.6.3–5.6.5: integrating the piecewise linear interpolant]{ref}

For $f\in C^2[a,b]$ and $K=\max_{x\in[a,b]}|f''(x)|$, summing panel <quadrature errors> gives
$$
T_n(f)-I(f)=\frac{b-a}{12}h^2f''(\xi),\qquad |T_n(f)-I(f)|\leq\frac{b-a}{12}h^2K
$$
for some $\xi\in(a,b)$. Thus panel <quadrature errors> of order $O(h^3)$ accumulate to order $O(h^2)$, using <big O notation>.<NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions/3.5 Quadrature, subsection (i) Trapezoidal Rules>[§3.5(i), equations 3.5.2–3.5.3: composite formula and signed error]{ref} On a nonuniform <partition of an interval> with widths $h_j$ and largest width $H$, the bound is $K\sum_jh_j^3/12\leq K(b-a)H^2/12$.

For the <polynomial> $f(x)=x^2$ on $[0,1]$, direct summation yields
$$
T_n(f)=\frac13+\frac{1}{6n^2},\qquad T_n(f)-I(f)=-2\bigl(M_n(f)-I(f)\bigr).
$$
Both <quadrature errors> shrink by exactly four when $n$ doubles, with opposite signs. This exact relation belongs to this <polynomial> example; the <trapezoidal rule> is not always twice as inaccurate as the <midpoint quadrature rule>.

\Image[midpoint-trapezoidal-error-refinement.png]
{title=Quadratic quadrature errors fall by four on refinement while a common sample bias persists}

The left plot uses exact <polynomial> sums; the right adds a common sample bias $\delta=0.002$ to the <composite trapezoidal rule>. That mathematical model converges to $1/3+\delta$, showing why smaller panels alone do not ensure accuracy for noisy samples. It illustrates <noise sensitivity of a quadrature rule>, rather than measured data.

= Trapezoidal rule fails B-stability
{parent=Trapezoidal rule}

The <trapezoidal rule> is <A-stable> but is not <B-stable>. For the <dissipative vector field> $f(y)=-y^3$ at step size one, its uniquely defined step map $T$ obeys $T+T^3/2=y-y^3/2$. At $y=\sqrt2$, $T=0$ and implicit differentiation gives $T'=-2$, so nearby states expand rather than contract. This is a concrete nonlinear obstruction, rather than merely failure of the sufficient <algebraic stability> criterion.

= Time-symmetric numerical method
{parent=Runge-Kutta method}

= Symmetric numerical method
{synonym}

A one-step numerical method with step map $\Phi_h$ is time symmetric when $\Phi_{-h}=\Phi_h^{-1}$. Its leading local-error power is odd, so every nontrivial consistent time-symmetric method has even order.

= Order of a Runge-Kutta method
{parent=Runge-Kutta method}
{wiki}

The order is the highest power through which the one-step expansion matches the exact Taylor expansion.

= Third-order conditions for an explicit Runge-Kutta method
{parent=Order of a Runge-Kutta method}

For an explicit <Runge-Kutta method> with coefficients $A,b$, put $c=A\mathbf1$ and let $c^2$ mean componentwise squaring. The conditions $b^T\mathbf1=1$, $b^Tc=1/2$, $b^Tc^2=1/3$ and $b^TAc=1/6$ match the exact flow expansion through degree three: the two cubic differentials are $f^{\prime\prime}[f,f]$ and $(f^\prime)^2f$. For sufficiently smooth vector fields these give local error $O(h^4)$ and, under the usual finite-interval <Lipschitz continuity> assumptions, global error $O(h^3)$.

= Simplex algorithm
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

= Dual simplex algorithm
{parent=Simplex algorithm}

The dual simplex algorithm keeps the objective row dual feasible while repairing negative basic values. In a maximization dictionary $x_B=b+\sum_j a_jx_j$ with $b<0$ and nonbasic objective coefficients $d_j\leq0$, eligible entering columns have $a_j>0$; choose one minimizing $(-d_j)/a_j$. The pivot restores progress toward primal feasibility without losing the objective bound.

= Simplex dictionary
{parent=Simplex algorithm}

A simplex dictionary expresses the basic variables and the objective as affine functions of the nonbasic variables in a <linear program>. Setting the nonbasic variables to zero gives a <basic feasible solution> when all basic values are nonnegative. A <simplex algorithm> pivot exchanges one basic and one nonbasic variable. For maximization, a feasible dictionary with all nonbasic objective coefficients nonpositive certifies optimality.

= Sparse optimization
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

= Compressed sensing
{parent=Sparse optimization}
{wiki}

Compressed sensing reconstructs a <sparse vector>, or an approximately sparse one, from fewer linear measurements than its ambient dimension. <Basis pursuit> replaces counting nonzero coordinates by <convex> $\ell^1$ minimization. A <null space property> characterizes exact uniform recovery, while a <robust null space property> controls errors from noise and nonsparse tails.

= Coherence of a normalized matrix
{parent=Compressed sensing}
{title2=$\mu(A)=\max_{i\ne j}|\langle a_i,a_j\rangle|$}

= Mutual coherence
{synonym}

For a <matrix> with at least two columns of unit <Euclidean norm>, its mutual coherence is the largest magnitude of an <inner product> between distinct columns. It measures the strongest pairwise ambiguity of linear measurements. The <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> gives $0\le\mu\le1$. The order-two <restricted isometry constant> equals the <mutual coherence>, because every two-column <Gram matrix> has eigenvalues $1\pm|\langle a_i,a_j\rangle|$.

= Cumulative coherence
{parent=Coherence of a normalized matrix}
{title2=$\mu_1(k)=\max_i\max_{|S|=k,\ i\notin S}\sum_{j\in S}|\langle a_i,a_j\rangle|$}

= L1 coherence
{c}
{synonym}

For unit <Euclidean norm> columns, cumulative coherence measures the largest sum of $k$ correlations with one other column. Its domain is $0\le k\le N-1$, with $\mu_1(0)=0$ by the empty-sum convention. It is nondecreasing, $\mu_1(1)=\mu$ for $N\ge2$, and $\mu_1(k)\le k\mu$, where $\mu$ is the <mutual coherence>. Keeping a sum of actual correlations can be substantially sharper than replacing each summand by the largest one.

= Cumulative coherence bound for restricted isometry
{parent=Cumulative coherence}
{title2=$\delta_s\le\mu_1(s-1)\le(s-1)\mu$}

For normalized columns and $1\le s\le N$, the <restricted isometry constant> is bounded by the <cumulative coherence> at $s-1$. A restricted <Gram matrix> has diagonal one and every off-diagonal row sum at most $\mu_1(s-1)$. The <Gershgorin circle theorem> and the <finite-dimensional spectral theorem> bound its <eigenvalues> between $1-\mu_1(s-1)$ and $1+\mu_1(s-1)$. At $s=1$ the distortion is zero, and at $s=2$ it is exactly the <mutual coherence>.

= Sparse injectivity
{parent=Compressed sensing}

A <linear map> is injective on the class of <sparse vectors> of order $s$ exactly when its <null space> contains no nonzero <vector> with at most $2s$ nonzero entries. Differences of two sparse <vectors> have at most $2s$ active coordinates. Conversely, splitting the <support of a vector> of a $2s$-sparse null <vector> into two parts constructs two distinct $s$-sparse <vectors> with the same image. The strict <null space property> implies sparse injectivity by applying it to both parts of such a split.

= Restricted isometry property
{parent=Compressed sensing}
{wiki}

The restricted isometry property controls $\|Ax\|_2^2/\|x\|_2^2$ uniformly over <sparse vectors>. It supplies quantitative near-orthogonality of disjoint sparse coordinate combinations, not merely normalization of individual columns.

= Restricted isometry constant
{parent=Restricted isometry property}
{title2=$\delta_s(A)$}

The order-s restricted isometry constant is the smallest nonnegative $\delta$ satisfying $(1-\delta)\|x\|_2^2\le\|Ax\|_2^2\le(1+\delta)\|x\|_2^2$ for every vector with at most $s$ nonzero coordinates. Equivalently it is $\max_{|S|\le s}\|A_S^*A_S-I\|_{2\to2}$. The sparse-vector quantifier is essential, and the constant can equal zero.

= Sharp order-s restricted-isometry recovery theorem
{parent=Restricted isometry constant}

For $s\ge2$ and $\delta=\delta_s(A)<1/3$, noisy <basis pursuit> has error $\|\hat x-x_0\|_2\le C_1\eta+C_2\sigma_s(x_0)/\sqrt s$, with constants depending only on $\delta$. One admissible pair is
$$
C_1=\frac{2\sqrt{2(1+\delta)}}{1-3\delta},\qquad
C_2=\frac{2\sqrt2(2\delta+\sqrt{(1-3\delta)\delta})+2(1-3\delta)}{1-3\delta}.
$$
These constants follow from Theorem 3.3 of https://arxiv.org/pdf/1302.1236[the sharp restricted-isometry recovery analysis], with the actual noise bounded by the tolerance $\eta$. The restriction $s\ge2$ is necessary: equal unit columns give $\delta_1=0$ without unique recovery. The coefficient of the approximation term cannot universally be one.

= Basis pursuit
{parent=Compressed sensing}
{wiki}
{title2=$\min\{\|x\|_1:Ax=y\}$}

Basis pursuit minimizes an $\ell^1$ <norm> subject to exact linear measurements. Basis pursuit with a noise tolerance replaces the equality by $\|Ax-y\|_2\le\eta$. A unique minimizer has linearly independent active columns, and hence at most $\operatorname{rank}A$ nonzero coordinates: a null direction on its support would make the objective locally affine on a feasible line, contradicting minimality or uniqueness.

= Strict dual certificate for basis pursuit
{parent=Basis pursuit}
{title2=$A_S^*h=\operatorname{sgn}(x_S),\quad\|A_{S^c}^*h\|_\infty<1$}

For a real <vector> $x$ with <support of a vector> $S$, this certificate is a <vector> $h$ whose measurements under the <adjoint operator> match the active <sign function> values and lie strictly between minus one and one on the inactive coordinates. If $A_S$ is <injective>, such a certificate proves that $x$ is the unique <basis pursuit> <minimizer>. For a nonzero $v\in\ker A$, <injectivity> ensures $v_{S^c}\ne0$, and $0=\langle A^*h,v\rangle$ implies the <fixed-sign null space condition>. The strict inequality is interpreted coordinatewise if $S^c$ is empty. The condition with the <sign function> written here is for real variables; complex <basis pursuit> uses the unit phases of active coordinates instead.

= Least-norm dual certificate
{parent=Strict dual certificate for basis pursuit}
{title2=$h_0=A_S(A_S^*A_S)^{-1}\operatorname{sgn}(x_S)$}

When $A_S$ is <injective>, this <vector> solves $A_S^*h_0=\operatorname{sgn}(x_S)$. Any other solution differs by a <vector> in $\ker A_S^*$, orthogonal to the range of $A_S$ containing $h_0$. The <Pythagorean identity> therefore proves that $h_0$ has the smallest <Euclidean norm>. It is a <strict dual certificate for basis pursuit> exactly when each inactive coordinate of $A^*h_0$ has magnitude less than one. Failure of that test does not rule out another certificate: a <null space> component of $A_S^*$ can alter those inactive coordinates.

= Nullspace property
{parent=Basis pursuit}
{wiki}

= Null space property
{synonym}

The null space property relative to $S$ is $\|h_S\|_1<\|h_{S^c}\|_1$ for every nonzero $h\in\ker A$. It is equivalent to exact <basis pursuit> recovery of every vector supported in $S$. Sufficiency follows from the <triangle inequality>; for necessity, compare $-h_S$ and $h_{S^c}$, which have the same image under $A$. Recovery of a single fixed signed vector can hold without this uniform property.

= Lq null space property
{c}
{parent=Nullspace property}
{title2=$\sum_{i\in S}|v_i|^q<\sum_{i\notin S}|v_i|^q$}

For $0<q\le1$, this strict inequality for every nonzero $v\in\ker A$ and every $|S|\le s$ is equivalent to unique constrained $\ell^q$ recovery of every <sparse vector> of order $s$. For sufficiency, use $|x_i+v_i|^q\ge|x_i|^q-|v_i|^q$ on the <support of a vector>. For necessity compare $x=-v_S$ with $v_{S^c}$, which have the same measurements. The statement concerns uniform recovery over the whole sparse class, rather than a single signed <vector>.

= Monotonicity of uniform sparse recovery in the exponent
{parent=Lq null space property}

If the <Lq null space property> holds at $q\le1$, it holds at every $0<p<q$. Order a nonzero null <vector>'s magnitudes $a_1\ge\cdots\ge a_N$ and put $t=a_s>0$. Since $p-q<0$, the top-$s$ $p$-power sum is at most $t^{p-q}$ times its $q$-power sum, while the tail $p$-power sum is at least that multiple of the tail $q$-power sum. The strict $q$ inequality therefore gives the strict $p$ inequality. The largest $s$ magnitudes are the worst <support of a vector>, so all other supports satisfy it too.

= Robust null space property
{parent=Nullspace property}
{title2=$\|h_S\|_2\le\rho\|h_{S^c}\|_1/\sqrt s+\tau\|Ah\|_2$}

The displayed property holds for every $h$ and every $|S|\le s$, with $0\le\rho<1$ and $\tau>0$. It converts the cone inequality from <basis pursuit> minimality and the tube inequality from noisy feasibility into a two-constant bound $\|\hat x-x_0\|_2\le C_1\eta+C_2\sigma_s(x_0)/\sqrt s$. Both constants are needed in general; a fixed coefficient one on the approximation term does not follow.

= Fixed-sign null space condition
{parent=Nullspace property}

For a real vector $x_0$ with support $S$, uniqueness in <basis pursuit> is equivalent to $|\langle\operatorname{sgn}(x_{0,S}),h_S\rangle|<\|h_{S^c}\|_1$ for every nonzero $h\in\ker A$. The supporting-line inequality for the <absolute value> proves sufficiency. Taking small positive and negative multiples of $h$ before any active sign changes proves necessity.

= Sparse vector
{parent=Compressed sensing}
{title2=$|\operatorname{supp}x|\le s$}

A vector is s-sparse if it has at most $s$ nonzero coordinates. For $x\in\mathbb R^N$, its best s-term $\ell^1$ approximation error is $\sigma_s(x)=\min_{z\text{ s-sparse}}\|x-z\|_1$, equal to the sum of the absolute values of the coordinates outside the largest $s$.

= L0 sparsity count
{c}
{parent=Sparse vector}
{title2=$\|x\|_0=|\operatorname{supp}x|$}

The number of nonzero coordinates of a finite <vector> is its L0 sparsity count. It vanishes only at zero, but is unchanged under multiplication by a nonzero scalar, so it is not a <norm>. Minimizing it under linear measurement constraints finds a <sparse vector> with the smallest possible <support of a vector>. <Sparse injectivity> of order $s$ guarantees unique recovery of every <sparse vector> with at most $s$ nonzero coordinates by this objective.

= Support of a vector
{parent=Sparse vector}
{title2=$\operatorname{supp}x$}

= Supports of a vector
{synonym}

The support of a finite vector is the set of indices of its nonzero coordinates. It is the <support of a function> on a finite discrete index set, and its cardinality defines sparsity.

= Maximum-support vector in a finite-dimensional subspace
{parent=Support of a vector}

For any <field> $F$ and <vector subspace> $W\subseteq F^m$, some $u\in W$ has at least $\dim W$ nonzero coordinates. Choose $u$ with largest <support of a vector> $S$. Restriction from $W$ to $F^S$ is injective: a nonzero vector in its <kernel of a linear map> vanishes on $S$, so adding it to $u$ would enlarge $S$. Thus $\dim W\leq|S|$. The argument works over <finite fields>, where assuming a generic vector avoids finitely many hyperplanes would be invalid.

= Stability function
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

= Dahlquist test equation
{title2=$y'=\lambda y$}
{parent=Stability function}
{c}
{wiki}

The Dahlquist test equation is the scalar linear initial-value problem $y'=\lambda y$. A one-step method reduces it to $y_{n+1}=R(h\lambda)y_n$, thereby defining its <stability function> $R$.

= Linear multistep method
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

= Multistep method
{synonym}

A linear multistep method approximates an ODE using several previous solution and derivative values.

= Reciprocal-root three-step multistep family
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{title2=$\rho(\zeta)=(\zeta-1)(\zeta^2-2\alpha\zeta+1)$}

Take $\sigma(\zeta)=\zeta[(5+\alpha)\zeta^2-(4+8\alpha)\zeta+11-5\alpha]/6$. The <root condition for a multistep method> holds exactly for $-1<\alpha<1$. Since $\rho'(1)=\sigma(1)=2(1-\alpha)$, these and only these parameters give a convergent method. Its <exponential-symbol order criterion for a multistep method> has defect $-(\alpha+5)z^4/12+O(z^5)$, giving formal order three except at $\alpha=-5$, where the first defect is $z^5/10$. None of the convergent family is <A-stable>: for a parasitic root $r=\alpha+i\sqrt{1-\alpha^2}$, perturbation of $\rho(r(z))-z\sigma(r(z))=0$ gives $\operatorname{Re}[r'(0)/r]=(\alpha-1)/12<0$. A small negative real $z$ therefore moves it outside the unit disk. At $\alpha=1$, cancellation would remove a double unit root, but the original recurrence retains that unstable parasitic behavior; it is not legitimately replaced by the reduced Backward Euler recurrence for arbitrary starting values.

= Simpson multistep method
{c}
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{title2=$Y_{n+2}-Y_n=\frac h3(f_{n+2}+4f_{n+1}+f_n)$}

Integrating a quadratic interpolant of the derivative over two steps gives this <linear multistep method>, which has order four. Its <local truncation error> is $-h^5y^{(5)}(t_{n+1})/90+O(h^7)$ and its <zero-stability> polynomial is $\rho(\zeta)=\zeta^2-1$, with simple roots $1,-1$. The <Dahlquist equivalence theorem> therefore gives fourth-order convergence with sufficiently accurate starts. This attains the even two-step <first Dahlquist barrier>. Its undamped parasitic zero-step mode need not have favorable long-time <absolute stability>; finite-time convergence and stiff damping are separate requirements.

= First Dahlquist barrier
{parent=Linear multistep method}

For a consistent <zero-stable> real linear $k$-step method with constant coefficients and only first-derivative evaluations, the attainable order is at most $k+1$ for odd $k$ and $k+2$ for even $k$. For an explicit method the bound is $k$. These are order barriers under the <root condition for a multistep method>, not bounds on multiderivative methods or on arbitrary variable-step formulations. The <second Dahlquist barrier> adds the stronger bound of order two for irreducible <A-stable> methods.

= Newton-Cotes multistep methods attaining the first Dahlquist barrier
{c}
{parent=First Dahlquist barrier}
{title2=$Y_{n+s}-Y_n=h\sum_{j=0}^sw_jf_{n+j}$}

Using <Newton-Cotes closed quadrature> for the integrated derivative yields the displayed <linear multistep method>. Its first <characteristic polynomial> is $\rho(z)=z^s-1$, whose unit-circle roots are all simple, proving <zero-stability>. Quadrature exactness gives order $s+1$ for odd $s$ and at least $s+2$ for even $s$. The <first Dahlquist barrier> gives equality, while the <Dahlquist equivalence theorem> gives convergence with consistent starts. These are finite-time convergence statements, not claims of <A-stability>.

= Cayley coefficient proof of the first Dahlquist barrier
{c}
{parent=First Dahlquist barrier}
{title2=$p\leq s+1\ (s\text{ odd}),\quad p\leq s+2\ (s\text{ even})$}

For a real consistent <zero-stable> <linear multistep method>, transform its first <characteristic polynomial> by $z=(1+x)/(1-x)$ to $(1-x)^s\rho(z)=xP(x)$. The <root condition for a multistep method> puts all finite roots of $P$ in the closed left half-plane; real linear factors and conjugate quadratic factors consequently give nonnegative coefficients, after choosing $P(0)>0$. The order criterion requires a <polynomial> of degree at most $s$ to agree through degree $p-1$ with $P(x)x/(2\operatorname{arctanh}x)$. The latter quotient has strictly negative nonconstant even coefficients. Its first even coefficient beyond degree $s$ includes a strictly negative multiple of $P(0)$ and cannot vanish. This gives the stated bound.

= Coefficient sign lemma for the first Dahlquist barrier
{parent=Cayley coefficient proof of the first Dahlquist barrier}
{title2=$x/\operatorname{arctanh}x=1-\sum_{j\geq1}\gamma_jx^{2j},\quad\gamma_j>0$}

Write $F(x)=\int_0^1(1+x)^t(1-x)^{1-t}\,dt=x/\operatorname{arctanh}x$. Pairing $t$ and $1-t$ after differentiating twice gives
$$
F''(x)=-4\int_0^1t(1-t)(1-x^2)^{-3/2}\cosh[(2t-1)\operatorname{arctanh}x]\,dt.
$$
The <power series> of $(1-x^2)^{-3/2}$ has strictly positive even coefficients, and the other factor has nonnegative even coefficients. Thus every even coefficient of $F''$ is negative. Since $F$ is even with $F(0)=1$, the displayed sign pattern follows. This supplies a short positivity argument for the <first Dahlquist barrier>.

= Two-step family with a third-order member
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{title2=$\rho(w)=(w-1)(w-a),\quad\sigma(w)=\tfrac12(1+a)w^2+\tfrac12(1-3a)w$}

The <exponential-symbol order criterion for a multistep method> gives leading defect $-(1+5a)z^3/12$, so the family has order two except at $a=-1/5$, where it has order three. The <root condition for a multistep method> holds exactly for $-1\leq a<1$, and the convergent <A-stable> members are exactly $0\leq a<1$. At $a=0$ it reduces to the <trapezoidal rule> after removal of a zero root. At $a=1$ a repeated root one destroys <convergence of a numerical method> of the original recurrence despite a common <polynomial> factor and a formally vanishing second-order residual.

= Adams-Bashforth method
{c}
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{title2=$y_{n+1}=y_n+h(3f_n-f_{n-1})/2$}

An explicit <linear multistep method> that integrates a polynomial extrapolation of the past derivative values. The displayed two-step member is second order in time: the linear interpolant through $f_{n-1},f_n$, integrated over the next step, gives coefficients $3/2,-1/2$. For a spatially discretized partial differential equation, the spatial error must also be included in the total local defect.

= Adams-Bashforth stability for centered diffusion
{c}
{parent=Adams-Bashforth method}
{title2=$0\leq\Delta t/(\Delta x)^2\leq1/4$}

For centered-space diffusion, a <Fourier mode> gives $z^2-(1-3a/2)z-a/2=0$, with $a=4\mu\sin^2(\theta/2)$. For $a>0$ the roots have opposite signs, the positive root lies in $(0,1)$, and the negative root lies in $[-1,0)$ exactly when $P(-1)=2-2a\geq0$. At $a=0,1$ the unit-modulus roots are simple. Hence the <root condition for a multistep method> holds for every Fourier frequency exactly when $\mu\leq1/4$. Under fixed $\mu$, the unscaled one-step defect is $-\Delta t^2u_{xxxx}/(12\mu)+O(\Delta t^3)$; dividing by the step changes its order to $O(\Delta t)$.

= Common-factor cancellation defect in a multistep recurrence
{parent=Linear multistep method}

Canceling a common factor of the characteristic polynomials changes the allowed starting relations and can remove a parasitic mode rather than prove stability of the original recurrence. For example $\rho=(\zeta-1)^2$ and $\sigma=(\zeta^2-1)/2$ fail <zero-stability>. On $y'=0$, the original recurrence admits $y_n=y_0+n(y_1-y_0)$; starting perturbations $y_1-y_0=\sqrt h$ vanish but give divergent error at fixed positive time. The canceled one-step recurrence excludes these data, so its stability cannot be assigned to the original scheme.

= Trapezoidal-BDF two-step family
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{title2=$\rho(\zeta)=\zeta^2-\alpha\zeta-(1-\alpha),\quad\sigma(\zeta)=(1-\alpha/4)\zeta^2+1-3\alpha/4$}

This real-parameter <linear multistep method> has formal order two except at the degenerate value $\alpha=2$, where its local symbol vanishes to fourth degree. The zero-stability roots are $1$ and $\alpha-1$, so it is convergent exactly for $0\leq\alpha<2$. Every convergent member is <A-stable>: the <boundary-locus test for multistep A-stability> gives nonnegative real part $\alpha(2-\alpha)(1-\cos\theta)^2/(2|\sigma(e^{i\theta})|^2)$, with no unit-circle denominator zero for $0<\alpha<2$. At $\alpha=0$ the two interlaced sequences obey a trapezoidal step of length $2h$. At $\alpha=4/3$ it is the <BDF2 method>. The endpoint two fails the <root condition for a multistep method> even though canceling a common polynomial factor resembles the <trapezoidal rule>.

= Multiderivative multistep method
{parent=Linear multistep method}

A multiderivative multistep method uses higher time derivatives as well as values and first derivatives from several time levels. For an autonomous <ordinary differential equation> $y'=f(y)$, the second time derivative is $y''=f'(y)f(y)$ by the <chain rule>. Its <local truncation error> must include the higher-derivative terms. Consequently the usual second-order barrier for <A-stable> <linear multistep methods>, which only use first derivatives, does not apply.

= Symmetric sixth-order two-derivative method
{parent=Multiderivative multistep method}
{title2=$y_{n+1}-\frac7{15}hf_{n+1}+\frac1{15}h^2g_{n+1}=y_{n-1}+\frac h{15}(16f_n+7f_{n-1})+\frac1{15}h^2g_{n-1}$}

For $y'=f(y)$ and $g=f'f$, inserting a smooth exact solution into the displayed recurrence gives defect $h^7y^{(7)}(t_n)/4725+O(h^9)$. This follows by expanding $2\sinh z-(14/15)z\cosh z-(16/15)z+(2/15)z^2\sinh z$. The terms through $z^6$ vanish and the $z^7$ coefficient is nonzero. Its zero-step polynomial is $\zeta^2-1$, whose roots are simple and of unit modulus. Thus it is <zero-stable> and has order six, provided starting values and derivative evaluations have corresponding accuracy.

= Symmetric two-step two-derivative formula
{parent=Multiderivative multistep method}
{title2=$\alpha=7/15,\quad\beta=1/15,\quad\gamma=16/15$}

For the <multiderivative multistep method> $y_{n+1}-\alpha h f_{n+1}+\beta h^2g_{n+1}=\gamma h f_n+y_{n-1}+\alpha h f_{n-1}+\beta h^2g_{n-1}$, with $g=f'f$, centered expansion gives defect coefficients $2-2\alpha-\gamma$, $1/3-\alpha+2\beta$, and $1/60-\alpha/12+\beta/3$ at powers $h,h^3,h^5$. Their simultaneous vanishing gives the displayed parameters. The next coefficient is $1/4725$ at $h^7$, so the formal order is six. Reflection symmetry removes every even defect power. The method satisfies fifth-order conditions but cannot have exact order five.

= Bounded-root stability set of the symmetric two-derivative formula
{parent=Symmetric two-step two-derivative formula}
{title2=$z=iy,\quad y^4-45y^2+225>0$}

For the <symmetric two-step two-derivative formula>, the closed-disk <root condition for a multistep method> fails off the imaginary axis. Positive real part makes the product of root moduli exceed one; negative real part fails the <complex quadratic Schur criterion>, as in the <empty strict decay domain of the symmetric two-derivative formula> proof. On $z=iy$, write $D=15-y^2-7iy=|D|e^{i\delta}$ and set $\xi=i e^{-i\delta}\eta$. The <amplification polynomial> becomes $\eta^2-16y\eta/|D|+1=0$. Its two roots are distinct and on the <unit circle> exactly when $|16y/|D||<2$, or $y^4-45y^2+225>0$. Thus the bounded-root set consists of the imaginary intervals $|y|<\sqrt{(45-15\sqrt5)/2}$ and $|y|>\sqrt{(45+15\sqrt5)/2}$, including $y=0$ and excluding the double-root endpoints. In particular, the set is unbounded. Unit-modulus oscillations are bounded but do not tend to zero.

= Empty strict decay domain of the symmetric two-derivative formula
{parent=Symmetric two-step two-derivative formula}
{title2=$\mathcal D_{\rm decay}=\varnothing$}

On the <Dahlquist test equation>, write the <amplification polynomial> as $D\xi^2-16z\xi-A$, where $D=z^2-7z+15$ and $A=z^2+7z+15$. The identity $|A|^2-|D|^2=28\operatorname{Re}z(|z|^2+15)$ shows that both <roots of a polynomial> can lie inside the open <unit disk> only if $\operatorname{Re}z<0$. But the <complex quadratic Schur criterion> would then require $32|\operatorname{Re}z|(|z|^2+15)<28|\operatorname{Re}z|(|z|^2+15)$, which is impossible. Thus the <strict linear stability domain> is empty. This concerns robust damping of all modes, rather than convergence on fixed time intervals or the <bounded-root stability set of the symmetric two-derivative formula>.

= Negative-real instability of the symmetric two-derivative formula
{parent=Symmetric two-step two-derivative formula}
{title2=$F_z(-1)=2z<0$}

On the <Dahlquist test equation>, the <symmetric two-step two-derivative formula> has amplification polynomial $F_z(w)=(z^2-7z+15)w^2-16zw-(z^2+7z+15)$. For every real $z<0$, its leading coefficient is positive and $F_z(-1)=2z<0$, whereas $F_z(w)\to+\infty$ as $w\to-\infty$. The <intermediate value theorem> therefore gives a real root below minus one. Thus the method is not <A-stable>, without any appeal to a multistep order barrier.

= A-stable third-order two-step multiderivative method
{parent=Multiderivative multistep method}
{c}
{title2=$7y_{n+2}-6hy'_{n+2}+2h^2y''_{n+2}=8y_{n+1}-y_n$}

The endpoint expansion of this <multiderivative multistep method> has unscaled defect $h^4y^{(4)}/3+O(h^5)$ and <zero-stability> roots $1,1/7$, giving third-order convergence with suitably accurate starting values. On the <Dahlquist test equation> its amplification roots obey $(7-6z+2z^2)\zeta^2-8\zeta+1=0$. The <complex quadratic Schur criterion> puts both roots in the open unit disk for $\operatorname{Re}z<0$, and only the simple root one reaches the boundary at $z=0$. Thus it is <A-stable>; its higher derivative places it outside the hypotheses of the <second Dahlquist barrier>.

= Convergence of a zero-stable multiderivative method
{parent=Multiderivative multistep method}

For a fixed-step <multiderivative multistep method>, the ordinary <zero-stability> root condition still controls propagation of the starting errors when all derivative evaluation maps are uniformly <Lipschitz continuous> on the relevant bounded region. A local defect $O(h^{p+1})$ then yields global error $O(h^p)$ over a fixed time interval, provided the starting errors are $O(h^p)$ and the implicit updates use the nearby solution branch.

For example, writing the numerical error equation as $\rho(E)e_n=hF_h(e_{n+s})+d_n$, with $F_h$ uniformly <Lipschitz continuous>, a bounded impulse response for the <root condition for a multistep method> gives
$$
\max_{j\leq n}\|e_j\|
\leq C\left(\max_{j<s}\|e_j\|+\sum_{j<n}\|d_j\|\right)
+Ch\sum_{j\leq n}\max_{i\leq j}\|e_i\|.
$$
Absorbing the last current-step term for small $h$ and applying the <discrete Gronwall inequality> gives the stated order. The higher derivatives enter through $F_h$, whose bound stays uniform as $h\to0$.

= Zero-stability
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{wiki}

= Zero-stable
{synonym}

Zero-stability requires the roots of the first characteristic polynomial to lie in the closed unit disc, with unit-modulus roots simple.

= Root condition for a multistep method
{parent=Zero-stability}

The root condition is the polynomial criterion equivalent to zero-stability for a linear multistep method.

= Parasitic amplification root
{parent=Root condition for a multistep method}

An amplification root of a <multistep method> is parasitic when it represents an additional numerical mode rather than the principal mode approximating the exact differential-equation flow. For a consistent method the principal root starts at one as the step tends to zero, while other roots can start elsewhere. A simple parasitic root on the unit circle is compatible with <zero-stability>, but its motion outside the circle for a negative test parameter can destroy <absolute stability>. Fixed-time convergence and all-time damping must be distinguished.

= Centered two-step discretization of exponential decay
{parent=Parasitic amplification root}
{title2=$\rho_\pm=-Kh\pm\sqrt{1+K^2h^2}$}

Replacing the derivative in $x'=-Kx$ by a <central finite difference> gives $x_{n+1}+2Khx_n-x_{n-1}=0$. Its principal root is $\rho_+=e^{-\operatorname{arsinh}(Kh)}$, while the <parasitic amplification root> is $\rho_-=-e^{\operatorname{arsinh}(Kh)}$. On a fixed time interval, $A_h\rho_+^n+B_h\rho_-^n$ converges uniformly to $Ce^{-Kt}$ exactly when $A_h\to C$ and $B_h\to0$. A fixed nonzero parasitic amplitude alternates between two incompatible limits, but a vanishing starting error can converge despite the root's modulus exceeding one. This distinguishes fixed-time <numerical convergence> from long-time <absolute stability>.

= Root stability of a cubic multistep polynomial
{parent=Root condition for a multistep method}
{title2=$\rho(w)=(w-1)(w^2+w+a)$}

The polynomial $\rho(w)=(w-1)(w^2+w+a)$ satisfies the <root condition for a multistep method> exactly for $0\leq a\leq1$. For $a<0$ a root is below $-1$; for $a>1$ the quadratic root product forces a root outside the unit disc. The endpoints have simple unit-modulus roots, and the repeated root at $a=1/4$ is strictly inside. A consistent method with this polynomial is convergent throughout the closed interval.

= Dahlquist equivalence theorem
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{c}
{wiki}

A consistent linear multistep method is convergent exactly when it is zero-stable.

= Necessity of the root condition for multistep convergence
{parent=Dahlquist equivalence theorem}

For a <linear multistep method>, convergence for every set of consistent starting values requires the <root condition for a multistep method>. Apply the recurrence to $y'=0$ over $N$ steps. A root $\xi$ with $|\xi|>1$ gives the error mode $e_n=|\xi|^{-N}\xi^n$: its fixed-index starting errors vanish as $N\to\infty$, but $|e_N|=1$. A unit-modulus root of multiplicity $m\geq2$ instead gives $e_n=N^{-(m-1)}n^{m-1}\xi^n$, with the same failure. Repeated roots inside the unit disk are harmless because their polynomial growth is dominated by exponential decay.

= Characteristic polynomials of a linear multistep method
{title2=$\rho,\sigma$}
{parent=Linear multistep method}

For $\sum_{j=0}^s\alpha_jy_{n+j}=h\sum_{j=0}^s\beta_jf_{n+j}$, the characteristic polynomials are $\rho(\zeta)=\sum_j\alpha_j\zeta^j$ and $\sigma(\zeta)=\sum_j\beta_j\zeta^j$. Applied to $y'=\lambda y$, the amplification roots solve $\rho(\zeta)-h\lambda\sigma(\zeta)=0$.

= Amplification polynomial of a multistep method
{parent=Characteristic polynomials of a linear multistep method}
{title2=$P_z(w)=\rho(w)-z\sigma(w)$}

= Amplification polynomial
{synonym}

Applying a <linear multistep method> to the <Dahlquist test equation> with $z=h\lambda$ produces a constant-coefficient recurrence whose modes satisfy $P_z(w)=0$. <Absolute stability> requires the resulting <roots of a polynomial> to lie in the unit disk, with unit-modulus roots simple. Thus multistep stability depends on every recurrence mode, rather than a single one-step <stability function>.

= Amplification root
{parent=Amplification polynomial of a multistep method}
{title2=$\xi(z)$}

An amplification root is a <root of a polynomial> governing a geometric mode $y_n=\xi^n$ of a numerical recurrence on the <Dahlquist test equation>. Its <modulus> measures damping or growth per step. A repeated root produces additional modes such as $n\xi^n$; therefore a <unit-circle root> can be permitted by a bounded-root condition only when it is simple. The principal root approximates $e^z$ near zero, while additional roots can describe <parasitic amplification roots>.

= Exterior roots of the derivative polynomial bound multistep stability
{parent=Amplification polynomial of a multistep method}
{title2=$\sigma(w_*)=0$, $|w_*|>1$}

If the derivative <characteristic polynomial> $\sigma$ of a <linear multistep method> has a simple root outside the <unit disk> and keeps its degree in the limit, then its <absolute stability> region is bounded. Otherwise a stable sequence with $|z|\to\infty$ would give amplification polynomials $\sigma-\rho/z$ tending coefficientwise to $\sigma$. A root near the exterior root would persist by continuity, contradicting the <root condition for a multistep method>. This argument excludes stability at every sufficiently large complex $z$, not only along the negative real axis.

= Order conditions for a linear multistep method
{parent=Characteristic polynomials of a linear multistep method}

A linear multistep method has order $p$ exactly when
$$
\sum_j\alpha_jj^q=q\sum_j\beta_jj^{q-1}
$$
for $0\leq q\leq p$, with the right side interpreted as zero for $q=0$, and the identity first fails at $q=p+1$.

= Exponential-symbol order criterion for a multistep method
{parent=Order conditions for a linear multistep method}
{title2=$\rho(e^z)-z\sigma(e^z)=O(z^{p+1})$}

The <Taylor expansion> of the exact-solution residual for a <linear multistep method> has the same coefficient sequence as
$$
\rho(e^z)-z\sigma(e^z).
$$
Vanishing through degree $p$ is therefore equivalent to order at least $p$. Exact order $p$ additionally requires a nonzero coefficient at degree $p+1$. This symbol conveniently collects all polynomial consistency and order conditions.

= Adams–Moulton method
{c}
{parent=Linear multistep method}
{wiki}

An Adams–Moulton method is an implicit <linear multistep method> obtained by integrating an interpolation polynomial for the vector field that includes the new time level.

= Second Dahlquist barrier
{c}
{parent=Linear multistep method}

An irreducible A-stable linear multistep method has order at most two.

This is an order restriction for an <A-stable> <linear multistep method>.

= Numerical linear algebra
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

Numerical linear algebra develops stable finite algorithms for matrix computations.

= Matrix preconditioning
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}
{title2=$M^{-1/2}AM^{-1/2}$}

Matrix preconditioning transforms a system using an inexpensive auxiliary solve to improve its spectrum. For symmetric positive definite matrices, a positive definite $M$ produces $M^{-1/2}AM^{-1/2}$, preserving the symmetric structure needed by <conjugate gradient method>.

= Matrix preconditioner
{parent=Matrix preconditioning}
{title2=$M$}

A matrix preconditioner is an auxiliary invertible matrix whose inverse is cheap to apply and whose use improves the spectrum of a linear system. In symmetric positive definite problems, a positive definite $M$ preserves that structure under the symmetric transformation $M^{-1/2}AM^{-1/2}$.

= Power iteration
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$x_{k+1}=Ax_k/\|Ax_k\|_2$}

<Power iteration> finds a <dominant eigenvalue> and its <eigenvector> by repeatedly applying a <matrix> and rescaling the resulting <vector>. The essential idea is selective amplification: components along different <eigenvectors> grow at different rates, so one direction can eventually dominate. It is the computational form of the <power method> and is useful when applying a large <sparse matrix> to a <vector> is cheap but computing its whole <spectral decomposition> is expensive.

For a square <matrix> over the <real numbers> or <complex numbers> $A$, choose a <unit vector> $x_0$ and repeat
$$
y_{k+1}=Ax_k,\qquad x_{k+1}=\frac{y_{k+1}}{\|y_{k+1}\|_2}.
$$
Here $\|\cdot\|_2$ is the <Euclidean norm>. If $y_{k+1}=0$, stop before division: the current <vector> is an <eigenvector> for the <zero eigenvalue>, and this iteration cannot continue. Otherwise, in exact arithmetic,
$$
x_k=\frac{A^kx_0}{\|A^kx_0\|_2}.
$$
Repeated normalization keeps the stored <vectors> at manageable size without changing their directions; it does not guarantee that an individual multiplication cannot exceed the range of <floating-point arithmetic>. The <dominant eigenvalue> means largest <modulus>, which need not mean largest signed real value.<Templates for the Solution of Algebraic Eigenvalue Problems: A Practical Guide/Power Method>{ref}

= Exact two-dimensional power iteration
{parent=Power iteration}
{title2=$\tan\theta_k=3^{-k}$}

Consider the <symmetric matrix> and initial <unit vector>
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad x_0=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Its <orthonormal eigenbasis> consists of $u_1=(1,1)^T/\sqrt2$ and $u_2=(1,-1)^T/\sqrt2$, with <eigenvalues> $3$ and $1$. Since $x_0=(u_1+u_2)/\sqrt2$, direct multiplication gives
$$
A^kx_0=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}3^k+1\\3^k-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad x_k=\frac{u_1+3^{-k}u_2}{\sqrt{1+3^{-2k}}}.
$$
The first unnormalized <vectors> are $(1,0)^T$, $(2,1)^T$, $(5,4)^T$, and $(14,13)^T$. If $\theta_k$ is the acute <angle> from the line spanned by the dominant <eigenvector>, then $\tan\theta_k=3^{-k}$. Every application divides the unwanted-to-wanted component ratio by three: normalization changes lengths, while the <spectral gap> selects the direction.

\Image[power-iteration-symmetric-direction-error.png]
{title=Exact <power iteration> directions and errors for a <symmetric matrix> with <eigenvalues> 3 and 1}

The right panel compares the <power iteration direction error>, <Rayleigh quotient> error, and <eigenpair residual>. With $t_k=3^{-k}$, their exact values are
$$
\varepsilon_k=\frac{t_k}{\sqrt{1+t_k^2}},\qquad 3-\rho_k=\frac{2t_k^2}{1+t_k^2},\qquad \|r_k\|_2=\frac{2t_k}{1+t_k^2}.
$$
Thus a very accurate <Rayleigh quotient> can appear well before the <eigenvector> is comparably accurate. This is an example of <quadratic Rayleigh-quotient improvement for the power method>.

= Power iteration convergence for a diagonalizable matrix
{parent=Power iteration}
{title2=$|\lambda_1|>|\lambda_2|,\quad (V^{-1}x_0)_1\ne0$}

Suppose $A\in\mathbb C^{n\times n}$ is a <diagonalizable matrix>, $n\ge2$, with an <eigenbasis> $v_1,\ldots,v_n$ and <spectral decomposition>
$$
A=V\Lambda V^{-1},\qquad V=(v_1\ \cdots\ v_n),\qquad \Lambda=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n).
$$
Assume the <dominant eigenvalue> $\lambda_1$ is a <simple eigenvalue> and
$$
|\lambda_1|>|\lambda_2|\ge\cdots\ge|\lambda_n|,\qquad x_0=\sum_{j=1}^n c_jv_j,\qquad c_1\ne0.
$$
Then the normalized <power iteration> approaches the one-dimensional <eigenspace> of $\lambda_1$. Its asymptotic direction-error bound has factor $\gamma=|\lambda_2/\lambda_1|<1$. This <spectral gap> is a gap in <modulus>: if $g=|\lambda_1|-|\lambda_2|$, then $\gamma=1-g/|\lambda_1|$. A small relative gap gives slow convergence.<Templates for the Solution of Algebraic Eigenvalue Problems: A Practical Guide/Power Method>{ref}

The short proof is the exact <eigenbasis> identity
$$
\frac{A^kx_0}{c_1\lambda_1^k}=v_1+\sum_{j=2}^n\frac{c_j}{c_1}\left(\frac{\lambda_j}{\lambda_1}\right)^kv_j.
$$
The remaining sum has <norm> bounded by a constant times $\gamma^k$. This proves convergence of direction, allowing a changing sign or complex factor. A tiny $c_1$ or a poorly conditioned, <nonorthogonal> <eigenbasis> can delay visible convergence: the <condition number> of $V$ affects constants, while $\gamma$ controls the eventual rate. The bound need not be an equality; if the initial coefficient of the second-largest <eigenvalue> vanishes, the <active spectrum of the power method> gives a faster rate. When $\gamma=0$, all unwanted components disappear after one multiplication.<Numerical Analysis, Spring 2022/Power iteration (2022-03-07 lecture notes)>[§1, PDF pp. 1–2: diagonalizable expansion and nonzero first coordinate of the starting vector]{ref}

The hypothesis is $c_1=(V^{-1}x_0)_1\ne0$, not an ordinary nonzero <inner product> with the right <eigenvector>. Let $w_1^*$ be the first row of $V^{-1}$, a <left eigenvector> normalized so $w_1^*v_j$ is $1$ for $j=1$ and $0$ otherwise; $*$ denotes <conjugate transpose>. Then $c_1=w_1^*x_0$. For a <Hermitian matrix>, choose an <orthonormal eigenbasis> and the criterion becomes $v_1^*x_0\ne0$: the starting <vector> must be <nonorthogonal> to the dominant <eigenvector>. For a general <diagonalizable matrix>, the <nonorthogonal> <eigenbasis> makes these two tests different, as illustrated under <power iteration failure cases>.

= Power iteration direction error
{parent=Power iteration convergence for a diagonalizable matrix}
{title2=$\varepsilon_k=\|(I-uu^*)x_k\|_2$}

For a <unit vector> $u$ spanning the target <eigenspace> and a normalized iterate $x_k$, define
$$
\varepsilon_k=\|(I-uu^*)x_k\|_2=\sqrt{1-|u^*x_k|^2}.
$$
This is the <norm> of the component in the <orthogonal complement> of $u$, equivalently the sine of the acute <angle> to its line. It ignores the arbitrary sign or complex unit factor of an <eigenvector>. Under <power iteration convergence for a diagonalizable matrix>, $\varepsilon_k=O(\gamma^k)$ in <Big O notation>. The <orthogonal projection> defines how we measure direction; it does not assume that the <eigenbasis> of $A$ is <orthogonal>. In computation, the projection form avoids subtracting nearly equal numbers inside the square root.

= Sign and phase in power iteration
{parent=Power iteration}
{title2=$x_k=\frac{c_1\lambda_1^k}{|c_1\lambda_1^k|}u_1+O(\gamma^k)$}

Under <power iteration convergence for a diagonalizable matrix>, take $v_1=u_1$ to be a <unit vector>. The displayed formula describes both the dominant <eigenvector> direction and its rotating complex unit factor. With real data and a positive <dominant eigenvalue>, $x_k$ converges to $u_1$ or $-u_1$ according to the sign of $c_1$. With a negative <dominant eigenvalue>, consecutive <vectors> approach opposite signs even though their <power iteration direction error> tends to zero.

For complex data write $\lambda_1=|\lambda_1|e^{i\phi}$, where $\phi$ is its <complex argument>. The leading factor turns by $e^{i\phi}$ per step, so the <vectors> generally do not converge. For example, $A=\operatorname{diag}(2i,1)$ and $x_0=(1,1)^T/\sqrt2$ give $A^kx_0=((2i)^k,1)^T/\sqrt2$: the first component cycles through four phases while the ratio of component <moduli> tends to zero. Its <power iteration direction error> is $2^{-k}/\sqrt{1+2^{-2k}}$. Multiplying $x_k$ by $e^{-i(\arg c_1+k\phi)}$ gives a sequence converging to $u_1$.

A stopping rule based only on $\|x_{k+1}-x_k\|_2$ therefore rejects successful sign-alternating iterations. A comparison invariant under complex unit factors is
$$
\min_{|\omega|=1}\|x_{k+1}-\omega x_k\|_2=\sqrt{2-2|x_k^*x_{k+1}|}.
$$
Even this comparison can be small for a slowly changing wrong mixture; the <eigenpair residual> supplies a separate diagnostic.

= Power iteration eigenpair diagnostics
{parent=Power iteration}
{title2=$\rho_k=x_k^*Ax_k,\quad r_k=Ax_k-\rho_kx_k$}

For each <unit vector> iterate compute the <Rayleigh quotient> $\rho_k=x_k^*Ax_k$ and the <eigenpair residual> $r_k=Ax_k-\rho_kx_k$. Use the same multiplication $Ax_k$ for both diagnostics and the next normalized iterate. For any complex <matrix>, this <Rayleigh quotient> minimizes $\|Ax_k-\mu x_k\|_2$ over complex <scalars> $\mu$, because $x_k^*r_k=0$. Under <power iteration convergence for a diagonalizable matrix>, $\rho_k\to\lambda_1$ and $\|r_k\|_2\to0$, even when the <vectors> alternate signs or phases.<Numerical Analysis, Spring 2022/Power iteration (2022-03-07 lecture notes)>[§3, PDF p. 3: approximate eigenvector equation and Rayleigh quotient]{ref}

A dimensionless stopping quantity is
$$
\eta_k=\frac{\|r_k\|_2}{\|A\|_2+|\rho_k|},
$$
when the denominator is positive; $\|A\|_2$ is the <operator norm>, and a known upper bound may replace it if computing it is costly. A small <eigenpair residual> certifies a small <backward error of an approximate eigenpair>, but does not establish that the <eigenvalue> found is dominant.

For a <Hermitian matrix> with <orthonormal eigenbasis> $u_j$, write $x_k=\sum_j a_ju_j$. Then the independently useful identity
$$
\|r_k\|_2^2=\sum_j|a_j|^2|\lambda_j-\rho_k|^2
$$
shows that some <eigenvalue> lies within $\|r_k\|_2$ of $\rho_k$. If a specific target <eigenvalue> $\lambda_1$ is separated from all the others by $\delta_k=\min_{j>1}|\lambda_j-\rho_k|>0$, it also gives $\varepsilon_k\le\|r_k\|_2/\delta_k$. This separation is measured from the computed <Rayleigh quotient>; it differs from the dominant-<modulus> <spectral gap>. For a <nonnormal matrix>, the same <orthogonal> expansion is unavailable and a small <eigenpair residual> alone need not imply a comparably small distance to the <spectrum>.

= Power iteration failure cases
{parent=Power iteration}

A tied largest <modulus> can prevent even direction convergence. With $D=\operatorname{diag}(2,-2)$ and $x_0=(1,1)^T/\sqrt2$,
$$
x_k=\frac1{\sqrt2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\(-1)^k\end{pmatrix},\qquad \rho_k=0,\qquad \|r_k\|_2=2.
$$
The two directions form a cycle; neither is an <eigenvector>. There is no dominant-<modulus> <spectral gap>. A tied <modulus> is not a universal failure: an initial <eigenvector> remains on its line, and $A=2I$ preserves every direction. <Subspace iteration> addresses a separated cluster by targeting a <dominant invariant subspace> instead of one <eigenvector>.

An <orthogonal> start can fail despite a strict <spectral gap>. For $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$ and $x_0=(1,-1)^T/\sqrt2$, the dominant component is zero, so $Ax_0=x_0$ and every iterate equals $x_0$. The <Rayleigh quotient> is exactly $1$ and the <eigenpair residual> is zero, although the <dominant eigenvalue> is $3$. Exact arithmetic never creates a missing component.<Templates for the Solution of Algebraic Eigenvalue Problems: A Practical Guide/Power Method>{ref}

\Image[power-iteration-tied-modulus-orthogonal-start.png]
{title=Tied <eigenvalue> <moduli>, an <orthogonal> start, and sign alternation in <power iteration>}

The third panel contrasts these failures with $N=\operatorname{diag}(-3,1)$, $x_0=(1,1)^T/\sqrt2$, for which
$$
x_k=\frac1{\sqrt{1+3^{-2k}}}\begin{pmatrix}(-1)^k\\3^{-k}\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Here the horizontal <eigenvector> direction converges with a strict <spectral gap>, while the normalized <vectors> alternate signs. This is the successful direction convergence described under <sign and phase in power iteration>.

For a <nonorthogonal> <eigenbasis>, even the word “orthogonal” must be used carefully. The <diagonalizable matrix> $C=\begin{pmatrix}3&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ has right <eigenvectors> $v_1=(1,0)^T$, $v_2=(-1,1)^T$, and dominant <left eigenvector> $w_1=(1,1)^T$. For $x_0=(a,b)^T$, the dominant coefficient is $c_1=w_1^Tx_0=a+b$. The starting <vector> $v_2$ is <nonorthogonal> to $v_1$ but stays at the smaller <eigenvalue> $1$; $(0,1)^T$ is <orthogonal> to $v_1$ but has $c_1=1$ and converges to the dominant direction. The <left eigenvector> test, not the right-<eigenvector> <inner product>, controls the outcome.

A <random vector> drawn from a <continuous probability distribution> with a <probability density function> on the full real or complex <vector space> has $c_1\ne0$ with probability one. This avoids the exact missing-component <hyperplane>, but does not guarantee a large component or fast convergence. <Floating-point arithmetic> may seed a missing component, yet a structurally preserved zero can remain zero, so accidental perturbations are not a convergence guarantee.

= Gaussian elimination
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

Gaussian elimination applies elementary row operations to reduce a linear system to triangular form. Dense elimination uses $O(n^3)$ arithmetic operations.

= Pivot element
{parent=Gaussian elimination}
{title2=$a_{kk}^{(k-1)}$}

A <pivot element> is the current diagonal entry used to eliminate entries below it in <Gaussian elimination>, after any row interchange. At step $k$ it is $a_{kk}^{(k-1)}$, where $A^{(k-1)}$ is the current reduced <matrix>. Computing <elimination multipliers> requires this entry to be nonzero. A zero <pivot element> can reflect an unsuitable row order even for an <invertible matrix>, as <pivoted LU decomposition> illustrates.

= Elimination multiplier
{parent=Gaussian elimination}
{title2=$l_{ik}=a_{ik}^{(k-1)}/a_{kk}^{(k-1)}$}

For $i>k$, an <elimination multiplier> is the <scalar> $l_{ik}=a_{ik}^{(k-1)}/a_{kk}^{(k-1)}$ used in the <elementary row operation> $R_i\leftarrow R_i-l_{ik}R_k$. This creates a zero below the <pivot element>. The remaining entries change by $a_{ij}\leftarrow a_{ij}-l_{ik}a_{kj}$ for $j>k$. Storing these <elimination multipliers> gives the subdiagonal entries of $L$ in <LU decomposition>; later row interchanges must also reorder the already stored coefficients.

= Sparse Gaussian elimination
{parent=Gaussian elimination}

A sparse direct solver performs <Gaussian elimination> while storing and updating only the predicted nonzero entries. A symbolic phase predicts the factor pattern from an elimination ordering, and a numerical phase evaluates the factors. The ordering can greatly alter <fill-in> and arithmetic work. For symmetric positive-definite systems, <Cholesky decomposition> avoids numerical pivoting; general systems require a balance between sparsity and stable pivot selection.

= Leaf elimination of a tree-pattern positive-definite matrix
{parent=Sparse Gaussian elimination}

Associate an undirected graph with a symmetric matrix's off-diagonal nonzero pattern. Eliminating vertex $i$ updates the remaining matrix by $a_{jk}\mapsto a_{jk}-a_{ji}a_{ik}/a_{ii}$. Only pairs of neighbors of $i$ can acquire <fill-in>. If the graph is a tree, remove leaves successively: each eliminated vertex has at most one remaining neighbor, so only a diagonal entry can change. Every <Schur complement> of a <positive-definite matrix> is positive definite, so no zero pivot forces a different ordering. Leaf removal therefore constructs a <perfect elimination ordering> and a fill-free factorization.

= Symbolic factorization
{parent=Sparse Gaussian elimination}

<Symbolic factorization> computes possible nonzero positions and update dependencies from a <sparse matrix> pattern and ordering before calculating numerical factor values. The <elimination graph> predicts fill and the <elimination tree> organizes dependencies. Numerical pivoting or exceptional cancellation can make the computed factor pattern differ from this structural prediction.

= Elimination tree
{parent=Sparse Gaussian elimination}
{title2=$\operatorname{parent}(j)=\min\{i>j:L_{ij}\ne0\}$}

For a symmetric factorization in a fixed ordering, the <elimination tree> assigns the displayed parent to a column of the lower-triangular factor when that set is nonempty. A column with no later entry is a root. The tree records dependencies of descendant updates on later pivots, supports symbolic factor allocation, and exposes parallel subtrees. It describes the factorization ordering, not merely a spanning tree of the original <matrix graph>.

= Nested dissection
{parent=Sparse Gaussian elimination}

A <vertex separator> divides the remaining unknowns into disconnected subdomains. Nested dissection recursively eliminates each subdomain before its separator, keeping interaction localized until late in the factorization. For a regular two-dimensional mesh with $N$ unknowns, separators have size $O(\sqrt N)$; the resulting direct factorization uses $O(N^{3/2})$ work and $O(N\log N)$ storage under the usual geometric separator assumptions.

= Minimum degree algorithm
{parent=Sparse Gaussian elimination}

At each step choose a remaining vertex of minimum degree in the current <elimination graph>. This heuristic reduces the size of the immediate <Schur complement> update and often reduces <fill-in>, but does not guarantee a globally optimal ordering. Its degrees must reflect earlier fill, rather than just the original <graph>.

= Fill-in
{parent=Sparse Gaussian elimination}

<Fill-in> consists of factor entries that become nonzero despite being zero in the original <sparse matrix>. In symmetric elimination these are the added edges of the <elimination graph>. Removing a high-degree vertex can create many fill entries, whereas a <perfect elimination ordering> creates none. Symbolic fill predicts possible nonzeros before floating-point arithmetic; special cancellation can reduce the numerical pattern.

= Matrix graph
{parent=Sparse Gaussian elimination}

For a symmetric <sparse matrix>, its <matrix graph> has one vertex per unknown and an edge $i-j$ whenever the off-diagonal entry $a_{ij}$ is structurally nonzero. Simultaneous row and column permutation relabels this <graph>. Nonsymmetric patterns are instead represented by directed or bipartite <graphs> when row and column roles must be distinguished.

= Elimination graph
{parent=Matrix graph}

One step of symmetric <Gaussian elimination> removes the pivot vertex and connects its remaining neighbors into a <clique>. The rule follows from the <Schur complement> update $a_{ij}\leftarrow a_{ij}-a_{ik}a_{kj}/a_{kk}$. Repeating it predicts the generic factor sparsity; exceptional numerical cancellations can remove structurally predicted entries.

= Fill-path criterion for symmetric elimination
{parent=Elimination graph}

After eliminating a vertex set $E$, a generic edge between remaining vertices $i,j$ exists exactly when the original <matrix graph> contains a path from $i$ to $j$ whose internal vertices all belong to $E$. Inductively, the new edges at pivot $k$ concatenate two such paths through $k$; conversely split any allowed path at $k$, or retain it when it avoids $k$. This characterizes fill by <graph> reachability.

= Columnwise partial pivoting
{parent=Gaussian elimination}
{title2=$PA=LU$}

= Partial pivoting
{synonym}

At each step of <Gaussian elimination>, select the largest absolute entry in the uneliminated part of the current column and interchange rows to put it on the diagonal. A nonsingular trailing <Schur complement> has a nonzero entry in its first column, so this procedure always finds a nonzero pivot in exact arithmetic. The <permutation matrix> $P$ records row interchanges; earlier multipliers must be interchanged too when assembling the lower-triangular factor. This yields the <LU decomposition> $PA=LU$ with unit diagonal in $L$. Searching within columns and interchanging rows is distinct from interchanging columns.

= Row echelon form
{parent=Gaussian elimination}

A <matrix> is in row echelon form if zero rows follow all nonzero rows, each nonzero row has a first nonzero entry (pivot) to the right of the pivot in the preceding row, and entries below each pivot vanish. <Elementary row operations> bring every <matrix> to this form: choose a nonzero entry in the earliest nonzero column, swap its row to the top, eliminate entries below it, and continue on the remaining submatrix. The nonzero rows are independent by their successively positioned pivots; the pivot columns are independent and span the <column space>. Thus the number of pivots equals both <row rank> and <column rank>.

= Reduced row echelon form
{parent=Row echelon form}

A <row echelon form> whose pivots are one and whose pivot columns have zeros in every other row. Over a <field>, each <row space> has a unique such <matrix> after zero rows are removed. It describes a <vector subspace> independently of which initial <basis> was used; <Gaussian binomial coefficients> exploit this uniqueness to count <vector subspaces> over a <finite field>.

= Elementary row operation
{parent=Gaussian elimination}

= Row operation
{synonym}

An <elementary row operation> interchanges two rows of a <matrix>, multiplies one row by a nonzero scalar, or adds a scalar multiple of another row to it. These invertible operations are used in <Gaussian elimination>. For a square <matrix>, interchange changes the sign of its <determinant>, multiplication scales its <determinant> by the same scalar, and adding another row leaves its <determinant> unchanged, by multilinearity and alternation.

= Elementary matrix
{parent=Elementary row operation}

An elementary matrix is obtained by applying one <elementary row operation> to the <identity matrix>. Multiplying $A$ by it on the left performs the same row operation on $A$. Such a <matrix> is invertible: reverse a row swap, reverse a nonzero row scaling by its reciprocal, or reverse adding a multiple of another row by adding its negative. Right multiplication gives an <elementary column operation>. In particular these operations preserve <matrix rank>.

= LU decomposition
{title2=$A=LU$}
{c}
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}
{wiki}

= LU factorization
{c}
{synonym}

An <LU decomposition> writes a <square matrix> as $A=LU$, with $L$ a <lower triangular matrix> and $U$ an <upper triangular matrix>. We use a <unit triangular matrix> $L$, meaning $l_{ii}=1$. The factors record <Gaussian elimination>: $U$ contains the reduced equations, while $L$ stores the <elimination multipliers> needed to reconstruct the original equations. This makes <reusing LU factors for multiple right-hand sides> inexpensive.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.4.3, Definition 2.4.2 and equation (2.4.7): unit lower triangular L and upper triangular U]{ref}

For an <invertible matrix>, unpivoted <LU decomposition> requires nonzero <pivot elements> at every elimination step; invertibility alone does not guarantee this. In general use <pivoted LU decomposition>, with the explicit convention $PA=LU$ and a <permutation matrix> $P$. Once the factors exist, solving a <linear system> $Ax=b$ needs <forward substitution> followed by <back substitution>, rather than forming a <matrix inverse>.

= Computed three-by-three LU decomposition
{c}
{parent=LU decomposition}
{title2=$A=LU$}

Here is an original example of <Gaussian elimination> that can be checked entirely with <integers>. Start with
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1&1\\4&5&3\\-2&8&4\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The first <pivot element> is $2$. The <elimination multipliers> are $l_{21}=4/2=2$ and $l_{31}=-2/2=-1$. Apply the <elementary row operations> $R_2\leftarrow R_2-2R_1$ and $R_3\leftarrow R_3+R_1$ to obtain
$$
A^{(1)}=\begin{pmatrix}2&1&1\\0&3&1\\0&9&5\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Now the second <pivot element> is $3$, and $l_{32}=9/3=3$. The <elementary row operation> $R_3\leftarrow R_3-3R_2$ gives
$$
U=\begin{pmatrix}2&1&1\\0&3&1\\0&0&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\2&1&0\\-1&3&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad LU=A.
$$
Importantly, $l_{32}$ comes from the current reduced <matrix>, not the original entry $a_{32}=8$. To reconstruct $A$ from $U$, its second row is twice the first row of $U$ plus the second; its third row is minus the first row of $U$ plus three times the second plus the third. These are exactly the coefficients in the <lower triangular matrix> $L$.

\Image[lu-elimination-structure.png]
{title=<Gaussian elimination> turns a three-by-three <matrix> into an <upper triangular matrix>, while <elimination multipliers> are stored in a <lower triangular matrix>}

The unpivoted calculation here illustrates the structure of <LU decomposition>; applying <partial pivoting> to this particular $A$ would choose a different first row and different factors. Both procedures can represent the same <linear system> when their <permutation matrices> are handled consistently.

= Unit triangular matrix
{parent=LU decomposition}
{title2=$t_{ii}=1$}

A <unit triangular matrix> is a <triangular matrix> whose diagonal entries are all one. It is an <invertible matrix> with <determinant> one. In the convention used for <LU decomposition>, $L$ is a unit <lower triangular matrix>, so <forward substitution> needs no division by its diagonal entries.

= Triangular solves for LU decomposition
{c}
{parent=LU decomposition}
{title2=$Ly=Pb,\quad Ux=y$}

Given <pivoted LU decomposition> $PA=LU$, the <linear system> $Ax=b$ is equivalent to $LUx=Pb$. Introduce the intermediate <vector> $y=Ux$: first use <forward substitution> to solve $Ly=Pb$, then <back substitution> to solve $Ux=y$. With unpivoted <LU decomposition>, $P=I$ is the <identity matrix>, so the first right-hand side is simply $b$.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.4.4, equation (2.4.8) and Algorithm 2.4.2, and §2.6.3: two triangular solves and permutation of b]{ref}

For the factors in <computed three-by-three LU decomposition> and $b=(7,23,26)^T$, <forward substitution> gives
$$
y_1=7,\qquad y_2=23-2(7)=9,\qquad y_3=26+7-3(9)=6.
$$
Then <back substitution> gives
$$
x_3=6/2=3,\qquad x_2=(9-3)/3=2,\qquad x_1=(7-2-3)/2=1.
$$
Direct <matrix multiplication> verifies $A(1,2,3)^T=(7,23,26)^T$. More generally, the <back substitution> recurrence is
$$
x_i=\frac{y_i-\sum_{j=i+1}^{n}u_{ij}x_j}{u_{ii}},\qquad i=n,n-1,\ldots,1.
$$
The direction matters: the last row contains only $x_n$, whereas the first row generally involves every unknown. No dense <matrix inverse> is needed for either triangular solve.

= Forward substitution
{parent=Triangular solves for LU decomposition}
{title2=$y_i=(c_i-\sum_{j<i}l_{ij}y_j)/l_{ii}$}

<Forward substitution> solves a <linear system> $Ly=c$ with a <lower triangular matrix> $L$ and nonzero diagonal, from the first row downward:
$$
y_i=\frac{c_i-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}l_{ij}y_j}{l_{ii}},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n.
$$
The first <sum> is empty, so $y_1=c_1/l_{11}$. Each subsequent row uses only components already found. For a <unit triangular matrix> $L$, omit the division because $l_{ii}=1$. The complementary procedure, <back substitution>, solves an <upper triangular matrix> system from the last row upward.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.3.2, equations (2.3.7)–(2.3.9), and §2.3.3, Algorithms 2.3.1–2.3.2: forward and backward substitution]{ref}

= Pivoted LU decomposition
{c}
{parent=LU decomposition}
{title2=$PA=LU$}

We fix the convention $PA=LU$: the <permutation matrix> $P$ acts on the rows of $A$, and the same row ordering must be applied to $b$ before the <triangular solves for LU decomposition>. Equivalently, $A=P^TLU$, since $P^{-1}=P^T$. LAPACK documents its factors as $A=PLU$ instead, so check the defining equation rather than just the letter $P$.<DGETRS (LAPACK 3.3.1) by LAPACK project>[LAPACK DGETRS, A argument: factors from DGETRF satisfy $A=PLU$]{ref} <Partial pivoting> selects the largest absolute candidate in the current column, among rows $k,\ldots,n$, before forming <elimination multipliers>.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.6.1, Definition 2.6.1; §2.6.2, Definition 2.6.2 and equation (2.6.1): pivot selection and reordered-row factorization]{ref}

A zero first <pivot element> does not imply a <singular matrix>. For example,
$$
A_0=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\2&3\end{pmatrix},\qquad \det A_0=-2,\qquad P=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad PA_0=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}}_{L}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}2&3\\0&1\end{pmatrix}}_{U}.
$$
Unpivoted <Gaussian elimination> would divide $2$ by zero. Indeed, no $A_0=LU$ with a unit <lower triangular matrix> $L$ exists: $u_{11}=0$ would force $(LU)_{21}=l_{21}u_{11}=0$, contradicting $(A_0)_{21}=2$. One row interchange resolves the obstruction. For $b_0=(1,5)^T$, use $Pb_0=(5,1)^T$; <back substitution> then gives $x=(1,1)^T$. Using the original $b_0$ with these factors would solve a different <linear system>.

<Partial pivoting> bounds each <elimination multiplier> by one in absolute value in exact arithmetic. This helps control <roundoff error>, but intermediate entries can still grow; it is not an unconditional guarantee of <numerical stability> for every <matrix>.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.6.4, Example 2.6.6 and the concluding discussion: stability of unpivoted and pivoted factorization]{ref} It also does not remove the original problem's <condition number>. For the <diagonal matrix> $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,\varepsilon)$ with $0<\varepsilon<1$, the <condition number> in the <Euclidean norm> is $\kappa_2(D)=1/\varepsilon$.
