Row permutations preserve this <condition number>, because they preserve <Euclidean norms>. Even an exact solve changes from $(1,1)^T$ to $(1,1+\eta/\varepsilon)^T$ when the right-hand side changes from $(1,\varepsilon)^T$ to $(1,\varepsilon+\eta)^T$. This sensitivity is a property of the <linear system>, not a zero-<pivot element> failure.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.8, Definition 2.8.1, and §2.8.1, Theorem 2.8.1, equations (2.8.5)–(2.8.6): condition number and right-hand-side sensitivity]{ref}

= Reusing LU factors for multiple right-hand sides
{c}
{parent=LU decomposition}
{title2=$AX=B,\quad LY=PB,\quad UX=Y$}

When a fixed <matrix> $A$ is used with several forcing or measurement <vectors>, compute its <pivoted LU decomposition> once. Collect $r$ right-hand sides into the columns of $B=(b^{(1)}\ \cdots\ b^{(r)})$, and the corresponding solutions into $X$. Then
$$
AX=B,\qquad LY=PB,\qquad UX=Y.
$$
Each column undergoes the same row permutation, <forward substitution> and <back substitution>. Neither $L$, $U$ nor $P$ depends on $B$. This is the useful separation between preparing a <linear system> and applying its solution procedure repeatedly. LAPACK implements it by taking previously computed factors, permuting all right-hand sides, and performing the two triangular solves.<DGETRS (LAPACK 3.3.1) by LAPACK project>[LAPACK DGETRS, version 3.3.1, April 2011, Purpose, NRHS and B arguments, and nontransposed branch: DLASWP followed by lower-unit and upper-nonunit DTRSM calls]{ref}

For the unpivoted factors in <computed three-by-three LU decomposition>, take
$$
B=\begin{pmatrix}7&0&4\\23&2&6\\26&4&-8\end{pmatrix},\qquad Y=\begin{pmatrix}7&0&4\\9&2&-2\\6&-2&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad X=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&2\\2&1&-1\\3&-1&1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Direct <matrix multiplication> checks all three identities $LY=B$, $UX=Y$ and $AX=B$. The first column reproduces the previous solve; the other two use exactly the same factors. New right-hand sides can also arrive one at a time. If $A$ changes, these stored factors generally need to be recomputed.

\Image[lu-pivot-repeated-solves.png]
{title=One row permutation fixes a zero <pivot element>; each new right-hand side reuses the same <LU decomposition> and two triangular solves}

= LU factorization and solve costs
{c}
{parent=LU decomposition}
{title2=$\tfrac23n^3+2rn^2$}

For classical dense <Gaussian elimination> over the <real numbers>, count each scalar <addition>, <subtraction>, <multiplication> or <division> as one <arithmetic operation>. At step $k$, there are $n-k$ <elimination multipliers> to divide out and $(n-k)^2$ remaining entries, each requiring one <multiplication> and one <subtraction>. Thus this implementation's unpivoted factorization count is
$$
F(n)=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\bigl((n-k)+2(n-k)^2\bigr)=\frac{n(n-1)(4n+1)}6=\frac23n^3+O(n^2).
$$
Here $O$ uses <Big O notation>. <Partial pivoting> adds $O(n^2)$ comparisons and row movement, leaving the leading arithmetic count unchanged. The leading $\tfrac23n^3$ count agrees with the standard dense <LU decomposition> analysis.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.5.2, Definition 2.5.2; §2.5, equations (2.5.7)–(2.5.8) and Theorem 2.5.1; lower-order counts depend on implementation]{ref}

With unit $L$, <forward substitution> uses $n(n-1)$ <arithmetic operations>. <Back substitution> uses another $n(n-1)$ plus $n$ diagonal divisions. Together these cost $2n^2-n$ per right-hand side; applying a stored row permutation moves $n$ entries and requires no scalar arithmetic. In <Big O notation>, <reusing LU factors for multiple right-hand sides> therefore costs
$$
\frac23n^3+2rn^2+O(n^2+rn),
$$
whereas refactorizing for each of the $r$ right-hand sides costs $\tfrac23rn^3+O(rn^2)$. For $n=1000$ and $r=10$, the leading counts are about $6.87\times10^8$ versus $6.69\times10^9$ <arithmetic operations>, nearly a tenfold saving. These are operation counts, not measured runtimes: memory access and optimized <matrix multiplication> also affect speed.<Fundamentals of Numerical Computation by Tobin A. Driscoll and others>[§2.5.2, Important note after Definition 2.5.2: flop counts are a rough runtime model; the numerical comparison here is an original calculation]{ref}

= LDL decomposition
{title2=$A=LDL^T$}
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}
{c}

An LDL decomposition of a real symmetric matrix is
$$
A=LDL^T,
$$
where $L$ is unit lower triangular and $D$ is diagonal. Symmetric elimination computes it one <Schur complement> at a time. The matrix is positive definite exactly when every diagonal pivot in $D$ is positive.

= Eigenpair residual
{title2=$r=Av-\lambda v$}
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}

For an approximate eigenpair $(\widetilde\lambda,\widetilde v)$, the residual is
$$
r=A\widetilde v-\widetilde\lambda\widetilde v.
$$

= Backward error of an approximate eigenpair
{parent=Eigenpair residual}

If $\|\widetilde v\|_2=1$, the smallest operator-norm perturbation that makes $(\widetilde\lambda,\widetilde v)$ an exact eigenpair has norm $\|r\|_2$. One such rank-one perturbation is
$$
E=-r\widetilde v^T.
$$

= Householder QR decomposition
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

Successive Householder reflections zero subdiagonal column entries and factor a matrix into an orthogonal factor and an upper-triangular factor.

= Householder reduction of an overdetermined consistent system
{parent=Householder QR decomposition}
{title2=$Q^Tb=(c,0)^T$}

For full column rank $A\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}$, a full <QR factorization> gives $Q^TA=(R_1,0)^T$ with invertible upper triangular $R_1$. The equation $Ax=b$ is consistent exactly when the final $m-n$ entries of $Q^Tb$ vanish. If consistent, <back substitution> gives its unique solution. This separates consistency from uniqueness in overdetermined systems.

= Householder tridiagonalization
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

Orthogonal Householder similarities reduce a real symmetric matrix to symmetric tridiagonal form.

= Skew-symmetric Householder tridiagonalization
{parent=Householder tridiagonalization}
{title2=$A'=A+vw^T-wv^T,\quad w=2Av/(v^Tv)$}

An <orthogonal> similarity preserves skew symmetry, so upper-Hessenberg reduction of a real <skew-symmetric matrix> is automatically tridiagonal with zero diagonal. For a <Householder reflection>, the scalar $v^TAv$ vanishes, eliminating the fourth term of the general similarity update. Storing only one triangle and using the displayed skew rank-two update costs $2n^3/3+O(n^2)$ scalar products, the same leading product count as optimized symmetric tridiagonalization; the simplification saves lower-order work.

= Unshifted QR algorithm
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}

The unshifted QR algorithm factors $A_k=Q_kR_k$ and forms $A_{k+1}=R_kQ_k=Q_k^TA_kQ_k$, preserving eigenvalues while driving the matrix toward block diagonal form.

This is the zero-shift case of the <QR algorithm>.

= Accumulated QR factorization identity
{parent=Unshifted QR algorithm}
{c}

Let
$$
\overline Q_k=Q_0Q_1\cdots Q_k,
\qquad
\overline R_k=R_kR_{k-1}\cdots R_0.
$$
Then
$$
A_{k+1}=\overline Q_k^TA\overline Q_k,
\qquad
A^{k+1}=\overline Q_k\overline R_k.
$$
Thus $\overline Q_k\overline R_k$ is a <QR decomposition> of the matrix power $A^{k+1}$. In particular, the first $r$ columns of $\overline Q_k$ span the same subspace as the first $r$ columns of $A^{k+1}$ whenever those columns are independent.

= Symmetric bandwidth preservation under QR iteration
{parent=Unshifted QR algorithm}

An unshifted QR step preserves the bandwidth of a real symmetric banded matrix. A Givens-rotation implementation exposes this as bulges created during triangularization and removed when the factors are multiplied in reverse order.

= Simultaneous iteration interpretation of the QR algorithm
{parent=Unshifted QR algorithm}

The accumulated orthogonal factor in QR iteration is the $Q$ factor of a matrix power, so its leading columns span the same spaces as simultaneous power iteration.

= Two-column dominant-subspace condition
{parent=Simultaneous iteration interpretation of the QR algorithm}

Let a real <symmetric matrix> have an <orthonormal eigenbasis> $w_1,\ldots,w_n$, with
$$
|\lambda_1|\leq\cdots\leq|\lambda_{n-2}|<
|\lambda_{n-1}|=|\lambda_n|.
$$
Write two starting vectors as
$$
u=\sum_i b_iw_i,
\qquad
v=\sum_i c_iw_i.
$$
For two-column <subspace iteration> to recover
$\operatorname{span}(w_{n-1},w_n)$, the two projections onto that dominant subspace must be independent. The exact condition is
$$
\det\begin{pmatrix}
b_{n-1}&c_{n-1}\\
b_n&c_n
\end{pmatrix}
=b_{n-1}c_n-b_nc_{n-1}\ne0.
$$
Requiring each of the four coefficients to be nonzero does not imply this determinant condition.

= Block deflation in the QR algorithm
{parent=Unshifted QR algorithm}

When an off-block subdiagonal entry tends to zero, QR iteration asymptotically separates the corresponding invariant spectral blocks even if eigenvalues within one block have equal modulus.

= Stationary iterative method for a linear system
{parent=Numerical linear algebra}

A stationary iteration has the form
$$
x^{(k+1)}=Hx^{(k)}+v,
$$
with a fixed iteration matrix $H$. It converges for every initial vector exactly when the <spectral radius> satisfies $\rho(H)<1$.

This is an <iterative method> whose update matrix remains fixed.

= Semiconvergence of cyclic Poisson iterations
{parent=Stationary iterative method for a linear system}
{title2=$A=C+C^{-1}-2I$}

The periodic second-difference <circulant matrix> has constant <null space>, so solvability requires $\sum b_j=0$. Its <Jacobi method> has eigenvalues $\cos(2\pi j/n)$: for odd $n$ it converges to some solution, while for even $n$ an alternating mode oscillates unless absent from the error. Lexicographic <Gauss-Seidel iteration> converges to some solution for every compatible right-hand side and every initial vector. For $K=-A=D+L+U$, a coordinate sweep decreases the energy $e^TKe/2$ by the sum of squared coordinate changes. This excludes all nonconstant unit-modulus eigenmodes. The constant unit eigenvalue is simple, since the invariant left vector $w=(0,1,\ldots,1,2)^T$ has $w^T\mathbf1=n\ne0$. Neither method is a strict contraction to a unique solution without fixing the constant nullspace.

= Richardson iteration
{c}
{parent=Stationary iterative method for a linear system}
{title2=$x_{n+1}=x_n-\tau(Ax_n-b)$}

For a real symmetric positive definite <matrix> $A$, the error in each <eigenvector> direction is multiplied by $1-\tau\lambda$. Convergence from every initial vector is therefore equivalent to $0<\tau<2/\lambda_{\max}$. At either endpoint there is a nondecaying error direction. The optimal constant parameter is $2/(\lambda_{\min}+\lambda_{\max})$, giving contraction factor $(\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min})/(\lambda_{\max}+\lambda_{\min})$.

= Gauss-Seidel method
{parent=Stationary iterative method for a linear system}
{c}
{wiki=Gauss–Seidel_method}

= Gauss-Seidel iteration
{c}
{synonym}

With $A=D+L+U$, the <Gauss-Seidel method> solves $(D+L)x^{(k+1)}=b-Ux^{(k)}$. It uses each newly computed coordinate immediately in the remaining triangular solve. It is <successive over-relaxation> with parameter one, and converges from every start when $A$ is symmetric positive definite.

= Successive over-relaxation
{parent=Stationary iterative method for a linear system}
{title2=$\omega$}
{wiki}

= SOR
{synonym}
{c}

For a splitting $A=D+L+U$, <successive over-relaxation> updates
$$
(D+\omega L)x^{(k+1)}=[(1-\omega)D-\omega U]x^{(k)}+\omega b.
$$
It reduces to the <Gauss-Seidel method> at $\omega=1$. If $A$ is symmetric positive definite, the <Householder-John theorem> applies to $M=D/\omega+L$, $N=(1/\omega-1)D-U$, since $M^T+N=(2/\omega-1)D$ is positive definite for $0<\omega<2$.

= Iteration matrix
{title2=$H$}
{parent=Stationary iterative method for a linear system}

For a stationary iteration $x^{(k+1)}=Hx^{(k)}+v$, the matrix $H$ propagates the error by $e^{(k+1)}=He^{(k)}$. Convergence from every initial error is equivalent to $\rho(H)<1$.

= Matrix splitting
{parent=Stationary iterative method for a linear system}
{wiki}

A splitting $A=M-N$ with invertible $M$ produces
$$
x^{(k+1)}=M^{-1}Nx^{(k)}+M^{-1}b.
$$

= Householder-John theorem
{parent=Matrix splitting}
{c}

Let $A=M-N$ be Hermitian positive definite. If $M^*+N$ is also Hermitian positive definite, then $M$ is invertible and
$$
\rho(M^{-1}N)<1.
$$
Thus the stationary iteration associated with the splitting converges from every initial vector.

= Jacobi method
{parent=Stationary iterative method for a linear system}
{c}
{wiki}

Writing $A=D+L+U$, where $D$ is diagonal and $L,U$ are strictly triangular, the Jacobi iteration is
$$
x^{(k+1)}=D^{-1}\left(b-(L+U)x^{(k)}\right).
$$

= Skew-tridiagonal Jacobi and Gauss-Seidel iterations
{parent=Jacobi method}

For $A=\mu^{-1}I+U-U^T$, with $U$ the four-dimensional unit superdiagonal, the <Jacobi method> has eigenvalues $2i\mu\cos(j\pi/5)$, $j=1,\ldots,4$. The <Gauss-Seidel method> has two zero eigenvalues and $-\mu^2(3\pm\sqrt5)/2$. Therefore both methods converge for every starting vector exactly when $0<|\mu|<(\sqrt5-1)/2$. This equality of convergence ranges follows from their spectra, not from positive-definiteness of the nonsymmetric coefficient matrix.

= Weighted Jacobi method
{parent=Jacobi method}

= Relaxed Jacobi method
{synonym}
{c}

= Weighted Jacobi
{synonym}

With relaxation parameter $\omega$, weighted Jacobi updates by
$$
x^{(\nu+1)}
=x^{(\nu)}+\omega D^{-1}(b-Ax^{(\nu)}).
$$
Its <iteration matrix> is $H_\omega=I-\omega D^{-1}A$.

= Weighted Jacobi eigenvalues for a square Dirichlet grid
{parent=Weighted Jacobi method}
{title2=$\lambda_{k,l}(H_\omega)$}

For the five-point discretization of $-\Delta+c$ on the unit square, the weighted iteration <eigenvalues> are $1-\omega[4\sin^2(\pi kh/2)+4\sin^2(\pi lh/2)+ch^2]/(4+ch^2)$. A fixed parameter converges for every mesh when $0<\omega\leq1$, but $\omega=1$ poorly damps the highest frequencies.

= Weighted Jacobi method for the one-dimensional Poisson equation
{parent=Weighted Jacobi method}

For the centered second-difference matrix with diagonal $-2$ and off-diagonals $1$, the weighted-Jacobi eigenvalues are
$$
\lambda_k(\omega)
=1-\omega+\omega\cos\frac{k\pi}{n+1},
\qquad 1\leq k\leq n.
$$
The method converges for every grid size exactly when $0<\omega\leq1$.

= High-frequency smoothing factor of weighted Jacobi
{title2=$\mu_\omega$}
{parent=Weighted Jacobi method for the one-dimensional Poisson equation}

For modes with $k/(n+1)\in[1/2,1]$ and large $n$, the worst attenuation factor is
$$
\mu_\omega=\max\{|1-\omega|,|1-2\omega|\}.
$$
It is minimized by $\omega=2/3$, for which $\mu_\omega=1/3$.

= Exact finite-grid high-frequency Jacobi smoothing optimum
{parent=High-frequency smoothing factor of weighted Jacobi}
{title2=$\omega_*=2/(a+b),\quad\mu_*=(b-a)/(a+b)$}

For the one-dimensional Dirichlet second-difference grid, high sine modes have eigenvalues $1-\omega t_k$, with $t_k=1-\cos(k\pi/(n+1))$ and $k\geq\lceil(n+1)/2\rceil$. Let $a$ and $b$ be the smallest and largest of these $t_k$. Convexity gives attenuation $\max(|1-\omega a|,|1-\omega b|)$. Balancing its endpoint values with opposite signs gives the displayed exact minimax choice. For odd $n$, $a=1$; for even $n$, $a=1+\sin(\pi/[2(n+1)])$, and always $b=1+\cos(\pi/(n+1))$. Only in the large-grid or uniform-in-grid limit do these become $\omega_*=2/3$, $\mu_*=1/3$.

= Jacobi convergence for a three-by-three equicorrelation matrix
{parent=Jacobi method}

For a three-by-three matrix with diagonal entries one and every off-diagonal entry $\alpha$, the <Jacobi method> iteration matrix has eigenvalue $-2\alpha$ on the all-ones vector and eigenvalue $\alpha$ with multiplicity two on its orthogonal complement. Its <spectral radius> is $2|\alpha|$, so Jacobi iteration converges exactly when $|\alpha|<1/2$.

= Jacobi convergence for a symmetric positive-definite tridiagonal matrix
{parent=Jacobi method}

If $A=D+L+L^T$ is symmetric positive definite and tridiagonal, set $S=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1,1,-1,\ldots)$. Then
$$
SAS=D-L-L^T
$$
is positive definite. Applying the <Householder-John theorem> to $M=D$ and $N=-(L+L^T)$ proves that the Jacobi iteration converges.

= Linear stability domain
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

= Absolute stability
{synonym}

For a one-step method applied to $y'=\lambda y$, write $y_{n+1}=R(h\lambda)y_n$. Its linear stability domain is
$$
\mathcal S=\{z\in\mathbb C:|R(z)|\leq1\}.
$$
Forward Euler has $R(z)=1+z$, while backward Euler has $R(z)=(1-z)^{-1}$ and is A-stable.

= Strict linear stability domain
{parent=Linear stability domain}
{title2=$\mathcal D_{\rm decay}=\{z:y_n\to0\}$}

The strict decay convention for a <linear stability domain> demands that every numerical mode tends to zero on the <Dahlquist test equation>. For a one-step <stability function> it requires $|R(z)|<1$; for a <multistep method> it requires every <amplification root> to have <modulus> below one. This differs from a boundedness convention that allows simple <unit-circle roots>, and from mesh-uniform finite-time <numerical stability>. https://www.damtp.cam.ac.uk/user/na/PartIB/Lect10.pdf[Cambridge numerical analysis notes] use the decay convention. Explicitly specifying the convention is essential for methods with undamped modes.

= A-alpha stability
{c}
{parent=Linear stability domain}
{title2=$A(\alpha)$}

A numerical time-stepping method is $A(\alpha)$-stable if its <absolute stability> region contains the negative-axis sector $\{z:|\arg(-z)|\leq\alpha\}$, for $0<\alpha\leq\pi/2$. With a <stability function> $R$, this requires $|R(z)|\leq1$ throughout that sector. At $\alpha=\pi/2$, this is <A-stability>.

= Euler method
{c}
{parent=Linear stability domain}
{wiki}
{title2=$y_{n+1}=y_n+h f(t_n,y_n)$}

= Explicit Euler method
{synonym}

= Forward Euler method
{synonym}
{c}

The <Euler method> approximates an <initial value problem> $y'=f(t,y)$, $y(t_0)=y_0$, by evaluating the <derivative> at the current point and moving along that <tangent line> for a time $h$. With $t_n=t_0+nh$, its displayed update needs one evaluation of $f$ per step. This simple rule turns local slope information into a whole approximate trajectory. MIT's course notes derive it by dropping the second-order <Taylor remainder>.https://web.mit.edu/10.001/Web/Course_Notes/Differential_Equations_Notes/node3.html[MIT 10.001, Forward and Backward Euler Methods, equations 6–7]{ref}

Take $y'=-2y$, $y(0)=1$, whose exact solution is the <exponential function> $y(t)=e^{-2t}$. The <Euler method> gives
$$
y_{n+1}=(1-2h)y_n,\qquad y_n=(1-2h)^n.
$$
At $h=0.2$, the first values are $1,0.6,0.36,0.216$. At $t=1$, five steps give $0.6^5=0.07776$, compared with $e^{-2}\approx0.135335$. The result decays but is too small: <absolute stability> alone does not ensure accuracy. At $h=1.2$, the first values are $1,-1.4,1.96,-2.744$, and at $t=6$ five steps give $-5.37824$, while the exact solution is about $0.00000614421$.

\Image[euler-stability-example.png]
{title=Euler approximations to exponential decay on the same finite time interval}

For the <Dahlquist test equation> $y'=\lambda y$, the <Euler method> has <stability function> $R(z)=1+z$ with $z=h\lambda$. Its <absolute stability> region is $|1+z|\leq1$, the closed disk centred at $-1$ with radius one. For the present real decay problem and $h>0$, bounded numerical values require $0<h\leq1$; strict decay requires $0<h<1$. At $h=1$ the values alternate $1,-1,1,\ldots$ without decay. These conditions follow directly from the <amplification factor> $1-2h$, matching MIT's criterion for $y'=-ay$.https://web.mit.edu/10.001/Web/Course_Notes/Differential_Equations_Notes/node3.html[MIT 10.001, Forward and Backward Euler Methods, linear decay example]{ref}

The <Euler method> is first order: with bounded second <derivative> and suitable <Lipschitz continuity>, its <global error> is $O(h)$ on a fixed interval. In this example the <logarithm> gives $\log(1-2h)=-2h-2h^2+O(h^3)$ for small positive $h$. At $t=nh$, this means $y_n=e^{-2t}(1-2th+O(h^2))$. It explains both the downward bias and why halving the step roughly halves the error once $h$ is sufficiently small.

= A-stability
{parent=Linear stability domain}
{c}
{wiki}

= A-stable
{c}
{synonym}

A method is A-stable when its stability domain contains the closed left half-plane.

= A-stability of near-diagonal exponential Padé approximants
{c}
{parent=A-stability}
{title2=$n-2\le m\le n$}

For nonnegative degrees $m,n$ with $n-2\le m\le n$, the $[m/n]$ <Padé approximant> $R$ to the <exponential function> has no poles in the left half-plane and satisfies $|R(z)|\le1$ for $\operatorname{Re}z\le0$. Consequently a numerical method having that <stability function> is <A-stable>. In particular, the near-diagonal choice $[s-1/s]$ gives the <Radau IIA method> stability function, while $[s/s]$ corresponds to the <Gauss collocation method>. The theorem concerns these exponential approximants, not arbitrary rational approximations with the same degrees.

= Principal-root obstruction to A-stability
{parent=A-stability}
{title2=$\operatorname{Re}\log w(z)>0\text{ for some }\operatorname{Re}z<0$}

Suppose a consistent <linear multistep method> has a simple principal <polynomial root> and its analytic <logarithm> satisfies $\log w(z)=z+Cz^{2r}+O(z^{2r+1})$ near zero, with real coefficients. If $C(-1)^r>0$, then at $z=i\varepsilon$ the real part of $\log w$ is positive to leading order. The same remains true after giving $z$ a small negative real part of smaller magnitude than that leading term. Thus a growing <amplification factor> occurs inside the left half-plane, ruling out <A-stability>. For an order-three method with $C>0$, choose $z=-C\varepsilon^4/2+i\varepsilon$.

= Explicit multistep methods cannot be A-stable
{parent=A-stability}

For an explicit <linear multistep method>, the highest-degree coefficient of $\rho(w)-z\sigma(w)$ is fixed while at least one lower coefficient grows unboundedly with negative real $z$. If all roots stayed in the <unit disk>, the <elementary symmetric polynomials> of those roots would keep every monic coefficient bounded. This contradiction proves that no nontrivial consistent explicit method is <A-stable>.

= Boundary-locus test for multistep A-stability
{parent=A-stability}
{title2=$z(\theta)=\rho(e^{i\theta})/\sigma(e^{i\theta})$}

A unit-modulus amplification root of a <linear multistep method> puts its test-equation parameter on this boundary locus, except when numerator and denominator both vanish. If the locus avoids the open left half-plane, the leading coefficient never vanishes there and the roots are initially inside the disk at one point there, continuity keeps them inside throughout that connected region. Checking <zero-stability>, imaginary-axis roots and common polynomial factors is still necessary. A boundary plot alone is not a proof of <A-stability>.

= L-stability
{parent=A-stability}
{c}

= L-stable
{c}
{synonym}

An A-stable one-step method is L-stable when its <stability function> also satisfies $R(z)\to0$ as $|z|\to\infty$ within the left half-plane. It damps modes whose decay time is far shorter than the numerical step.

= B-stability
{parent=A-stability}
{c}

= B-stable
{c}
{synonym}

A Runge--Kutta method is B-stable when it does not increase distances between numerical solutions of a dissipative differential equation. <Algebraic stability of a Runge-Kutta method> is a standard sufficient condition.

= Backward Euler method
{parent=A-stability}
{c}
{wiki}

Backward Euler advances $y'=f(t,y)$ by $y_{n+1}=y_n+h f(t_{n+1},y_{n+1})$. Its stability function $R(z)=(1-z)^{-1}$ makes it <L-stable>.

= A-stability of a symmetric two-stage implicit Runge-Kutta family
{parent=A-stability}
{c}

For the two-stage <implicit Runge-Kutta method> with
$$
A=
\begin{pmatrix}
\frac14&\frac14-a\\
\frac14+a&\frac14
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
b=\begin{pmatrix}\frac12\\\frac12\end{pmatrix},
$$
the <stability function> is
$$
R(z)=\frac{2+z+2a^2z^2}{2-z+2a^2z^2}.
$$
For $z=x+iy$,
$$
|2-z+2a^2z^2|^2-|2+z+2a^2z^2|^2
=-8x(1+a^2|z|^2).
$$
The denominator has no zero in the closed left half-plane, so the method is <A-stable> for every real $a$.

= Stiff equation
{parent=Linear stability domain}
{wiki}

= Stiff differential equation
{synonym}

A stiff differential equation contains rapidly decaying modes alongside much slower dynamics. An explicit method may then require a small step for stability even when accuracy only requires resolving the slow modes.

= Stiff two-mode linear system
{parent=Stiff equation}

If a linear system has negative eigenvalues with widely separated magnitudes, an explicit method can be forced to resolve the fastest decaying mode solely for stability. For eigenvalues $-1$ and $-100$, forward Euler requires $h\leq0.02$, whereas backward Euler is stable for every positive step size.

= Milne device for forward and backward Euler
{parent=Linear stability domain}
{c}

Starting at the same value, forward and backward Euler have opposite leading local errors. Their half-difference therefore estimates either local error:
$$
E_n=\frac12\lVert y_B-y_F\rVert.
$$
For a first-order method, a local-tolerance controller consequently scales the next step by $(\mathrm{tol}/E_n)^{1/2}$, usually with a safety factor.

= Orthogonal polynomial
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

Orthogonal polynomials of distinct degrees are orthogonal under a positive weighted inner product.

= Kravchuk polynomials
{parent=Orthogonal polynomial}
{c}
{wiki}
{title2=$K_s(t;n,q)=\sum_{j=0}^s(-1)^j(q-1)^{s-j}\binom tj\binom{n-t}{s-j}$}

= Krawtchouk polynomials
{c}
{synonym}

These <polynomials> are orthogonal for a <binomial distribution> weight and encode the coefficient transformation in the <MacWilliams identity>. Their <generating function> follows by expanding the two factors:
$$
\sum_{s=0}^nK_s(t;n,q)z^s=(1-z)^t(1+(q-1)z)^{n-t}.
$$
For binary <linear codes>, $q=2$. Expanding the factors gives $K_s(t;n,2)=\sum_j(-1)^j\binom tj\binom{n-t}{s-j}$, where <binomial coefficients> outside their usual integer range are zero. Orthogonality follows by multiplying <generating functions> and summing over $t$ with weight $\binom nt(q-1)^t$: the result is $q^n(1+(q-1)zw)^n$, whose coefficient of $z^rw^s$ is zero for $r\ne s$ and $q^n(q-1)^r\binom nr$ for $r=s$.

= Orthogonality forces many interior zeros
{parent=Orthogonal polynomial}

If a nonzero continuous real function on an interval is orthogonal to every <polynomial> of degree below $n$, it has at least $n$ distinct interior zeros. If it had only finitely many zeros and fewer than $n$ sign changes, multiply it by the product of linear factors at its sign-changing zeros. The product has constant sign and is nonzero on an <open interval>, so its integral cannot vanish. Orthogonality to this degree-below-$n$ <polynomial> contradicts that conclusion. Infinitely many interior zeros already satisfy the assertion. Orthogonal <polynomials> of respective degrees $0,\ldots,n-1$ form a <basis> of the needed <polynomial> space.

= Zernike polynomials
{c}
{parent=Orthogonal polynomial}
{title2=$Z_n^m(\rho,\phi)$}
{wiki}

= Zernike polynomial
{c}
{synonym}

The real Zernike polynomials form an <orthogonal polynomial> basis on the unit <Euclidean disk>, with radial factor $R_n^m(\rho)$ and angular factor $\cos m\phi$ or $\sin m\phi$. Here $0\leq m\leq n$ and $n-m$ is <even>. They decompose <wavefront errors> into modes useful for optical correction. Normalizations differ, so coefficients should specify the chosen convention.

= Zernike spherical mode
{c}
{parent=Zernike polynomials}
{title2=$Z_4^0=6\rho^4-6\rho^2+1$}

The unnormalized rotationally symmetric mode is $Z_4^0(\rho)=6\rho^4-6\rho^2+1$. It describes balanced primary <spherical aberration>. The lower-degree terms make it orthogonal to constant piston and quadratic defocus under the disk area measure: direct integration gives $\int_0^1 Z_4^0\rho\,d\rho=0$ and $\int_0^1 Z_4^0(2\rho^2-1)\rho\,d\rho=0$.

= Monic orthogonal polynomial
{parent=Orthogonal polynomial}

A monic orthogonal polynomial has leading coefficient one. For a fixed positive weight, there is a unique monic orthogonal polynomial of each degree.

= Three-term recurrence for monic orthogonal polynomials
{parent=Monic orthogonal polynomial}

For monic orthogonal polynomials,
$$
p_{n+1}(x)=(x-\alpha_n)p_n(x)-\beta_np_{n-1}(x),
$$
where
$$
\alpha_n=\frac{\langle xp_n,p_n\rangle}{\langle p_n,p_n\rangle},
\qquad
\beta_n=\frac{\langle p_n,p_n\rangle}{\langle p_{n-1},p_{n-1}\rangle}.
$$

= Hermite polynomial
{parent=Orthogonal polynomial}
{c}
{wiki}

= Hermite function
{c}
{parent=Hermite polynomial}
{title2=$H_n(x)=\frac{e^{-x^2/2}}{\pi^{1/4}\sqrt{2^nn!}}(-1)^ne^{x^2}\frac{d^n}{dx^n}e^{-x^2}$}

The normalized Hermite functions multiply the physicists' <Hermite polynomials> by a Gaussian. They form an <orthonormal basis> of $L^2(\mathbb R)$ and satisfy $(-d^2/dx^2+x^2)H_n=(2n+1)H_n$. With $a=(x+d/dx)/\sqrt2$ and $a^*=(x-d/dx)/\sqrt2$, they are $(a^*)^nH_0/\sqrt{n!}$ and the <quantum harmonic oscillator> is $2a^*a+1$. For completeness, if $u$ is orthogonal to all of them, all moments of $u(x)e^{-x^2/2}$ vanish. Its transform $\int u(x)e^{-x^2/2}e^{zx}\,dx$ is entire by <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality>, has every derivative zero at zero, and hence vanishes identically. Uniqueness of the <Fourier transform> gives $u=0$.

= Hermite functions are Fourier eigenfunctions
{parent=Hermite function}
{c}
{title2=$\mathcal F(H_n(x)e^{-x^2/2})=(-i)^nH_n(x)e^{-x^2/2}$}

Use the unitary <Fourier transform> with phase $e^{-i\lambda x}$. It commutes with $-d^2/dx^2+x^2$, because differentiation transforms to multiplication by $i\lambda$ and multiplication by $x$ transforms to $i\,d/d\lambda$. The <Hermite differential equation> has a unique polynomial solution of each degree up to scale, and multiplying by the Gaussian gives a <Hermite function>. Transforming a polynomial times this Gaussian again gives a polynomial times the Gaussian of the same degree; comparison of leading coefficients gives the eigenvalue $(-i)^n$.

= Mehler formula for Hermite functions
{c}
{parent=Hermite function}
{title2=$\sum_{n\geq0}t^nH_n(x)H_n(y)=\frac{\exp\!\left(\frac{4xyt-(1+t^2)(x^2+y^2)}{2(1-t^2)}\right)}{\sqrt{\pi(1-t^2)}}$}

For $|t|<1$, this identity sums the <Hermite function> expansion into a Gaussian. One proof takes the <Bargmann-Fock space> inner product of the generating functions $\pi^{-1/4}\exp(-x^2/2+\sqrt2 xz-z^2/2)$ at $\sqrt t\,z$. Writing $z=u+iv$, the two resulting real <Gaussian integrals> have quadratic coefficients $1+t$ and $1-t$ and linear coefficients $\sqrt{2t}(x+y)$ and $i\sqrt{2t}(x-y)$. Evaluation gives the formula. Setting $t=e^{-2s}$ and multiplying by $e^{-s}$ gives the <harmonic oscillator transition kernel> of $e^{-s(-d^2/dx^2+x^2)}$.

= Probabilists' Hermite polynomial
{title2=$\mathrm{He}_n$}
{parent=Hermite polynomial}
{c}

The probabilists' Hermite polynomials are
$$
\mathrm{He}_n(x)=(-1)^ne^{x^2/2}\frac{d^n}{dx^n}e^{-x^2/2}.
$$
They are orthogonal for the standard Gaussian weight and satisfy $\mathrm{He}_{n+1}=x\mathrm{He}_n-n\mathrm{He}_{n-1}$.

This is the Gaussian-probability normalization of the <Hermite polynomial> family.

= Space-time Hermite polynomial
{title2=$H_n(x,t)$}
{parent=Probabilists' Hermite polynomial}

= Hermite polynomial martingale
{c}
{synonym}

The space-time <probabilists' Hermite polynomial> $H_n(x,t)=t^{n/2}\mathrm{He}_n(x/\sqrt t)$ extends polynomially to $t=0$ and solves $\partial_tH_n+\frac12\partial_x^2H_n=0$. Therefore $H_n(B_t,t)$ is a <martingale> for a standard <Brownian motion>, by the <Itô formula> and Gaussian <moments>.

= Zeros of orthogonal polynomials
{parent=Orthogonal polynomial}
{wiki}

A degree-n orthogonal polynomial for a positive interval weight has n simple zeros in the interval interior.

= Jacobi matrix
{parent=Orthogonal polynomial}
{c}
{wiki}

A Jacobi matrix is a symmetric tridiagonal matrix whose characteristic polynomials obey the orthogonal-polynomial three-term recurrence.

= Numerical integration
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

Numerical integration approximates definite integrals using finitely many arithmetic operations and function evaluations.

= Boole's rule
{c}
{parent=Numerical integration}
{title2=$\int_{-1}^1p=(7p(-1)+32p(-1/2)+12p(0)+32p(1/2)+7p(1))/45$}

Integrate the five <Lagrange interpolation polynomials> at the equally spaced nodes to obtain the displayed rule. It is exact for <polynomials> through degree four, and symmetry also makes it exact in degree five. Symmetric endpoint, inner-node and midpoint weights $a,b,c$ satisfy $2a+2b+c=2$, $2a+b/2=2/3$, $2a+b/8=2/5$, giving $a=7/45$, $b=32/45$, $c=12/45$.

= Quadrature rule
{parent=Numerical integration}
{title2=$Q(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_i f(x_i)$}

A <quadrature rule> approximates an <integral> by a finite weighted sum of sampled <function> values:
$$
I_\rho(f)=\int_a^b\rho(x)f(x)\,dx\approx Q(f)=\sum_{i=1}^m w_i f(x_i).
$$
The <quadrature nodes> $x_i$ specify where to sample, and the <quadrature weights> $w_i$ specify how much each sample contributes. The density $\rho(x)$ specifies the target <integral>, whereas the discrete <quadrature weights> belong to the approximation. Ordinary <numerical integration> has $\rho=1$. <Gaussian quadrature> and the <midpoint quadrature rule> choose interior <quadrature nodes>; the <trapezoidal rule> includes endpoints.<NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions/3.5 Quadrature, subsection (iv) Interpolatory Quadrature Rules>[§3.5(iv), equation 3.5.15 and the following discussion of nodes and weights]{ref}

For ordinary <numerical integration>, exactness on the <constant function> $1$ requires $\sum_iw_i=b-a$; exactness on $x$ additionally requires $\sum_iw_ix_i=(b^2-a^2)/2$. These two checks make the <quadrature rule> exact for every <affine function>. For example, both the <midpoint quadrature rule> and <trapezoidal rule> give $6$ for $\int_0^2(2x+1)\,dx$. Testing progressively higher <polynomial degrees> defines the <degree of precision of a quadrature rule>. Exactness describes a class of <integrands>, rather than promising exact results for every <function>.

<Composite midpoint quadrature rules> and <composite trapezoidal rules> apply a simple <quadrature rule> on every panel of a <partition of an interval>. This buys accuracy by shrinking the panels while keeping the elementary formula fixed. For <continuous functions> sampled exactly, their <quadrature errors> tend to zero as the largest panel width tends to zero. The familiar second-order bounds require stronger assumptions about <derivatives>. The signed <approximation error> used here is $Q(f)-I_\rho(f)$; some sources define <quadrature error> with the opposite sign, so the convention must accompany any formula.

= Quadrature node
{parent=Quadrature rule}
{title2=$x_i$}

A <quadrature node> is a point at which the <integrand> is sampled by a <quadrature rule>. Under the <affine map> $x=(a+b)/2+(b-a)t/2$ from $[-1,1]$ to $[a,b]$, a reference <quadrature node> $t_i$ becomes $x_i=(a+b)/2+(b-a)t_i/2$; for ordinary <numerical integration>, the corresponding <quadrature weight> is multiplied by $(b-a)/2$.

= Quadrature weight
{parent=Quadrature rule}
{title2=$w_i$}

A <quadrature weight> multiplies a sampled <integrand> value in a <quadrature rule>. For ordinary <numerical integration>, its units match those of the integration variable. Positive <quadrature weights> that sum to $b-a$ make $Q(f)/(b-a)$ a weighted average of the samples; signed <quadrature weights> can magnify sample perturbations, as quantified by <noise sensitivity of a quadrature rule>.

= Degree of precision of a quadrature rule
{parent=Quadrature rule}
{title2=$d$}

The <degree of precision of a quadrature rule> is the largest integer $d$ such that $Q(p)=I_\rho(p)$ for every <polynomial> $p$ of <polynomial degree> at most $d$. By <linearity>, checking the <monomials> $1,x,\ldots,x^d$ suffices. Exactness for one higher-degree <polynomial> alone does not raise this degree: every lower <polynomial degree> must also be covered.

= Noise sensitivity of a quadrature rule
{parent=Quadrature rule}
{title2=$|\Delta Q|\leq\delta\sum_i|w_i|$}

If the observed samples are $y_i=f(x_i)+\varepsilon_i$, the resulting <quadrature rule> changes by $\Delta Q=\sum_iw_i\varepsilon_i$. Bounded sample perturbations $|\varepsilon_i|\leq\delta$ therefore satisfy
$$
|\Delta Q|\leq\delta\sum_i|w_i|.
$$
For ordinary <numerical integration> with nonnegative <quadrature weights> exact on <constant functions>, the bound is $(b-a)\delta$, independent of panel refinement. A common bias $\varepsilon_i=\delta$ attains it: the <quadrature error> due to the <function> approximation may vanish while this sample error persists. Interpolating a fixed data set onto a finer grid likewise does not create new observations or remove its uncertainty.

With new samples whose errors are <independent random variables> of zero <expectation> and common <variance> $\sigma^2$, a different model gives
$$
\operatorname{Var}(\Delta Q)=\sigma^2\sum_iw_i^2.
$$
For equal panels this is $\sigma^2(b-a)^2/n$ for the <composite midpoint quadrature rule> and $\sigma^2h^2(n-1/2)$ for the <composite trapezoidal rule>. These <variance> reductions require the stated independence and unbiasedness assumptions; a common bias or correlated sample errors need not shrink. The two displayed perturbation formulas follow directly from the <triangle inequality> and the <variance additivity for independent random variables>, respectively.

= Newton-Cotes closed quadrature
{c}
{parent=Quadrature rule}
{title2=$w_j=\int_0^s L_j(t)\,dt$}

= Closed Newton-Cotes quadrature
{synonym}

Integrate the <Lagrange polynomial> through values at all equally spaced nodes $0,\ldots,s$, including both endpoints. The resulting <quadrature rule> is exact through degree $s$. For even $s$, the node <polynomial> $\prod_{j=0}^s(t-j)$ is odd about $s/2$, so its integral vanishes and exactness extends through degree $s+1$. The weights need not remain positive as the number of nodes increases.

= Simpson's rule
{c}
{parent=Quadrature rule}
{wiki}
{title2=$\int_{a}^{a+2h}f\approx\frac h3[f(a)+4f(a+h)+f(a+2h)]$}

<Simpson's rule> integrates the quadratic interpolant through two endpoints and their midpoint. It is exact also on cubic <polynomials>. On $[0,2]$, define the raw kernel $K(t)=L[(x-t)_+^3]$, where $L$ is integral minus the rule. Then $K(t)=-t^3(4-3t)/12$ for $0\leq t\leq1$ and $K(t)=-(2-t)^3(3t-2)/12$ for $1\leq t\leq2$. It is strictly negative inside the interval and integrates to $-1/15$. The <Peano kernel theorem> therefore gives the sharp bound $|L(f)|\leq\|f^{(4)}\|_\infty/90$; $f(x)=x^4/24$ attains equality. Scaling to endpoint spacing $h$ gives $h^5/90$. The normalized <Peano kernel> is $K/3!$, so the external factorial must be omitted when using that normalization.

= Convergence of positive quadrature on continuous functions
{parent=Quadrature rule}

On a compact interval, positive <quadrature rule> weights whose sum is the interval length define uniformly bounded functionals on <continuous functions>. If the rules eventually integrate every fixed <polynomial> exactly, the <Weierstrass approximation theorem> proves convergence for every <continuous function>.

= Nodal-polynomial criterion for quadrature exactness
{parent=Quadrature rule}

An $(n+1)$-node <quadrature rule> already exact through degree $n$ is exact through degree $n+1+k$ exactly when its nodal polynomial
$$
Q_{n+1}(x)=\prod_{i=0}^n(x-x_i)
$$
is orthogonal to every polynomial of degree at most $k$.

= Degree ceiling for quadrature exactness
{parent=Quadrature rule}

No $(n+1)$-node quadrature rule for a positive interval weight can be exact through degree $2n+2$, because it evaluates the nonnegative nodal square $Q_{n+1}^2$ as zero although its integral is positive.

= Positivity of quadrature weights from degree 2n exactness
{parent=Quadrature rule}

If an $(n+1)$-node quadrature rule for a positive interval weight is exact through degree $2n$, every weight is positive. Indeed, applying the rule to the square of the corresponding degree-$n$ Lagrange cardinal polynomial isolates that weight.

= Convergence of positive quadrature rules
{parent=Quadrature rule}

If each $(n+1)$-node quadrature rule is exact through degree $n$ and has positive weights, then it converges on every continuous function. The <Weierstrass approximation theorem> reduces the error to the uniform polynomial-approximation error, while exactness on constants controls the sum of the weights.

= Moment matching
{parent=Quadrature rule}

A quadrature rule is exact through degree $d$ precisely when its node weights reproduce the first $d+1$ moments of the integration measure.

= Midpoint method
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

The <midpoint method> advances an <ordinary differential equation> using a derivative evaluated at a temporal midpoint. Its explicit form predicts that midpoint from the initial derivative, while the <implicit midpoint rule> solves for the derivative at the mean of the endpoint states.

= Collocation method
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

A <collocation method> chooses an approximate solution from a finite-dimensional trial space and forces the residual of a <differential equation> to vanish at selected nodes. <Collocation Runge-Kutta methods> apply this principle to polynomial approximations on each time step.

= QR algorithm
{c}
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

The <QR algorithm> computes <eigenvalues> through repeated <QR decompositions> and similarity updates. Shifted and unshifted forms have different convergence rates; the <unshifted QR algorithm> uses a zero shift at every step.

= Iterative method
{parent=Numerical analysis}
{wiki}

An <iterative method> approximates a solution by repeatedly updating an initial guess. In <numerical linear algebra>, a <stationary iterative method for a linear system> uses a fixed iteration map; other iterations can change their update rule or search space at each step.
